e^{-\tilde{\epsilon}}\leq q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})/q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y})\leq e^{\tilde{\epsilon}}
,

–

\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(R(0,e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))\big)\subset\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y})\big)
and
\Psi_{\hat{y}}\big(R(0,e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y}))\big)\subset\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
,

–

\lVert\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}-\operatorname{id}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}<\tilde{\epsilon}
on
R(0,e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))
and
\lVert\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{y}}-\operatorname{id}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{1}}<\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}
on
R(0,e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y}))
.

Let
V^{s}_{\hat{x}}=\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(\{(t,F^{s}(t)),|t|\leq q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\}\big)
be a
s
-manifold in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
. Let
p_{1},p_{2}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the projections on the first and second coordinate. We will first show that
\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}(V^{s}_{\hat{x}})
is a graph, where it makes sense.

Define the map
h:t\mapsto p_{1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}(t,F^{s}(t))
, which is well defined on
[-e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}),e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})]
since
q(V^{s}_{\hat{x}})=q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})
. As in the proof of Proposition
2.17
, if
h
is one-to-one, then
\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}(V^{s}_{\hat{x}})
is a graph on the image of
h
. Write
h=\operatorname{id}+\tilde{h}
. It is sufficient to show that
\tilde{h}
is a contraction of
[-e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}),e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})]
. Let
t_{1},t_{2}\in[-e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}),e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})]
, we have:

\displaystyle|\tilde{h}(t_{1})-\tilde{h}(t_{2})|

\displaystyle=|t_{1}-\big(p_{1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big)(t_{1},F^{s}(t_{1}))-t_{2}+\big(p_{1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big)(t_{2},F^{s}(t_{2}))|

\displaystyle=|p_{1}\circ\big(\operatorname{id}-\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big)(t_{1},F^{s}(t_{1}))-p_{1}\circ\big(\operatorname{id}-\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big)(t_{2},F^{s}(t_{2}))|

\displaystyle\leq|\big(\operatorname{id}-\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big)(t_{1},F^{s}(t_{1}))-\big(\operatorname{id}-\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big)(t_{2},F^{s}(t_{2}))|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{v\in R(0,e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))}\lVert d_{v}\big(\operatorname{id}-\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big)\rVert\lVert(t_{1},F^{s}(t_{1}))-(t_{2},F^{s}(t_{2}))\rVert

\displaystyle<\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}\big(|t_{1}-t_{2}|+|F^{s}(t_{1})-F^{s}(t_{2})|\big)

\displaystyle<\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}(1+\gamma)|t_{1}-t_{2}|.

This then proves that
\tilde{h}
is a contraction for a good choice of
\tilde{\epsilon}
. We now want to estimate the size of the image by
h
of
[-e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}),e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})]
. Let
t\in[-e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}),e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})]
, we have:

\displaystyle|h(t)|

\displaystyle=|t+\tilde{h}(t)|\geq|t|-|\tilde{h}(t)|

\displaystyle\geq|t|-\big(|\tilde{h}(t)-\tilde{h}(0)|+|\tilde{h}(0)|\big)

\displaystyle>|t|-\big(\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}(1+\gamma)|t|+\lVert(\operatorname{id}-\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}})(0,F^{s}(0))\rVert\big)

\displaystyle>|t|-\big(\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}(1+\gamma)|t|+\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle>|t|\big(1-\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}(1+\gamma)\big)-\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}

\displaystyle>|t|(1-2\tilde{\epsilon}^{2})-\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}

for
\gamma
sufficiently small. This shows that
h([-e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}),e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})])\supset[-(1-2\tilde{\epsilon})e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})+\tilde{\epsilon},(1-2\tilde{\epsilon})e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})-\tilde{\epsilon}]\supset[-e^{-4\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y}),e^{-4\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y})]
for
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough.
We just showed that
\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}(V^{s}_{\hat{x}})\cap R(0,e^{-4\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y}))=\big\{(t,G^{s}(t)),|t|\leq e^{-4\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y})\big\}
for some map
G^{s}:[-e^{-4\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y}),e^{-4\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y})]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

We will now show that
G^{s}
is Lipschitz and estimate its constant. Let
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in[-e^{-4\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y}),e^{-4\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{y})]
. Let also
t_{1},t_{2}\in[-e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}),e^{-2\tilde{\epsilon}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})]
such that
h(t_{i})=\tau_{i}
. We have:

\displaystyle|G^{s}(\tau_{1})-G^{s}(\tau_{2})|

\displaystyle=|p_{2}\circ\big(\operatorname{id}+(\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}-\operatorname{id})\big)(t_{1},F^{s}(t_{1}))

\displaystyle-p_{2}\circ\big(\operatorname{id}+(\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}-\operatorname{id})\big)(t_{2},F^{s}(t_{2}))|

\displaystyle\leq|F(t_{1})-F(t_{2})|+|(\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}-\operatorname{id})(t_{1},F^{s}(t_{1}))-(\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}-\operatorname{id})(t_{2},F^{s}(t_{2}))|

\displaystyle<\text{Lip}(F^{s})|t_{1}-t_{2}|+\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}\big(1+\text{Lip}(F^{s})\big)|t_{1}-t_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq\big(\text{Lip}(F^{s})+\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}(1+\text{Lip}(F^{s})\big)|t_{1}-t_{2}|.

On the other hand, we have:

\displaystyle|\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}|

\displaystyle\geq|t_{1}-t_{2}|-|\big(\operatorname{id}-\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big)(t_{1},F^{s}(t_{1}))-\big(\operatorname{id}-\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big)(t_{2},F^{s}(t_{2}))|

\displaystyle>\big(1-\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}(1+\gamma)\big)|t_{1}-t_{2}|.

Putting it all together, we finally have:

|G^{s}(\tau_{1})-G^{s}(\tau_{2})|\leq\frac{\text{Lip}(F^{s})+\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}\big(1+\text{Lip}(F^{s})\big)}{1-\tilde{\epsilon}^{2}\big(1+\gamma\big)}|\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}|\leq e^{2\tilde{\epsilon}}\text{Lip}(F^{s})|\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}|

the last inequality being true for
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough. This gives us the desired
s
-manifold
V^{s}_{\hat{y}}
from the statement.

We still have to estimate
\varphi(V^{s}_{\hat{y}})
. We have:

|G^{s}(0)|=|p_{2}\circ\big(\operatorname{id}+(\Psi_{\hat{y}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}-\operatorname{id})\big)(0,F^{s}(0))|<(1+\tilde{\epsilon}^{2})|F^{s}(0)|<e^{\tilde{\epsilon}}|F^{s}(0)|.

for
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough. This then concludes the proof.
∎

3.
Two families of
us
-rectangles having a Markov-type property

In this section, we fix
\mu\in\mathbb{P}(f)
, a saddle hyperbolic measure, possibly non-ergodic. Write
\hat{\mu}=\pi_{*}^{-1}\mu
. The goal of the present section is to construct two families of rectangles with a Markov-type property. Let us fix a Pesin set
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon}
. We take
\epsilon
such that all the results from Section
2
hold. Fix then a Pesin block
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}\subset\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon}
that we will simply write as
\hat{\Lambda}
to simplify the notation. Let also
\tilde{\epsilon}<\epsilon
.

3.1.
Natural unstable cone fields

In this paragraph, we will construct a natural unstable cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u}
on
\Lambda:=\pi(\hat{\Lambda})
, which does not depend on the fibers of the bundle
M_{f}
. We will then study the properties of admissible manifolds tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{u}
.

Proposition 3.1

(Canonical unstable cone field)
.

There exists a continuous cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u}
on
\Lambda
with the following properties:

(1)

For every
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
,
E^{u}(\hat{x})\subset\text{Int}\big(\mathscr{C}^{u}_{x}\big)
, where
x=\pi(\hat{x})
.

(2)

There exists a neighborhood
V
of
\Lambda
such that
\mathscr{C}^{u}
can be extended to a continuous cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
on
V
.

(3)

There exists an integer
N\geq 1
such that for all
n\geq N
and all
x\in\pi\big(\hat{\Lambda}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{\Lambda})\big)
, we have:

df^{n}\mathscr{C}^{u}_{x}\subset\text{Int}\big(\mathscr{C}^{u}_{f^{n}(x)}\big).

Proof.

Recall that for every
x\in\Lambda
and every
\hat{x},\hat{y}\in\pi^{-1}(x)
, we have
E^{s}(\hat{x})=E^{s}(\hat{y})=E^{s}(x)
. In particular, this implies that the map
x\rightarrow E^{s}(x)
is continuous on
\Lambda
. By the definition of the Pesin block
2.2
, we know that there exists a constant
\gamma_{s}>0
such that for all
x\in\Lambda
, the cone:

\mathscr{C}^{s}_{x}=\{v=v_{s}+v_{c}\in E^{s}(x)\oplus(E^{s}(x))^{\perp},\ \lVert v_{c}\rVert\leq\gamma_{s}\lVert v_{s}\rVert\}

does not intersect any of the unstable directions
E^{u}(\hat{x})
for
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}\cap\pi^{-1}(x)
.

Define then
\mathscr{C}^{u}_{x}
to be the complement of the cone
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{x}
in
T_{x}M
, removing the zero vector. Since the continuity of
x\mapsto\mathscr{C}^{s}_{x}
is implied by the continuity of
x\mapsto E^{s}(x)
, the map
x\mapsto\mathscr{C}^{u}_{x}
is also continuous. This proves the first item.

To prove the second item, consider the map
x\mapsto E^{s}(x)
from
\Lambda
to
PTM
, the projective tangent bundle of
M
. It is standard that, given a vector bundle over a compact subset, one can extend it to a neighborhood. For completeness, let us sketch the argument. Cover
\Lambda
by a finite number of charts
U_{i}
such that
E^{s}
is homeomorphic to
(U_{i}\cap\Lambda)\times\mathbb{R}
. Extend
E^{s}
trivially on each chart. Now, up to reducing the
U_{i}
, one can extend the transition functions to
U_{i}
by Tietze extension Theorem. This allows us to build a vector bundle via the usual way on
V:=\cup U_{i}
which extends
E^{s}
.

It remains to prove the last point. Take
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
and consider the Pesin chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
. Recall that by Theorem
2.7
, for all
n\geq 1
, the map
f^{n}_{\hat{x}}=\Psi_{\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x})}^{-1}\circ f^{n}\circ\Psi_{\hat{x}}
is a well-defined diffeomorphism from
R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))
onto its image. Recall that
d_{0}f^{n}_{\hat{x}}
is the following hyperbolic matrix:

\begin{pmatrix}A^{n}&0\\
0&B^{n}\end{pmatrix},

where
|A|,|B|^{-1}\leq e^{-\chi/2}
.

Let
\gamma>0
and define the
\gamma
-horizontal cone:

\mathscr{C}^{H}_{\gamma}=\{(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\ |u_{2}|\leq\gamma|u_{1}|\}.

We are going to show that for
n\geq 1
, the map
(d_{0}f^{n}_{\hat{x}})^{-1}
contracts exponentially the
\gamma
-horizontal cone. Let
u=u_{1}+u_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{H}_{\gamma}
. We then have:

\displaystyle|(d_{0}f^{n}_{\hat{x}})^{-1}u_{2}|

\displaystyle=|B^{n}|^{-1}|u_{2}|\leq e^{-n\chi/2}|u_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma e^{-n\chi/2}|u_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma e^{-n\chi/2}e^{-n\chi/2}|(d_{0}f^{n}_{\hat{x}})^{-1}u_{1}|.

This proves that
(d_{0}f^{n}_{\hat{x}})^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{H}_{\gamma}\subset\mathscr{C}^{H}_{\gamma e^{-n\chi}}
.

Let now
n\geq 1
. Recall the definition of the Lyapunov change of coordinates
C(\hat{x})
from Definition
2.3
. Take
\gamma_{0}
such that
C(\hat{x})^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{s}_{x}\supset\mathscr{C}^{H}_{\gamma_{0}}
, which exists by continuity of
\hat{x}\mapsto C(\hat{x})
on the compact set
\hat{\Lambda}
. Define
\gamma_{n}=e^{n\chi}\gamma_{0}
.

Take
N\geq 1
such that for all
n\geq N
and for all
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\Lambda)
, we have:

C(\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x}))^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{s}_{f^{n}(\pi(x))}\subset\text{Int}\big(\mathscr{C}^{H}_{\gamma_{n}}\big).

Such an
N
exists because since
\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x})\in\hat{\Lambda}
, the norm of the map
C(\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x}))
and the opening coefficient of the cone
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{f^{n}(x_{0})}
are bounded by constants depending only on
\hat{\Lambda}
.

Take then
x\in\pi\big(\hat{\Lambda}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{\Lambda})\big)
and take any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{\Lambda})\cap\pi^{-1}(x)
.

Now, we have:

\displaystyle(d_{0}f^{n}_{\hat{x}})^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{H}_{\gamma_{n}}

\displaystyle=C(\hat{x})^{-1}\circ(d_{x}f^{n})^{-1}\circ C(\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x}))\mathscr{C}^{H}_{\gamma_{n}}

\displaystyle\subset\mathscr{C}^{H}_{\gamma_{1}}\subset C(\hat{x})^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{s}_{x}.

But then this implies:

\text{Int}\big(d_{x}f^{n}\mathscr{C}^{s}_{x}\big)\supset C(\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x}))\text{Int}\big(\mathscr{C}^{H}_{\gamma_{n}}\big)\supset\mathscr{C}^{s}_{f^{n}(x)}.

This concludes the proof since
\mathscr{C}^{u}_{x}=(\mathscr{C}^{s}_{x}\cup\{0\})^{c}
.
∎

At some point, we may have to use thinner cones to have better control over geometry.

Definition 3.2

(
\alpha
-stable/unstable cone field)
.

Let
\alpha>0
. Let
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext},V
be the cone field and the neighborhood constructed in Proposition
3.1
. Let
\gamma_{u},\gamma_{s}:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
F_{u},F_{s}:V\rightarrow TV
be the opening map and the direction of the cone fields
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
and respectively
(\mathscr{C}^{u,ext})^{c}
. We then define
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext,\alpha}
and
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
, the
\alpha
-unstable/stable cone fields, to be the ones with direction
F_{u}
, respectively
F_{s}
, and opening map
\alpha\gamma_{u}
, respectively
\alpha\gamma_{s}
.

We are now able to define a natural cone field in the Pesin charts of points in
\hat{\Lambda}
.

Definition 3.3

(Canonical unstable cone field in the charts)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}^{u},\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
and
V
be the two cone fields and the neighborhood constructed in Proposition
3.1
. Let
x\in\Lambda
and let
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon}\cap\pi^{-1}(x)
. Up to reducing the size of the chart, we can ensure that
\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))\big)\subset V
. We then define the canonical unstable cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}
in the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
to be:

(3.1)

\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}=d\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}\big).

Lemma 3.4
.

There exists
q^{*}:=q^{*}(\hat{\Lambda})
with the following property. For all
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}_{|R(0,q^{*})}
contains the vertical direction and does not contain the horizontal one.

Proof.

We keep the notation of the proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Take
x\in\Lambda
and any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}\cap\pi^{-1}(x)
. Recall that
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}_{0}=C(\hat{x})^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{u}_{x}
. By Proposition
3.1
, the unstable direction
E^{u}(\hat{x})
belongs to
\text{Int}\big(\mathscr{C}^{u}_{x}\big)
and since
C(\hat{x})^{-1}E^{u}(\hat{x})=\text{span}\{e_{2}\}
, we deduce that
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}_{0}
contains the vertical direction in its interior. A similar argument shows that
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}_{0}
does not contain the horizontal direction
\text{span}\{e_{1}\}
. By continuity of the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
, of the Pesin charts, and compactness of
\hat{\Lambda}
, we can find
q^{*}
such that
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}_{|R(0,q^{*})}
contains the vertical direction and does not contain the horizontal one.
∎

Lemma 3.5
.

For all
n\geq 1
large enough, there exists an open neighborhood
\hat{U}:=\hat{U}(n)
of
\hat{\Lambda}
and constants
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}:=\delta_{1}(n,\hat{U}),\delta_{2}(n,\hat{U})
such that:

(1)

\pi(\hat{U})\subset V
, where
V
is the neighborhood from Proposition
3.1
.

(2)

For all
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, if there exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{U})
such that
d(\pi(\hat{z}),\pi(\hat{x}))<\delta_{1}
, then
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}_{f^{n}(y)}
is well defined and we have:

d_{y}f^{n}\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}_{y}\subset\text{Int}(\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}_{f^{n}(y)})

for all
y\in B(x,\delta_{2})
.

Proof.

First of all, take
n\geq 1
large enough so that for all
y\in\pi\big(\hat{\Lambda}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{\Lambda})\big)
, we have:

d_{y}f^{n}\mathscr{C}^{u}_{y}\subset\text{Int}\big(\mathscr{C}^{u}_{f^{n}(y)}\big).

This can be done by Proposition
3.1
. From now on, we fix such an
n
large enough. By continuity of the maps
x\mapsto d_{x}f^{n}
and
z\mapsto\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}_{z}
, and compactness of
\pi\big(\hat{\Lambda}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{\Lambda})\big)
, there exists a constant
\tilde{\delta}
such that for all
y\in\pi\big(\hat{\Lambda}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{\Lambda})\big)
and all
z\in B(y,\tilde{\delta})
, we have:

d_{z}f^{n}\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}_{z}\subset\text{Int}\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}_{f^{n}(z)}\big).

Note that taking
\tilde{\delta}
small enough, depending on
n
, guarantees that
z,f^{n}(z)\in V
.

Now, by compactness of
\hat{\Lambda}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{\Lambda})
, we can find a neighborhood
\hat{U}
of
\hat{\Lambda}
, depending on
n
, such that for all
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{U})
, there exists
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{\Lambda})
such that
d(\pi(\hat{z}),\pi(\hat{y}))<\tilde{\delta}/2
. Take
\delta_{1}
such that for any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, if there exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{U})
with
d(\pi(\hat{x}),\pi(\hat{z}))<\delta_{1}
, then there exists
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{\Lambda})
with
d(\pi(\hat{x}),\pi(\hat{y}))<\tilde{\delta}
.

Again, by compactness of
\hat{\Lambda}
and continuity of
x\mapsto d_{x}f^{n}
and
z\mapsto\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}_{z}
, there exists
\delta_{2}
such that for all
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
such that there exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{U})
with
d(\pi(\hat{x}),\pi(\hat{z}))<\delta_{1}
, the derivative
d_{y}f^{n}
contracts
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
for all
y\in B(\pi(\hat{x}),\delta_{2})
. Taking
\delta_{2}
small enough guarantees that
y,f^{n}(y)\in V
for all
y\in B(\pi(\hat{x}),\delta_{2})
, and this concludes the proof.
∎

We will work with foliations tangent to these canonical unstable cone fields.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\mathscr{C}^{u},\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
and
V
be the two cone fields and the neighborhood constructed in Proposition
3.1
. Let
\alpha>0
. Let
\mathscr{F}
be a smooth foliation of an open set
U\subset V
tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext,\alpha}
. The following holds for
\tilde{\epsilon}:=\tilde{\epsilon}(\mathscr{F},\hat{\Lambda})
and
\alpha:=\alpha(\hat{\Lambda})
sufficiently small. There exists
q\leq\min_{\hat{\Lambda}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})
with the following property. Let
x\in\Lambda
and
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}\cap\pi^{-1}(x)
. For any
y\in\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(R(0,q)\big)\cap U
, the leaf
\mathscr{F}(y)
contains a
(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u)
-manifold through
y
in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
of size
q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})
.

Proof.

First, for each
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, we can take
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough so that every leaf of
\mathscr{F}
intersecting
\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))\big)
is the graph of a Lipschitz function in the chart. This can be done since the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext,\alpha}
is continuous, so reducing
\tilde{\epsilon}
ensures that
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}
is almost constant. By continuity of the foliation and the Pesin charts on
\hat{\Lambda}
, we can take the same
\tilde{\epsilon}
for all
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
. For each
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, we can take
\tilde{\epsilon}
,
q
, and
\alpha
small enough so that for each
y\in\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(R(0,q)\big)\cap U
, the representing function
G_{y}
of the leaf
\mathscr{F}(y)
in the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
is well defined on
[-q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}),q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})]
. Indeed, since each leaf is smooth, reducing
q
ensures that each
G_{y}
is close to the line of slope
|G_{x}^{{}^{\prime}}(0)|
passing through
0
, where
x:=\pi(\hat{x})
. Taking
\alpha
small enough guarantees that
|G_{x}^{{}^{\prime}}(0)|<1
, and taking
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough will guarantee that
G_{x}
is close enough to the straight line of slope
|G_{x}^{{}^{\prime}}(0)|
passing through
0
, so all the
G_{y}
for
y
close will be Lipschitz graphs defined on the whole chart. Still by a compactness argument, we can choose
\tilde{\epsilon}
,
q
, and
\alpha
uniformly. Choosing
q
such that for any
y\in\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(R(0,q)\big)\cap U
, we have
|G_{y}(0)|<10^{-3}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})
concludes the proof. Again, it is easy to see that for any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, such a
q
exists, then a compactness argument gives a uniform
q
.
∎

3.2.
us
-rectangles

Recall the cone fields
\mathscr{C}^{u},\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
and the neighborhood
V
constructed in Proposition
3.1
, for the chosen Pesin block
\hat{\Lambda}
. Let
\tilde{\epsilon}>0
such that
\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))\big)\subset V
for any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
.

Let us first clarify what we mean by rectangles.

Definition 3.7

(
us
-rectangle)
.

A
us
-rectangle
R\subset M
is an open set such that there exists a Pesin chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
, with
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, for which the following properties hold.

(R1)

The boundary of
R
is a Jordan curve which decomposes as the union of four compact simple curves that we denote by:
\partial R=\partial^{s,1}R\cup\partial^{u,1}R\cup\partial^{s,2}R\cup\partial^{u,2}R
.

We call
\partial^{s}R=\partial^{s,1}R\cup\partial^{s,2}R
the stable boundary. We define similarly
\partial^{u}R
as the unstable boundary.

(R2)

Each of the curves of the stable boundary is contained in
s
-manifolds in the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))\big)
.

(R3)

Each of the curves of the unstable boundary is contained in
(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u)
-manifolds in the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))\big)
.

We call these manifolds the representative
s
-, respectively
u
-, manifolds of the stable, respectively unstable, boundaries.

We say that the Pesin chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
is adapted for the
us
-rectangle
R
.

We are going to define some notions to control the geometry of rectangles. For some cone field
\mathscr{C}
defined on an open set of
M
, let
\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{C})
, respectively
\mathcal{T}^{1}(\mathscr{C})
, be the set of all the piecewise
\mathcal{C}^{1}
curves, respectively all the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
curves, parameterized by
[0,1]
, tangent to
\mathscr{C}
on every piece, respectively everywhere.

Definition 3.8

(Parameters of
us
-rectangles)
.

Let
R
be a
us
-rectangle. We define the following parameters of
R
:

–

\sigma^{s}_{\max}(R)=\sup\big\{l(\gamma),\ \gamma\in\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}),\ \gamma([0,1])\subset R,\ \gamma(0)\in\partial^{s,1}R,\ \gamma(1)\in\partial^{s,2}R\big\}
.

–

\sigma^{u}_{\min}(R)=\inf\big\{l(\gamma),\ \gamma\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([0,1],M),\ \gamma(0)\in\partial^{u,1}R,\ \gamma(1)\in\partial^{u,2}R\big\}
.

–

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(R)=\sup\big\{l(\gamma),\ \gamma\in\mathcal{T}^{1}(\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}),\ \gamma([0,1])\subset R,\ \gamma(0)\in\partial^{u,1}R,\ \gamma(1)\in\partial^{u,2}R\big\}
.

The previous definition extends trivially to
u/s
-manifolds. Let
V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2}
be two
u
-manifolds in a chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},\kappa\big)
. We define:

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2})=\sup\big\{l(\gamma),\ \gamma\in\mathcal{T}^{1}(\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}),\ \gamma(0)\in V^{u}_{1},\ \gamma(1)\in V^{u}_{2}\big\}.

The following lemma asserts that controlling the parameters of a
us
-rectangle allows us to control its diameter.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
R
be a
us
-rectangle. Let
\alpha,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}>0
. Suppose that
\partial^{s,i}R\in\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Suppose also that we have the following estimates:

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(R)\leq\sigma_{1}\ \text{and}\ \sigma^{s}_{\max}(R)\leq\sigma_{2}.

Then we have the following control on the diameter:

\text{diam}(R)\leq\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}.

Proof.

Let any
y,z\in R
. Let
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
and
(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}})
be an adapted chart for
R
. Take two Lipschitz maps
F,G
which satisfy the following properties:

–

\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(\{(t,F(t)),\ |t|\leq q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\}\big)\in\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha})
and
\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(\{(G(t),t),\ |t|\leq q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\}\big)\in\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{C}^{u,ext})
.

–

y\in\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(\{(t,F(t)),\ |t|\leq q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\}\big)
.

–

z\in\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(\{(G(t),t),\ |t|\leq q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\}\big)
.

The map
F
represents then a
s
-manifold in
(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}})
tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
that we denote by
V^{s}
. Similarly,
G
represents a
(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u)
-manifold in
(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}})
that we denote by
V^{u}
. Since
\partial^{s,i}R
are tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
,
F
can be taken so that
V^{s}
does not intersect any of
\partial^{s,i}R
. Then,
V^{s}
intersects both
\partial^{u,1}R
and
\partial^{u,2}R
. Similarly, we can take
G
so that
V^{u}
intersects both
\partial^{s,1}R
and
\partial^{s,2}R
. Denote by
c^{s}\subset V^{s}
the curve going from
\partial^{u,1}R
to
\partial^{u,2}R
. Denote also
c^{u}\subset V^{u}
the curve going from
\partial^{s,1}R
to
\partial^{s,2}R
. Note that
y\in c^{s}
and
z\in c^{u}
. We then have:

d(y,z)\leq l(c^{s})+l(c^{u})\leq\sigma_{1}+\sigma_{2}

which concludes the proof.
∎

The following lemma discusses the geometry of the rectangles in charts and in the manifold. In particular, it allows us to compare the distances in the Pesin charts and in the manifold.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\alpha<1
. The following holds for
\tilde{\epsilon}:=\tilde{\epsilon}(\alpha,\hat{\Lambda})
small enough.

There exists a constant
C_{1}:=C_{1}(\hat{\Lambda})
with the following properties. Let
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
. Let
\kappa\leq q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})
. Let
V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2}
be two
u
-manifolds in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},\kappa\big)
which are almost everywhere tangent to the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}
. Then we have:

\frac{1}{C_{1}(\Lambda)}\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2})\leq d_{\hat{x}}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2})\leq C_{1}(\Lambda)\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2}).

Proof.

Let
V
be the neighborhood defined in Proposition
3.1
. Let
V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2}
be two
(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u)
-manifolds in
(\Psi_{\hat{x}},\kappa)
and write
F^{u}_{1},F^{u}_{2}
for their representative maps. For any
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, let
L^{u}(\hat{y})
be the constant from Lemma
2.15
for the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{y}}
defined on
\overline{R(0,\sup_{\hat{\Lambda}}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}})}
. Recall that
F^{u}_{1}
and
F^{u}_{2}
are
L^{u}(\hat{x})
-Lipschitz by Lemma
2.15
. Write also
q=\min(q(V^{u}_{1}),q(V^{u}_{2}))
. Define also, as for the unstable one, the
\alpha
-stable cone field in charts
\mathscr{C}^{s,\alpha,\hat{x}}=d\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
. As for the unstable cone fields, define
L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{y})
for any
y\in\hat{\Lambda}
. Note that
\alpha<1
implies, taking
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough, that for every
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, the two cone fields
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{y}}
and
\mathscr{C}^{s,\hat{y},\alpha}
are contained in disjoint constant cone fields. In particular, this implies
L^{u}(\hat{y})L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{y})<1
. Up to reducing
\tilde{\epsilon}
again, we can suppose that for all
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{s,\hat{y}}
contains the horizontal direction.

Let us first prove that there exists a constant
K_{1}
, depending only on
\hat{\Lambda}
, such that:

d_{\hat{x}}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2})\leq K_{1}\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2}).

Let
t\in[-q,q]
. Let
\gamma_{t}
be the straight line joining
F^{u}_{1}
and
F^{u}_{2}
at
t
. Write
\tilde{\gamma}_{t}:=\Psi_{\hat{x}}\circ\gamma_{t}
.
First, write
q=\min(q(V^{u}_{1}),q(V^{u}_{2}))
. Remark that, since
\mathscr{C}^{s,\hat{x}}
contains the horizontal direction, the curve
\tilde{\gamma}_{t}\in\mathcal{T}^{1}(\mathscr{C}^{s,ext})
and verifies, up to reparameterizing,
\tilde{\gamma}_{t}(0)\in V^{u}_{1}
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{t}(1)\in V^{u}_{2}
. We then have:

l(\gamma_{t})\leq\sup_{\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}}\lVert d\Psi^{-1}_{\hat{y}}\rVert l(\tilde{\gamma}_{t})\leq\sup_{\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}}\lVert d\Psi^{-1}_{\hat{y}}\rVert\sigma^{u}_{\max}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2}).

We then have the desired inequality by taking the supremum over
t
.

We now want to prove the other inequality, showing that there exists a constant
K_{2}
, depending only on
\hat{\Lambda}
, such that:

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2})\leq K_{2}d_{\hat{x}}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2}).

Let
\tilde{\gamma}\in\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha})
such that
\tilde{\gamma}(0)\in V^{u}_{1}
and
\tilde{\gamma}(1)\in V^{u}_{2}
. Write
\gamma:=\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}\circ\tilde{\gamma}
. Let
t_{1}\in[-q(V^{u}_{1}),q(V^{u}_{1})]
and
t_{2}\in[-q(V^{u}_{2}),q(V^{u}_{2})]
such that
\gamma(0)=(F^{u}_{1}(t_{1}),t_{1})
and
\gamma(1)=(F^{u}_{2}(t_{2}),t_{2})
. Note that for almost every
t\in[0,1]
, we have that
\gamma^{{}^{\prime}}(t)\in\mathscr{C}^{s,\hat{x},\alpha}
. Recall that
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
denote the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By our assumption on
\tilde{\epsilon}
, we have that
e_{2}\notin\mathscr{C}^{s,\hat{x},\alpha}_{\tilde{\gamma}(t)}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. This implies that there exists a map
G:[F^{u}_{1}(t_{1}),F^{u}_{2}(t_{2})]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, which is almost everywhere differentiable, such that
\gamma([0,1])=\{(t,G(t)),t\in[F^{u}_{1}(t_{1}),F^{u}_{2}(t_{2})]\}
.

Note that:

l(\tilde{\gamma})\leq\sup_{\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}}\lVert d\Psi_{\hat{y}}\rVert l(\gamma).

Since
\gamma
is tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,\hat{x},\alpha}
almost everywhere, there exists a constant
C(\hat{x})
such that
|G^{{}^{\prime}}(t)|\leq C(\hat{x})\alpha
for almost every
t\in[F^{u}_{1}(t_{1}),F^{u}_{2}(t_{2})]
. We can then compute
l(\gamma)
:

\displaystyle l(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\int_{F^{u}_{1}(t_{1})}^{F^{u}_{2}(t_{2})}\lVert(1,G^{{}^{\prime}}(s))\rVert ds\leq|F^{u}_{1}(t_{1})-F^{u}_{2}(t_{2})|(1+\alpha C(\hat{x}))

\displaystyle\leq(1+C(\hat{x}))\big(d_{\hat{x}}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2})+L^{u}(\hat{x})|t_{1}-t_{2}|\big).

But note also that since
L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{x})L^{u}(\hat{x})<1
, we get:

\displaystyle|t_{1}-t_{2}|

\displaystyle=|G(F^{u}_{2}(t_{2}))-G(F^{u}_{1}(t_{1}))|\leq L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{x})|F^{u}_{1}(t_{1})-F^{u}_{2}(t_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{x})d_{\hat{x}}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2})+L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{x})L^{u}(\hat{x})|t_{1}-t_{2}|.

And hence:

|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\frac{L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{x})}{1-L^{u}(\hat{x})L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{x})}d_{\hat{x}}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2}).

So finally we get:

l(\gamma)\leq\Big(\big(\frac{L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{x})}{1-L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{x})L^{u}(\hat{x})}\big)L^{u}(\hat{x})(1+C(\hat{x}))+1+C(\hat{x})\Big)d_{\hat{x}}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2}).

By compactness, continuity of the Pesin charts and the cone fields, there exists
K_{2}>0
depending only on
\hat{\Lambda}
such that:

\Big(\big(\frac{L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{x})}{1-L^{s,\alpha}(\hat{x})L^{u}(\hat{x})}\big)L^{u}(\hat{x})(1+C(\hat{x}))+1+C(\hat{x})\Big)\leq K_{2}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

3.3.
The two families of rectangles

The boundaries of the rectangles we are going to construct will be contained in stable manifolds of well-chosen points. Let us define the set of points in which we are interested.

Let
\hat{Y}^{\#}\subset M_{f}
be the set of points
\hat{x}
such that the following holds.

(1)

There exist two sequences of positive integers
(n_{k})_{k}
and
(m_{k})_{k}
converging to
+\infty
such that
\hat{f}^{n_{k}}(\hat{x})
and
\hat{f}^{-m_{k}}(\hat{x})
belong to the same Pesin block and converge to
\hat{x}
.

(2)

There exists a set of points
\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}
with well-defined one-dimensional stable manifolds, which accumulate
\hat{x}
by points projecting on both sides of
W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})
and such that the union of local stable manifolds of the points in
\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}
forms a continuous lamination with Lipschitz holonomies and with
\mathcal{C}^{r}
leaves. For more details and precise definitions, see sub-Section
6.4
.

(3)

For any point
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}
, for any open neighborhood
\hat{U}
of
\hat{y}
, there exist two sequences of integers
(n_{k})_{k}
and
(m_{k})_{k}
with positive density, i.e.,
\lim_{k}n_{k}/k>0
, such that
\hat{f}^{n_{k}}(\hat{y})
and
\hat{f}^{-m_{k}}(\hat{y})
belong to the intersection of
\hat{U}
and some common Pesin block, independent of
\hat{y}
.

The first item forms a set of full measure for any ergodic measure by the Birkhoff Theorem. By Theorem
6.10
, any ergodic measure which is hyperbolic of saddle type and such that almost every of its ergodic components have positive entropy gives full measure to the set of points satisfying the second and the third items. Remark that the last item guarantees that, given any ergodic, hyperbolic,
\hat{f}
-invariant measure
\hat{\mu}
of saddle type, for
\hat{\mu}
-almost every
\hat{y}
, the set
\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}
is included in the support of
\hat{\mu}
restricted to some Pesin block.

The goal of this section is to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 3.11

(Existence of family of
us
-rectangle with a Markov-type property)
.

Let
\nu
be a
f
-invariant measure, possibly non-ergodic. Let
\hat{\nu}:=\pi^{-1}_{\star}\nu
. Suppose that
\hat{\nu}
satisfies the following.

–

For
\hat{\nu}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, we have
\lambda^{u}(\hat{x})>0>\lambda^{s}(\hat{x})
, where
\lambda^{u/s}(\hat{x})
are the two Lyapunov exponents at
\hat{x}
.

–

\hat{\nu}
-almost every ergodic component of
\hat{\nu}
has positive entropy.

Let
K>8
and let
\eta\in(0,1)
. There exists a Pesin block
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}:=\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}(\eta)
such that the following holds for an integer
N:=N(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},K)
sufficiently large and
\alpha^{\prime}
,
\sigma:=\sigma(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},n)
sufficiently small.

There exist two families of
us
-rectangles
\mathcal{R}=\big(R_{1},...,R_{L}\big)
and
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}=\big(\tilde{R}_{1},...,\tilde{R}_{L}\big)
, and a foliation
\mathscr{F}^{u}
of an open set
V\supset\Lambda_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
such that:

(MR1)

For any
i\in\{1,...,L\}
, we have that
R_{i}
and
\tilde{R}_{i}
are two
us
-rectangles such that:

–

R_{i}\subset\tilde{R}_{i}
;

–

there exists
\hat{x}_{i}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
such that
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
is an adapted chart for
R_{i}
and
\tilde{R}_{i}
;

–

\partial^{s,j}R_{i}\subset\partial^{s,j}\tilde{R}_{i}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i}^{j})
where
\hat{y}_{i}^{j}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}\cap\hat{Y}^{\#}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
.

(MR2)

Let
\hat{U}
be the neighborhood of
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
constructed in Lemma
3.5
.

\hat{\nu}\Big(\hat{U}\Big)>1-\eta\ \text{and}\ \pi(\hat{U})\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{L}R_{i}

(MR3)

For any
i\in\{1,...,L\}
, each unstable boundary of
R_{i}
is contained in a leaf of
\mathscr{F}^{u}
. Moreover, they are not the same leaf:
\partial^{u,l}R_{i}\cap\partial^{u,k}R_{j}=\emptyset
for all
k,l\in\{1,2\}
whenever
i\neq j
or
i=j
and
k\neq l
.

(MR4)

For any
i\in\{1,...,L\}
, we have the following:

\frac{1}{2}\sigma\leq\sigma_{\min}^{u}(R_{i})\leq\sigma^{u,\alpha^{\prime}}_{\max}(R_{i})\leq\sigma;\quad\sigma^{s}_{\max}(R_{i})\leq\sigma;\quad\sigma^{s}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{i}),\sigma^{u,\alpha^{\prime}}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{i})\leq K\sigma.

(MR5)

For any
i,j\in\{1,...,L\}
such that
f^{N}(R_{i})\cap R_{j}\neq\emptyset
, if there exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-N}(\hat{U})
such that
\pi(z)\in R_{i}
, then:

f^{N}(\tilde{R}_{i})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}_{j}=\emptyset.

The last property is a type of Markov property. We ask that the rectangles intersect in a nice way, such that the image by
f^{n}
of some rectangle does not intersect the unstable boundary of the intersected rectangle. One can compare it to the classical Markov property on Figure
1
.

Figure 1
.
On the left, an example of an intersection between
\tilde{R}_{j}
and
f^{n}(\tilde{R}_{i})
which does not satisfy Property
(MR5)
. On the right, an example of an intersection satisfying
(MR5)
.

Before going into the technical details, let us explain the main steps and ideas of the proof.

Step 1: Construction of a foliation tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
.
First of all, we fix a Pesin block
\hat{\Lambda}
such that
\hat{\nu}(\hat{\Lambda})>1-\eta
. We construct a smooth foliation
\mathscr{F}^{u}
tangent to the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
. The plaques will be
(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u)
-manifolds in the Pesin charts.

Step 2: Construction of the family
\mathcal{R}
.
We take
x\in\Lambda
and
\hat{x}\in\hat{Y}^{\#}\cap\hat{\Lambda}\cap\pi^{-1}(\hat{x})
. We can then suppose that there exists points of
\hat{\Lambda}
on each side of the local unstable set of
\hat{x}
. These points have Pesin charts close to the Pesin chart of
\hat{x}
. Looking at the stable manifolds of these points, they will intersect each plaque of the foliation
\mathscr{F}^{u}
on a uniform neighborhood. Taking two plaques at a good scale, this will form a rectangle. Covering
\Lambda
by finitely many of these, we obtain the first family of
us
-rectangles
\mathcal{R}
.

Step 3: Obtaining
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
by applying successive approximations to
\mathcal{R}
.
The idea is similar to Sinai’s construction of Markov partitions for Axiom A diffeomorphisms. We start with the rectangles of the first family
\mathcal{R}
and look at the rectangles
R_{i},R_{j}
whose iterates intersect in a "bad" way:
f^{n}(R_{i})\cap\partial^{u}R_{j}\neq\emptyset
, but such that there exists a "good" Pesin chart: there exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{U})
with
\pi(\hat{z})\in R_{i}
. We then enlarge the rectangle
R_{j}
such that the intersection with
f^{n}(R_{i})
becomes nice. Doing this for all the
R_{i}
which intersect
R_{j}
in a bad way but with a good chart, we obtain
\tilde{R}_{j}^{1}
. We then repeat this construction, but just looking at the bad intersections of rectangles
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i},\tilde{R}^{1}_{j}
such that their "base" rectangles
R_{i},R_{j}
intersect. Note that at step
k
, the portions we add are delimited by
(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u)
-manifolds which had been contracted
k
times by the graph transform operator. By the choice of
n
, large enough ensuring that the graph transform is sufficiently contracting, we can then control the size of the portion we add.

3.4.
The key lemma.

We are first going to prove a lemma which will be the key step of the proof of Proposition
3.11
. This lemma allows us to iterate the successive approximations we explained.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
be a Pesin block. For any
K>4
, the following holds for
N:=N(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},K)
sufficiently large and
\alpha
,
\sigma:=\sigma(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},n)
and
\tilde{\epsilon}:=\tilde{\epsilon}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},n,\alpha)
sufficiently small. Let
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
be two
us
-rectangles such that
\partial^{s,i}R_{l}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{l,i})
for some
\hat{y}_{l,i}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}\cap\partial^{s,i}R_{l}
. Suppose that they satisfy the following:

–

There exists an adapted chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
to the rectangle
R_{i}
such that
\hat{x}_{i}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
.

–

Let
\hat{U}:=\hat{U}(N)
be the neighborhood of
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
constructed in Lemma
3.5
. There exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-N}(\hat{U})
such that
\pi(\hat{z})\in R_{1}
.

–

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(R_{1})\leq K\sigma,\ \sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(R_{2})\leq\sigma
and
\sigma^{s}_{\max}(W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,1}),W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,2}))\leq\sigma
.

–

f^{N}(R_{1})\cap\partial^{u}R_{2}\neq\emptyset
.

Then there exists a
us
-rectangle
\tilde{R}_{2}
with the following properties:

–

R_{2}\subset\tilde{R}_{2}
and
\partial^{s,i}R_{2}\subset\partial^{s,i}\tilde{R}_{2}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i})
.

–

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{2})\leq K\sigma
.

–

f^{N}(R_{1})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}_{2}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let us fix a Pesin block
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
and a constant
K>4
. Let
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
and
V
be the cone field and the neighborhood of
\Lambda_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
constructed in Proposition
3.1
. Take any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
and
V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2}
two
u
-manifolds almost everywhere tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}
in the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
. For any
\alpha<1
and
n\geq 1
, combining Proposition
2.19
and Lemma
3.10
whenever it makes sense, we have:

(3.2)

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}\big((\mathcal{F}^{u}_{\hat{x}})^{n}(V^{u}_{1}),(\mathcal{F}^{u}_{\hat{x}})^{n}(V^{u}_{2})\big)\leq C(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l})^{2}e^{-n\chi/4}\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(V^{u}_{1},V^{u}_{2})

where
(\mathcal{F}^{u}_{\hat{x}})^{n}
is the graph transform operator (Definition
2.18
) between the charts
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
and
\big(\Psi_{\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x})},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x}))\big)
for the map
f^{n}
(it can also be seen as the composition
n
times of the graph transform operator for
f
), and
C(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l})
is the constant given by Lemma
3.10
. Choose
\lambda\in(0,1)
such that
\lambda K<1
and take
n
large enough so that:

(3.3)

C(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l})^{2}e^{-n\chi/4}<\lambda.

From now on, we fix such an
n
and we will work with the map
f^{n}
. Let
\hat{U}
be the neighborhood of
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
and
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
be the constants given by Lemma
3.5
. Take
\gamma:=\gamma(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},\alpha)
and
\tilde{\epsilon}:=\tilde{\epsilon}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},n,\delta_{2},\gamma)
small enough so that:

–

For any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
, we have
\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))\big)\subset B(\pi(\hat{x}),\delta_{2})
.

–

Propositions
2.17
and
2.19
hold for any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
such that there exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{U})
satisfying
d(\pi(\hat{x}),\pi(\hat{z}))<\delta_{1}
, with the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}
and the map
f^{n}
.

–

Proposition
2.16
holds for any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
, with the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}
.

–

For any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
, the local stable manifold
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
is a
\gamma/2
-admissible
s
-manifold in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
and is tangent to
\text{Int}(\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha})
.

Indeed, for all
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
, such a
\gamma
exists. Now, by the continuity of the Pesin charts and the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
, and the compactness of
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
, it can be taken uniformly in
\hat{x}
. The same argument applies to
\tilde{\epsilon}
, with
\gamma
fixed. Note that the second condition holds since, under the mentioned hypothesis, the cone filed
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}
is contracted by
f^{n}
in the right Pesin charts.

Take
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{1}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{1})\big)
and
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2})\big)
two adapted charts for
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
such that
\hat{x}_{1},\hat{x}_{2}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
. The following construction will hold for any
\sigma:=\sigma(n,\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l})
sufficiently small. We assume that
f^{n}(R_{1})\cap\partial^{u}R_{2}\neq\emptyset
. Denote by
V^{u}_{i,j}
the
\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}_{i}},u\big)
-manifold in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
containing
\partial^{u,j}R_{i}
, for
j\in\{1,2\}
. By definition,
R_{i}\subset\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i}))\big)
. By Lemma
3.5
, we can then take the graph transform between the cone fields
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}_{1}}
and
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x}_{1})}
. We get:
W^{u}_{1,i}:=(\mathcal{F}^{u}_{\hat{x}_{1}})^{n}(V^{u}_{1,i})
. By Proposition
2.17
, the curves
W^{u}_{1,i}
are
\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x}_{1})},u)
-manifolds in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x}_{1})},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x}_{1}))\big)
.

Claim 3.13
.

The curves
f^{n}(\partial^{u,i}R_{1})
are contained in
\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}_{2}},u\big)
-manifolds in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2})\big)
that we denote by
\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,i}
.

Proof.

By definition, each
W^{u}_{1,i}
is almost everywhere tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
. This then implies that the curve
w^{u}_{1,i}:=\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}}^{-1}\Big(W^{u}_{1,i}\cap\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2}))\big)\Big)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is almost everywhere tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}_{2}}
. Note that taking
\sigma
small enough guarantees that
f^{n}(\partial^{u,i}R_{1})\subset(W^{u}_{1,i}\cap\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2}))\big)
. Since
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}_{2}}
does not contain the horizontal direction,
w^{u}_{1,i}
is a well-defined Lipschitz graph. Call its representative function
F_{i}
. Since by (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), we have:

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(f^{n}(R_{1}))\leq\lambda\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(R_{1})\leq\lambda K\sigma

and since
f^{n}(R_{1})\cap R_{2}\neq\emptyset
, it follows that taking
\sigma
small enough guarantees that
|F_{i}(0)|<10^{-3}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2})
.

Now, if
q\Big(W^{u}_{1,i}\cap\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2}))\big)\Big)<10^{-2}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2})
(in the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2})\big)
), then we can just extend
w^{u}_{1,i}
, in an arbitrary way, to a
\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u\big)
-manifold in the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2})\big)
, which proves the claim.
∎

Taking
\sigma
small enough and up to extending it, by continuity of local stable manifolds on
\Lambda_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
,
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{2,j})
are
\gamma
-admissible
s
-manifolds in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2})\big)
, tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
for
j=1,2
. Write
F^{2}_{i}
for the representative function of
V^{u}_{2,i}
and
F^{1}_{i}
the representative function of
\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,i}
. Denote by
U^{u}_{1}
the
\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}_{2}},u\big)
-manifold defined by the representative function
U^{u}_{1}=\min(F^{j}_{i})
, for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, where the min is taken on
[-\min\big(q(V^{u}_{i,j}),q(\tilde{W}^{u}_{i,j})\big),\min\big(q(V^{u}_{i,j}),q(\tilde{W}^{u}_{i,j})\big)]
. Define similarly
U^{u}_{2}
by taking the max of these four functions on the same domain. Then, by Proposition
2.16
, for each
j\in\{1,2\}
, the curve
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{2,j})
intersects in a unique point each
U^{u}_{i}
. Note that Proposition
2.16
guarantees that this intersection point belongs to
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{2,j})
, taking
\sigma
small enough. This defines naturally a Jordan curve, which bounds a topological disk
\tilde{R_{2}}
. Figure
2
shows how the construction works. We sum up the properties of
\tilde{R}_{2}
in the following claim.

Claim 3.14
.

\tilde{R}_{2}
is a topological disk with the following properties:

–

\tilde{R}_{2}
is a
us
-rectangle in the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2})\big)
.

–

R_{2}\subset\tilde{R}_{2}
and
\partial^{s,i}R_{2}\subset\partial^{s,i}\tilde{R}_{2}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{2,i})
.

–

f^{n}(R_{1})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}_{2}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

The first two points follow directly from the construction. For the last point, suppose that
f^{n}(R_{1})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}_{2}\neq\emptyset
. It then implies, without loss of generality, that there is a point
z\in\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}_{2}
such that
z\in f^{n}(R_{1})
. Recall that
F^{1}_{i}
is the representative function of
\tilde{W}^{u}_{i,1}
and
U^{u}_{1}
the one of
\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}_{2}
. Write
\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}}^{-1}(z)=(z_{1},z_{2})
. We then have
F^{1}_{1}(z_{2})<z_{1}<F^{1}_{2}(z_{2})
, which is absurd since
z_{1}=U^{u}_{1}(z_{2})
and
U^{u}_{1}
is the min (or the max) of all the
F^{j}_{i}
.
∎

(a)

The intersection between
R_{2}
(in gray) and
f^{n}(R_{1})
(in blue).

(b)

The
us
-rectangle
\tilde{R}_{2}
in red.

Figure 2
.
The construction of the
us
-rectangle
\tilde{R}_{2}
.

To complete the proof, we still have to estimate the unstable size of
\tilde{R}_{2}
. For that, we first prove that we can approach the unstable size
\sigma_{\max}^{u,\alpha}(\tilde{R}_{2})
by a geodesic.

Claim 3.15
.

The following holds for
\tilde{\epsilon}:=\tilde{\epsilon}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l})
sufficiently small. For any constant
C>1
, there exists
\alpha:=\alpha(C)
and a geodesic curve
c:=c(C)
going from
\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}_{2}
to
\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}_{2}
such that:

\sigma_{\max}^{u,\alpha}(\tilde{R})\leq C^{2}l(c).

Proof.

Let
C>1
. We will first show that there exists
\alpha>0
such that for any two curves
c_{1},c_{2}
tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
such that
c_{1}(0)=c_{2}(0)\in\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}_{2}
and
c_{1}(1)=c_{2}(1)\in\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}_{2}
, we have:

l(c_{1})\leq Cl(c_{2}).

Taking exponential charts and composing with a homothety, we can suppose that
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are graphs of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
maps
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
defined on
[0,1]
, tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
. Since
\tilde{R}_{2}\subset\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{2}))\big)
, taking
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough allows us to suppose that
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
is contained in a constant cone field
\tilde{\mathscr{C}}
, of horizontal direction and with opening coefficient
2\alpha
. We can then compute:

l\big(\text{Graph}(F_{1})\big)=\int_{0}^{1}\lVert(1,F_{1}^{{}^{\prime}}(t))\rVert dt\leq 1+\int_{0}^{1}|F^{{}^{\prime}}_{1}(t)|dt\leq 1+2\alpha.

Moreover, we trivially have that
l\big(\text{Graph}(F_{2})\big)\geq 1
. Going back to the manifold, there exists a constant
K
depending on
\tilde{\epsilon}
and on the exponential map such that:

\frac{l(c_{1})}{l(c_{2})}\leq K\frac{l\big(\text{Graph}(F_{1})\big)}{l\big(\text{Graph}(F_{2})\big)}\leq K(1+2\alpha).

Since
K
is given essentially by the differential of the exponential map, taking
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough allows us to take
K
as close to
1
as we want. We then obtain:

\frac{l(c_{1})}{l(c_{2})}\leq C

for
\alpha
taken small enough. From now on, we fix such an
\alpha
.

Fix a curve
\gamma\in\mathcal{T}^{1}(\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha})
such that
\gamma(0)\in\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}_{2}
and
\gamma(1)\in\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}_{2}
which satisfies:

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{2})\leq Cl(\gamma).

Let
c
be the geodesic between
\gamma(0)
and
\gamma(1)
. Parameterize it such that
c(0)=\gamma(0)
and
c(1)=\gamma(1)
. Up to taking
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough, we can lift
\tilde{R}_{2}
, the curve
\gamma
, and the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
on
\Psi_{\hat{x}_{2}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}))\big)
to
T_{\gamma(0)}M
by the exponential map. Denote the lift of
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
by
\mathscr{C}^{\star}
. Again, taking
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough allows us to suppose that
\mathscr{C}^{\star}
is constant. There exists some
v^{\star}\in T_{\gamma(0)}M
and some
r^{\star}>0
such that
c([0,1])=\exp_{\gamma(0)}(\{tv^{\star},\ t\in[0,r^{\star}]\})
. If
v^{\star}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\star}
, since the straight line
t\mapsto tv^{\star}
is the only one joining the lift of
\gamma(0)
to the lift of
\gamma(1)
, and since
\mathscr{C}^{\star}
is constant, then it is impossible that there exists a curve tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{\star}
joining these two points. We then conclude that
v^{\star}\in\mathscr{C}^{\star}
and then that
c
is tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
.

We finally have:

\sigma_{\max}^{u,\alpha}(\tilde{R}_{2})\leq Cl(\gamma)\leq C^{2}l(c)

which concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Now, we are going to investigate two cases to estimate
\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{2})
.

Case 1:

\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,i}\cap R_{2}=\emptyset
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Figure 3
.
The estimation of
\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{2})
in Case 1. In blue, the rectangle
f^{n}(R_{1})
. In gray, the rectangle
R_{2}
. In red, the geodesic curve
c_{\delta}
. In green, the curves
c^{u}_{1},c^{u}_{2},c^{s}
used to estimate the length of
c_{\delta}
.

See Figure
3
for a visual interpretation of the proof in Case 1. Remark that this implies that
\partial^{u,i}\tilde{R}_{2}\subset\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,i}
(up to changing the numeration). Indeed, since
f^{n}(R_{1})\cap R_{2}\neq\emptyset
, if there exists
z\in\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}_{2}
such that
z\notin\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,1}
, then we must have
z\in\partial^{u,1}R_{2}
. By a similar argument as before, this must imply that
|F^{2}_{1}(v)|>|F^{1}_{1}(v)|
for some point
v
. But this means that
\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,1}\cap R_{2}\neq\emptyset
.

Let
C>1
such that
4C^{2}<K
. Let
c
be the geodesic given by Claim
3.15
. Parameterize it such that
c(0)\in\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}_{2}
and
c(1)\in\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}_{2}
. Take any curve
c^{s}
going from
f^{n}(\partial^{u,1}R_{1})
to
f^{n}(\partial^{u,2}R_{1})
tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
. Such a curve exists because, since we fixed
n
, the curves
f^{n}\big(W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{1,i})\big)
are close enough to
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}_{1})
and then are tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
, taking
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough. Denote by
c^{u}_{1}
the subcurve of
\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,1}
going from
c(0)
to the intersection between
c^{s}
and
\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,1}
. Define similarly
c_{2}^{u}
to be the subcurve of
\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,2}
going from
c(1)
to the intersection between
c^{s}
and
\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,2}
. Now we have:

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{2})\leq C^{2}l(c)\leq C^{2}(l(c^{u}_{1})+l(c^{s})+l(c_{2}^{u}))\leq C^{2}(\sigma+\lambda\sigma K+\sigma).

Indeed,
c^{u}_{1}
and
c_{u}^{2}
are tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
and go from
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{2,1})
to
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{2,2})
, then
l(c_{1}^{u}),l(c_{2}^{u})\leq\sigma
by hypothesis. Moreover, by (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), we obtain
l(c^{s})\leq\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}\big(f^{n}(\partial^{u,1}R_{1}),f^{n}(\partial^{u,2}R_{1})\big)\leq\lambda\sigma K
.

Case 2:

\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,i}\cap R_{2}\neq\emptyset
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
.

(a)

Case 2a.

(b)

Case 2b.

Figure 4
.
The estimation of
\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{2})
in Case 2. In blue, the rectangle
f^{n}(R_{1})
. In gray, the rectangle
R_{2}
. In red, the geodesic curve
c
. In green, the curves
c^{u}_{1},c^{u}_{2},c^{s}_{1},c^{s}_{2}
used to estimate the length of
c
.
c^{s}
is the whole horizontal green curve while
c^{s}_{1}
and
c^{s}_{2}
are the subcurves going from
V^{u}_{2,1}
to
z
and
z
to
V^{u}_{2,2}
in Case 2a, or
\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,2}
in Case 2b.

See Figure
4
for a visual interpretation of the proof. Suppose without loss of generality that
\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,1}\cap R_{2}\neq\emptyset
. Let us first notice that there exists
z\in f^{n}(\partial^{u,1}R_{1})\cap R_{2}
. Indeed, suppose that it was not the case. Since
\tilde{W}^{u}_{1,1}
crosses
\partial^{s}R_{2}
, we can assume without loss of generality that
f^{n}(\partial^{u,1}R_{1})
is above
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{2,1})
(which has a sense in the Pesin chart), which contains the upper boundary of
R_{2}
. Since
f^{n}(\partial^{s}R_{1})
is contained in some local stable manifold, it cannot cross transversally
\partial^{s}R_{2}
. This implies that
f^{n}(\partial^{s}R_{1})
is above
\partial^{s}R_{2}
and then
f^{n}(R_{1})\cap R_{2}=\emptyset
.

Consider then such a
z\in f^{n}(\partial^{u,1}R_{1})\cap R_{2}
. Take a curve
c^{s}
tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
, passing through
z
which has the following properties:

–

c^{s}
stays between
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{2,1})
and
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{2,2})
,

–

c^{s}
does not cross
f^{n}(\partial^{s}R_{1})
,

–

c^{s}
intersects both of
\partial^{u,k}\tilde{R}_{2}
and
f^{n}(\partial^{u,k}R_{1})
uniquely.

To do this, notice that
z
can be taken as close as we want from the intersection of
f^{n}(\partial^{u,1}R_{1})
with a local stable manifold (
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{2,k})
or with the local stable manifolds containing
f^{n}(\partial^{s}R_{1})
). In the Pesin chart, translate this local stable manifold as a curve passing through
z
and extend it so that the size of this curve, seen as a graph, is the size of the chart. This gives us the desired curve.

Again, take
C>1
such that
4C^{2}<K
and let
c
be the geodesic given by Claim 3. Parameterize it such that
c(0)\in\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}_{2}
and
c(1)\in\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}_{2}
. Consider again
c^{u}_{1}
and
c^{u}_{2}
the curves contained in
\partial^{u,k}\tilde{R}_{2}
going from
c(0)
, and respectively
c(1)
, to the intersection with
c^{s}
. Denote by
c^{s}_{1}
the piece of curve contained in
c^{s}
going from the intersection with
\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}_{2}
to
z
(this is non-empty since
z\in R_{2}
). Denote by
c_{2}^{s}
the piece of curve contained in
c^{s}
going from
z
to the intersection of
c^{s}
and
\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}_{2}
. We then distinguish two subcases.

Case 2a:

c^{s}\cap\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}_{2}\in\partial^{u}R_{2}
.

Then we have the following estimate:

\displaystyle\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}l(c)

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\big(l(c^{u}_{1})+l(c^{s}_{1}\cup c^{s}_{2})+l(c^{u}_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}3\sigma.

Indeed, by the same argument as previously,
l(c^{u}_{1}),l(c^{u}_{2})\leq\sigma
. Now notice that
c^{s}_{1}\cup c^{s}_{2}
is contained in
c^{s}
and is then tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
, and goes from
\partial^{u,1}R_{2}
to
\partial^{u,2}R_{2}
.

Case 2b:

c^{s}\cap\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}_{2}\in f^{n}(\partial^{u,2}R_{1})
.

Then we have the following estimate:

\displaystyle\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{2})

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}l(c)

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\big(l(c^{u}_{1})+l(c^{s}_{1})+l(c^{s}_{2})+l(c^{u}_{2})\big)

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}(3\sigma+\lambda\sigma K).

Indeed, here the estimates are the same as before for
c^{u}_{1}
and
c^{u}_{2}
. The curve
c^{s}_{1}
is contained in a curve tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
going from
\partial^{u,1}R_{2}
to
\partial^{u,2}R_{2}
. The curve
c^{s}_{2}
goes from
z\in f^{n}(\partial^{u,1}R_{1})
to
f^{n}(\partial^{u,2}R_{1})
.

Finally, taking into account all the cases, since
\lambda K<1
, we have:

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{2})\leq C^{2}4\sigma\leq K\sigma

by the choice of
C
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
3.12
.
∎

3.5.
Proof of Proposition
3.11
.

3.5.1.
Choice of constants.

The proof involves many constants that we have to choose carefully. The order of the choice and the dependency between the constants plays a very important role. The goal of this paragraph is to try to give a clear description of the role and the dependency of each of these constants.

We first fix
\eta\in(0,1)
and
K>8
. We start by choosing a Pesin block
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
such that
\hat{\mu}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l})>1-\eta
. For the sake of simplicity, denote it by
\hat{\Lambda}
. Let also
\Lambda=\pi(\hat{\Lambda})
. We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
the canonical extended unstable cone field on
V
, a neighborhood of
\Lambda
(see Proposition
3.1
). Choose
n:=n(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},K)
large enough and
\alpha
,
\sigma:=\sigma(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},n)
, and
\tilde{\epsilon}:=\tilde{\epsilon}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},n,\alpha)
so that Lemma
3.12
holds. Let
\hat{U}:=\hat{U}(n)
be the neighborhood of
\hat{\Lambda}
constructed in Lemma
3.5
. From now on, we will work with the map
f^{n}
.

3.5.2.
Construction of a foliation tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
.

We first construct a foliation tangent to the cone field
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
on a neighborhood of
\Lambda
. For each
x\in\Lambda
, define as before
e^{s}(x)
the unit vector such that
E^{s}(x)=\text{span}\{e^{s}(x)\}
. Note that we have
e^{s}(x)^{\perp}\in\text{Int}\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,ext,\alpha}_{x}\big)
.

As in the proof of Proposition
3.1
, one can extend the direction
E^{s}(x)
continuously on the neighborhood
V
. This gives us a continuous sub-bundle on an open neighborhood
V
of
\Lambda
. Up to taking an orientable cover, we can assume that there exists a continuous vector field
X:V\rightarrow TV
which generates the line field
(E^{s})^{\perp}
. Approximate
X
by a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
vector field
Y
such that we still have
Y(y)\in\text{Int}\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,ext,\alpha}\big)
. This gives a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
line field on
V
. Integrating this line field gives then a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
foliation
\mathscr{F}^{u}
on
V
such that for all
y\in V
, we have:

T_{y}\mathscr{F}^{u}(y)=\mathbb{R}.Y(y)\in\text{Int}\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,ext,\alpha}\big).

This will be the foliation tangent to the unstable cone field with which we will work.

3.5.3.
Construction of the family
\mathcal{R}

In this paragraph, we construct the first family of
us
-rectangles
\mathcal{R}
. Take any
x\in\Lambda
. Let
q
be the constant given by Lemma
3.6
applied for the open set
V
and the foliation
\mathscr{F}^{u}
. Up to reducing
\sigma
, we can assume that for all
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}
, we have:
B(y,\sigma)\subset\Psi_{\hat{y}}(R(0,q))
. Recall the definition of
\hat{Y}^{\#}
before the statement of Proposition
3.11
. The set of such
\hat{x}
which are accumulated by points of
\hat{\Lambda}
projecting on
W^{u}(\hat{x})
, on both sides of their unstable manifold, have the same
\hat{\nu}
-measure has
\hat{\Lambda}
. Indeed, we supposed that
\hat{\nu}
-almost every ergodic component of
\hat{\nu}
has positive entropy and is hyperbolic of saddle type. We will the suppose that for any
x\in\Lambda
, there exists
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
displaying this property.

Applying a similar argument as in Lemma
3.12
, reducing
\tilde{\epsilon}
and
\sigma
gives that for any
y\in B(x,\sigma)\cap\Lambda
, the local stable manifold
W^{s}_{loc}(y)
is a
\gamma
-admissible
s
-manifold in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
. By Lemma
3.6
, for any
z\in B(x,\sigma)
, the leaf
\mathscr{F}^{u}(z)
contains a
(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u)
-manifold
V^{u}_{z}
of size
q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})
. Then, by Proposition
2.16
,
V^{u}_{z}
intersects
W^{s}_{loc}(y)
in a unique point.

Now, pick two points
z^{+},z^{-}\in W^{s}_{loc}(x)
such that
d_{W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})}(z^{+},z^{-})=\frac{3}{4}\sigma
and
\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}(z^{+/-})\in\mathbb{R}^{+/-}\times\mathbb{R}
. Note that this implies
z^{+},z^{-}\in B(x,\sigma)
.

Claim 3.16
.

The following holds for
\alpha
and
\sigma
sufficiently small:

\displaystyle\sigma^{u}_{\min}\big(\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{-})\cap B(x,2\sigma),\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{+})\cap B(x,2\sigma)\big)\geq\sigma/2;

\displaystyle\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}\big(\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{-})\cap B(x,2\sigma),\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{+})\cap B(x,2\sigma)\big)\leq\sigma.

Proof.

The argument is very similar to arguments in the proof of Lemma
3.12
. By lifting
B(x,2\sigma)
to
T_{x}M
by the exponential map, and since taking
\sigma\rightarrow 0
implies
d_{y}\exp_{x}\rightarrow Id
for any
y\in B(x,2\sigma)
, it is enough to show these inequalities for a foliation
\mathscr{F}^{u}
of a ball around
0
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Each leaf of the foliation
\mathscr{F}^{u}
on
B(0,2\sigma)
converges uniformly to a straight line taking
\alpha\rightarrow 0
. Taking
\sigma
small enough guarantees also that the curve
c^{s}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
going from
z^{-}
to
z^{+}
is close to the geodesic between
z^{-}
and
z^{+}
. Since the foliation
\mathscr{F}^{u}
is as close as we want to straight lines, the length of
c^{s}
approaches
\sigma^{u}_{\min}\big(\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{-})\cap B(0,2\sigma),\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{+})\cap B(0,2\sigma)\big)
. This proves the first inequality. For the second, taking
\alpha
small enough guarantees that the length of any curve going from
\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{-})\cap B(0,2\sigma)
to
\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{+})\cap B(0,2\sigma)
tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha}
is very close to the length of
c^{s}
. This gives the second inequality. Note that all these choices of constants can be done uniformly by compactness of
\Lambda
and continuity of
\mathscr{F}^{u}
.
∎

Claim 3.17
.

The following holds for
\tilde{\epsilon}
and
\sigma
sufficiently small. There exist two points
y^{+},y^{-}\in W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})
such that:

\sigma^{s}_{\max}\big(W^{s}_{loc}(y^{-}),W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+})\big)\leq\sigma.

Proof.

Repeating the arguments of the proof of Claim
3.15
in the proof of Lemma
3.12
, we can show that taking
\tilde{\epsilon}
small enough guarantees that there exists a constant
K>1
depending only on
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext}
with the following property. For any
s
-manifolds
V^{s}_{1},V^{s}_{2}
in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
, there exists a geodesic curve
c
going from
V^{s}_{1}
to
V^{s}_{2}
such that:

\sigma^{s}_{\max}(V^{s}_{1},V^{s}_{2})\leq Kl(c).

Since
\hat{x}
is accumulate by points of
\hat{\Lambda}
projecting on each sides of
W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})
, we can find two points
\hat{y}^{+},\hat{y}^{-}\in\hat{\Lambda}\cap\pi^{-1}(W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
such that
d_{W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})}(y^{+},y^{-})\leq\sigma/2K
and
y^{+},y^{-}\in B(x,\sigma)
. Recall that taking
\tilde{\epsilon}
and
\sigma
sufficiently small guarantees
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+/-})
is a
\gamma
-admissible
s
-manifold in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
. Let
c
be the geodesic curve going from
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{-})
to
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+})
such that:

\sigma_{\max}^{s}\big(W^{s}_{loc}(y^{-}),W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+})\big)\leq Kl(c).

Now, arguments very similar to the proof of Claim
3.16
give us that the length of
c
is close to the distance between
y^{-}
and
y^{+}
in
W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})
. We then conclude that:

\sigma_{\max}^{s}\big(W^{s}_{loc}(y^{-}),W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+})\big)\leq Kl(c)\leq 2Kd_{W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})}(y^{-},y^{+})\leq\sigma.

Again, note that all the constants can be taken uniformly in
x
, which concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Until now, we fix
\alpha
,
\tilde{\epsilon}
, and
\sigma
such that the two claims hold. By the Jordan theorem, the union of the four curves given by:

–

The subcurve of
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+})
going from the intersection of
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+})
with
V^{u}_{z^{-}}
to the intersection of
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+})
with
V^{u}_{z^{+}}
.

–

The subcurve of
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{-})
going from the intersection of
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{-})
with
V^{u}_{z^{-}}
to the intersection of
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+})
with
V^{u}_{z^{+}}
.

–

The subcurve of
\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{+})
going from the intersection of
V^{u}_{z^{+}}
with
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+})
to the intersection of
V^{u}_{z^{+}}
with
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{-})
.

–

The subcurve of
\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{-})
going from the intersection of
V^{u}_{z^{-}}
with
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{-})
to the intersection of
V^{u}_{z^{-}}
with
W^{s}_{loc}(y^{+})
,

is a closed simple curve that bounds a topological disk that we call
R_{x}
. See Figure
5
. The first two curves are the stable boundaries of
R_{x}
and we denote them by
\partial^{s,l}R_{x}
. The last two curves are the unstable boundaries of
R_{x}
and we denote them by
\partial^{u,l}R_{x}
. Note that we trivially have
\Lambda\subset\bigcup_{x}R_{x}
. We can then extract
R_{1},...,R_{L}
such that
\Lambda\subset\bigcup R_{i}
.

Let us now check that the family
\{R_{i}\}_{i\in\{1,...,L\}}
satisfies Properties
(MR1)
,
(MR2)
,
(MR3)
, and
(MR4)
from Proposition
3.11
. Fix some
i\in\{1,...,L\}
. To simplify the notation, we still denote by
x,\hat{x},z^{-},z^{+}
the points we used to construct the rectangle.

–

The curves
\partial^{s,l}R_{i}
are contained in some
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,l})
for some
\hat{y}_{i,l}\in\hat{\Lambda}
. The curves
\partial^{u,l}R_{i}
are contained in some
(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u)
-manifold in the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
for
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}
. Then
R_{i}
is a
us
-rectangle and
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
is an adapted chart. This proves the part of Property
(MR1)
concerning the first family
\mathcal{R}=(R_{1},...,R_{L})
.

–

By definition, we have
\Lambda\subset\bigcup R_{i}
, which proves Property
(MR2)
.

–

Property
(MR3)
follows from the fact that
\mathscr{F}^{u}
is a foliation on
V
and since the number of
us
-rectangles is finite, up to taking very close leaves, we obtain that all their unstable boundaries are contained in different leaves.

–

Claim
3.17
gives that
\sigma^{s}_{\max}(R)\leq\sigma
. Pick any point
y\in\partial^{u,1}R_{i}
. Let
c_{1}
be the curve contained in
\partial^{u,1}R_{i}
going from
y
to
z^{-}
(or
z^{+}
depending on the numeration). By Claim
3.17
,
l(c_{1})\leq\sigma
. Let
c_{2}
be the curve contained in
W^{s}_{loc}(x)
going from
z^{-}
to
x
. By construction,
l(c_{2})<\sigma
. We have:

d(x,y)\leq l(c_{1})+l(c_{2})<2\sigma.

We conclude that
\partial^{u,1}R_{i}\subset\mathscr{F}^{u}(z^{-})\cap B(x,2\sigma)
and the same for
\partial^{u,2}R_{i}
. Claim
3.16
then gives
\sigma^{u}_{\min}(R_{i})\geq\sigma/2
and
\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(R_{i})\leq\sigma
. This proves Property
(MR4)
.

See Figure
5
for a representation of the
us
-rectangle
R_{x}
. This then finishes the construction of the first family of
us
-rectangles
\mathcal{R}
.

Figure 5
.
The construction of the first family of rectangles: in light blue, the foliation
\mathscr{F}^{u}
; in dark blue, the
us
-rectangle.

3.5.4.
Obtaining
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
by applying successive approximations to
\mathcal{R}
.

This step goes by successive approximations. At each step, we will construct a family of
us
-rectangles
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{l}
displaying Property
(MR5)
with the rectangles of the family
\tilde{R}^{l-1}
. Letting
l
go to infinity will give us the desired family.

The construction goes by induction. Fix
i\in\{1,...,L\}
. Define
I(i)\subset\{1,...,L\}
to be the set of
j\in\{1,...,L\}
such that there exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{U})
, with
\pi(\hat{z})\in R_{j}
, and
f^{n}(R_{j})\cap R_{i}\neq\emptyset
.

Step 1.
Let then
i\in\{1,...,L\}
, we will define a new
us
-rectangle
\tilde{R}_{i}^{1}
. Denote by
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
the adapted chart to
R_{i}
constructed in the previous paragraph. If for all
j\in I(i)
, we have
f^{n}(R_{j})\cap\partial^{u}R_{i}=\emptyset
, then define
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}=R_{i}
. Suppose now that there exists
j\in I(i)
such that
f^{n}(R_{j})\cap\partial^{u}R_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Recall that
R_{i}
and
R_{j}
are two
us
-rectangles such that for each
l\in\{1,2\}
, we have
\partial^{s,l}R_{k}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{k,l})
for some
\hat{y}_{k,l}\in\hat{\Lambda}\cap\pi^{-1}(\partial^{s,l}R_{k})
. Recall also that we have the following estimate:

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(R_{j})\leq K\sigma/2,\ \sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(R_{i})\leq\sigma\ \text{and}\ \sigma^{s}_{\max}(W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{k,1}),W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{k,2}))\leq\sigma.

We can then apply Lemma
3.12
with the constant
K/2>4
and with the rectangles
R_{j}
and
R_{i}
, where
R_{j}
plays the role of
R_{1}
and
R_{i}
of
R_{2}
, following the notation of Lemma
3.12
. This gives us a new
us
-rectangle
\tilde{R}_{i,j}^{1}
with the following properties:

–

R_{i}\subset\tilde{R}_{i,j}^{1}
and
\partial^{s,l}R_{i}\subset\partial^{s,l}\tilde{R}_{i,j}^{1}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,l})
.

–

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j})\leq K\sigma/2
.

–

f^{n}(R_{j})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}_{i,j}^{1}=\emptyset
.

Do the same construction for each
j\in I(i)
and define:

\tilde{R}_{i}^{1}=\bigcup_{j\in I(i)}\tilde{R}_{i,j}^{1}.

Claim 3.18
.

The open set
\tilde{R}_{i}^{1}
has the following properties:

–

\tilde{R}_{i}^{1}
is a
us
-rectangle and
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
is an adapted chart.

–

R_{i}\subset\tilde{R}_{i}^{1}
and
\partial^{s,l}R_{i}\subset\partial^{s,l}\tilde{R}_{i}^{1}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,l})
.

–

\sigma/2\leq\sigma^{u}_{\min}(\tilde{R}_{i}^{1})\leq\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{i}^{1})\leq K\sigma
and
\sigma^{s}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{i}^{1})\leq\sigma
.

–

For all
j\in\{1,...,L\}
such that
f^{n}(R_{j})\cap R_{i}\neq\emptyset
, if there exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{U})
such that
\pi(\hat{z})\in R_{j}
, then we have:

f^{n}(R_{j})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}=\emptyset.

Proof.

For each
j\in I(i)
, write
F^{l}_{i,j}
for the representative function of
V^{l}_{i,j}
, the
\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}_{i}},u\big)
-manifold in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
containing
\partial^{u,l}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j}
. Let
q_{i}=\min_{j,l}q(V^{l}_{i,j})
. Define the following functions on the interval
[-q_{i},q_{i}]
:

G^{1}=\min_{j,l}F^{l}_{i,j}\ \text{and}\ G^{2}=\max_{j,l}F^{l}_{i,j}.

Remark that
G^{1}
and
G^{2}
are representing functions of
\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}_{i}},u\big)
-manifolds in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
that we denote by
W^{1}
and
W^{2}
. Indeed, since
G^{1}
is the minimum of a finite number of functions tangent almost everywhere to
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}_{i}}
, it satisfies also this property. We also have
q(W^{1})=\min_{j,l}q(V^{l}_{i,j})\geq 10^{-2}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})
and
\varphi(W^{l})\leq\max_{j,l}\varphi(V^{l}_{i,j})<10^{-3}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})
(recall the definition
2.10
of the parameter
\varphi
of a
u
-manifold). By Proposition
2.16
, each
W^{l}
intersects in a unique point each
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,k})
. This defines a
us
-rectangle
Q
for which
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
is an adapted chart. We are going to show that
Q=\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}
.

First, we have by construction that
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j}\subset Q
for each
j\in I(i)
, which implies that
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}\subset Q
. Now suppose that there exists
z\in Q
such that
z\notin\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}
. Write
z=\Psi_{\hat{x}}(z_{1},z_{2})
. Since
z\in Q
, we have
G^{1}(z_{2})<z_{1}<G^{2}(z_{2})
. But since
z\notin\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}
, then for all
j
, we have
z\notin\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j}
and then
z_{1}>F^{l}_{i,j}
for all
l\in\{1,2\}
and
j\in I(i)
. But this is absurd by the definition of
G^{1}
and
G^{2}
. This proves the first two points of the claim by construction of
Q
.

Since
R_{i}\subset\tilde{R}_{i}^{1}
and
\partial^{s,l}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,l})
, we trivially have:

\sigma^{u}_{\min}(\tilde{R}^{1}_{i})\geq\sigma/2\ \text{and}\ \sigma^{s}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{i}^{1})\leq\sigma.

Let now
\gamma\in\mathcal{T}^{1}(\mathscr{C}^{s,ext,\alpha})
such that
\gamma([0,1])\subset\tilde{R}_{i}^{1}
,
\gamma(0)\in\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}
and
\gamma(1)\in\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}
. There exist
j_{1},j_{2}\in I(i)
such that
\gamma(0)\in\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j_{1}}
and
\gamma(1)\in\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j_{2}}
. If
j_{1}=j_{2}
, then we have:

l(\gamma)\leq\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j_{1}})\leq K\sigma/2.

Suppose then
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
. Using the Pesin chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
, we can order any
us
-rectangle
R
in the chart such that
\partial^{u,1}R
is on the left of
\partial^{u,2}R
as graphs. Define
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(t_{1})\in\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j_{1}}
and
\gamma(t_{2})\in\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j_{2}}
. Note that
t_{1},t_{2}
must exist. Indeed, since for any
j
and
k,l\in\{1,2\}
, we have
|F_{i,j}^{l}|\leq|G_{k}|
, the curve
\gamma
has to cross the graph of
F^{l}_{i,j_{1}}
and
F^{l}_{i,j_{2}}
for
l\in\{1,2\}
. Since the rectangles
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j_{1}}
and
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j_{2}}
share the same stable boundary and
\gamma([0,1])\subset\tilde{R}_{i}^{1}
, then
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are well defined. Observe also that we must have
t_{1}>t_{2}
. Indeed,
\partial^{u,1}\tilde{R}_{i,j_{2}}
must be on the left of
\partial^{u,2}\tilde{R}_{i,j_{1}}
, otherwise we are in the situation where
j_{1}=j_{2}
. We then have:

l(\gamma)\leq l(\gamma_{|[0,t_{1}]})+l(\gamma_{|[t_{2},1]})\leq\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j_{1}})+\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j_{2}})\leq K\sigma.

This proves the third point of the claim.

For the last point, let
j\in I(i)
. We know that
f^{n}(R_{j})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j}=\emptyset
and
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j}\subset\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}
. In the Pesin chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
, the two curves
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,l})
, for
l\in\{1,2\}
, are
\gamma
-admissible
s
-manifolds of size
q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})/2
, up to reducing
\sigma
. These curves are horizontal graphs which do not intersect and we can suppose without loss of generality that
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i_{1}})
is below
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,2})
in
(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i}))
. Let
P
be the Jordan domain delimited by the
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,1})
and the vertical bands
\{\pm q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\}\times[-q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i}),q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})]
. Note that
P
contains both
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j}
and
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}
. Up to reduce
\sigma
we can ensure that
f^{n}(R_{j})\subset\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}}(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})))
. The curves
f^{n}(\partial^{u,l}R_{j})
are then contained in
(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}_{i}},u)
-manifolds
V^{u,l}
in
(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i}))
for
l=1,2
. By Proposition
2.16
, they intersect each
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,l})
in a unique point. The intersection between each
f^{n}(\partial^{u,k}R_{j})
and
P
is then composed by a single connected curve
c_{k}
. Now, since
f^{n}(R_{j})\cap\partial^{u,l}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j}=\emptyset
, the curve
c_{k}
is contained in
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i,j}
for
k=1,2
, and then in
\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}
. This implies that
f^{n}(R_{j})\cap\partial^{u,l}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}=\emptyset
for
l=1,2
.
∎

Repeating this construction for all
i\in\{1,...,L\}
gives the family
\mathcal{\tilde{R}}^{1}=(\tilde{R}_{1}^{1},...,\tilde{R}^{1}_{L})
.

Step k.
We now have to iterate this construction. Suppose that we have families
\mathcal{\tilde{R}}^{1},...,\mathcal{\tilde{R}}^{k}
of exactly
L

us
-rectangles which satisfy the following properties:

–

For each
i\in\{1,...,L\}
and each
l\in\{1,...,k\}
,
\tilde{R}^{l}_{i}
is a
us
-rectangle and
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
is an adapted chart for
\tilde{R}^{l}_{i}
for some
\hat{x}_{i}\in\hat{\Lambda}
.

–

For each
i\in\{1,...,L\}
, all the rectangles are nested:

R_{i}\subset\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}\subset...\subset\tilde{R}^{k}_{i}\ \text{and}\ \partial^{s,l}R_{i}\subset\partial^{s,l}\tilde{R}^{1}_{i}\subset...\subset\partial^{s,l}\tilde{R}^{k}_{i}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,l})

for some
\hat{y}_{i,l}\in\hat{\Lambda}
and for
l\in\{1,2\}
.

–

We have the following control of the geometry for each
i,l
:

\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{i}^{l})\leq K\sigma\ \text{and}\ \sigma^{s}_{\max}(W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,1}),W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,2}))\leq\sigma.

–

For each
i,l
, there exists a finite set
J^{l}(i)\subset\{1,...,L\}
such that:

\tilde{R}^{l}_{i}=\bigcup_{j\in J^{l}(i)}\tilde{R}^{l}_{i,j}

where
\tilde{R}^{l}_{i,j}
is a
us
-rectangle satisfying the conclusion of Lemma
3.12
. In particular, we have
\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}^{l}_{i,j})\leq K\sigma/2
.

–

For each
l_{1}\in\{1,...,k\}
, for each
i,j\in\{1,...,L\}
, and for each
l_{2}<l_{1}
, if
f^{n}(R_{j})\cap R_{i}\neq\emptyset
and if there exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{U})
such that
\pi(\hat{z})\in R_{j}
, then we have:

f^{n}(\tilde{R}^{l_{2}}_{j})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}^{l_{1}}_{i}=\emptyset.

Note that the families
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{0}:=\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{\tilde{R}}^{1}
satisfy these properties. The goal of this step is to construct a family
\mathcal{\tilde{R}}^{k+1}
such that the families
\mathcal{\tilde{R}}^{1},...,\mathcal{\tilde{R}}^{k+1}
will satisfy these previous properties.
