Let then
i\in\{1,...,L\}
. If for all
j\in I(i)
, we have
f^{n}(\tilde{R}^{k}_{j})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}^{k}_{i}=\emptyset
, then define
\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i}=\tilde{R}^{k}_{i}
. Suppose now that there exists
j\in I(i)
such that
f^{n}(\tilde{R}^{k}_{j})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}^{k}_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Let then
v\in J^{k}(j)
such that
f^{n}(\tilde{R}^{k}_{j,v})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}^{k}_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Denote by
J(j,i)
the set of such
v
. Apply then Lemma
3.12
with the rectangles
\tilde{R}^{k}_{j,v}
and
R_{i}
, and with the constant
K/2
. It gives us a
us
-rectangle
\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i,j,v}
. Define:

\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i,j}=\bigcup_{v\in J(j,i)}\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i,j,v}\ \text{and}\ \tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i}=\bigcup_{j\in I(i)}\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i,j}.

Claim 3.19
.

The family
\mathcal{\tilde{R}}^{k+1}=(\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{1},...,\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{L})
has the desired properties.

Proof.

The proof of the first two desired properties is the same as in Claim
3.18
. Note that we can write:

\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i}=\bigcup_{j\in I(i)}\bigcup_{v\in J(j,i)}\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i,j,v}.

This shows that
\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i}
is the union of a finite number of
us
-rectangles satisfying the conclusion of Lemma
3.12
, and in particular
\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i,j,v})\leq K\sigma/2
. Let us denote this finite set by
J^{k+1}(i)
. This gives us the fourth property. Once we can write:

\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i}=\bigcup_{j\in J^{k+1}(i)}\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i,j}

then the proof of the third property is the same as in Claim
3.18
. For the last property, take any
j\in I(i)
and any
v\in J^{k}(j)
such that
f^{n}(\tilde{R}^{k}_{j,v})\cap\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i}\neq\emptyset
. By construction, there exists
s\in J^{k+1}(i)
such that
f^{n}(\tilde{R}^{k}_{j,v})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i,s}=\emptyset
(the rectangle
\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i,s}
is
\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i,j,v}
constructed earlier). By applying the same argument as the proof of the last point in Claim
3.18
, we obtain that
f^{n}(\tilde{R}^{k}_{j,v})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}^{k+1}_{i}=\emptyset
. Since this is true for any
v\in J^{k}(j)
, this concludes the proof of the last property.
∎

This concludes the construction of the family
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{k+1}
. Combining Step 1 and Step k, we are then able to build a family
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{k}
satisfying the properties listed above for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Conclusion.
Let us define the following set:

\tilde{R}_{i}=\bigcup_{l\geq 1}\tilde{R}_{i}^{l}.

We claim that this set has the following properties:

–

For each
i\in\{1,...,L\}
,
\tilde{R}_{i}
is a
us
-rectangle and
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
is an adapted chart.

–

For each
i\in\{1,...,L\}
, we have the nested property:
R_{i}\subset\tilde{R}_{i}
and
\partial^{s,k}(R_{i})\subset\partial^{s,k}\tilde{R}_{i}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,k})
for
k\in\{1,2\}
.

–

\sigma/2\leq\sigma^{u}_{\min}(\tilde{R}_{i})\leq\sigma^{u}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{i})\leq K\sigma
and
\sigma^{s}_{\max}(\tilde{R}_{i})\leq\sigma
.

–

For each
i,j\in\{1,...,L\}
, if
f^{n}(R_{j})\cap R_{i}\neq\emptyset
and if there exists
\hat{z}\in\hat{U}\cap\hat{f}^{-n}\hat{U}
such that
\pi(\hat{z})\in R_{j}
, then we have:

f^{n}(\tilde{R}_{j})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}_{i}=\emptyset.

Let us denote by
V^{l}_{i,k}
the
\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u\big)
-manifold in
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}\big)
containing
\partial^{u,k}\tilde{R}^{l}_{i}
. Denote by
F^{l}_{i,k}
their representative function. Let us fix
i
and
k
. Let
q=\inf_{l}q(V^{l}_{i,k})
. Note that by definition,
q\geq 10^{-2}q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})
. Since we have
\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(\tilde{R}^{l}_{i})\leq K\sigma
for all
l\geq 1
, Lemma
3.10
implies that the distance between
F^{l_{1}}_{i,k}
and
F^{l_{2}}_{i,k}
on
[-q,q]
is uniformly bounded. Using the Arzéla-Ascoli theorem, up to taking a subsequence,
F^{l}_{i,k}
converges then to a continuous map
F_{i,k}
on
[-q,q]
. Note that
F^{l}_{i,k}
is still almost everywhere tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}}
. Denote by
V_{i,k}
the
\big(\mathscr{C}^{u,\hat{x}},u\big)
-manifold represented by
F_{i,k}
. Each
V_{i,k}
intersects each
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i,j})
in a unique point. This defines a
us
-rectangle. A similar argument as in Claim
3.18
shows that this
us
-rectangle is exactly
\tilde{R}_{i}
. This proves the first point.

The second and the third items are direct from the construction. Let us prove the last item by contradiction. Assume that there exists
j\in I(i)
such that
f^{n}(R_{j})\cap R_{i}\neq\emptyset
but
f^{n}(\tilde{R}^{j})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Since the
\tilde{R}^{l}_{j}
are nested open sets, there exists then some
l\geq 1
such that
f^{n}(\tilde{R}^{l}_{j})\cap\partial^{u}\tilde{R}_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Now, using a similar argument as in the proof of Claim
3.18
shows that this cannot be possible. This concludes the proof of Proposition
3.11
.

4.
Curves intersecting families of
us
-rectangles

In this section, we study the intersections of a curve
\gamma
with a family of
us
-rectangles
\mathcal{R}=\big(R_{1},\dots,R_{L}\big)
that satisfy the properties of the first family in Proposition
3.11
. More precisely, if we assume that
\gamma
does not intersect any stable boundary of the rectangles, we will show that the number of rectangles needed to cover the intersection between the curve
\gamma
and the union of the rectangles is bounded, and the bound does not depend on the number of rectangles.

4.1.
Statement of the curve-covering Proposition

Throughout this section, we consider
f:M\to M
a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
local diffeomorphism, with
r>1
, and
\mu
a saddle hyperbolic measure, possibly non-ergodic. We write
\hat{\mu}=\pi_{\star}^{-1}\mu
. We assume that
\mu
satisfies the hypothesis of Proposition
3.11
. Let us fix
\mathcal{R}=(R_{1},\dots,R_{L})
, a family of
us
-rectangles satisfying the same properties as the first family given by Proposition
3.11
. We recall here their properties. There exists a Pesin block
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
and two parameters
\sigma,\alpha>0
such that:

–

For any
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
, there exists
\hat{x}_{i}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
such that
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}_{i}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}_{i})\big)
is an adapted chart for
R_{i}
.

–

\partial^{s,l}R_{i}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i}^{l})
where
\hat{y}_{i}^{l}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
.

–

There exists a foliation
\mathscr{F}^{u}
of an open set
V\supset\pi(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l})
tangent to
\mathscr{C}^{u,ext,\alpha}
such that for any
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
the unstable boundary
\partial^{u,l}R_{i}
is contained in some leaf of
\mathscr{F}^{u}
. Moreover,
\partial^{u,l}R_{i}\neq\partial^{u,k}R_{j}
whenever
i\neq j
or
i=j
and
k\neq l
.

–

\sigma/2\leq\sigma^{u}_{\min}(R_{i})\leq\sigma^{u,\alpha}_{\max}(R_{i})\leq\sigma
.

Note that the family
\mathcal{R}
depends on the parameters
\sigma
and
\alpha
. Let us first define which curves we are considering. If
N
is a compact manifold and
\varphi:N\to M
is a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
map, we define the
\mathcal{C}^{r}
-size of
\varphi
to be the maximum of the norms of all the
k
-derivatives of
\varphi
in any chart, for
k=1,\dots,r
. We emphasize that the maximum is taken over all the charts of some fixed finite atlas on
M
and
N
. Note that the
\mathcal{C}^{r}
-size depends on the choice of atlases, but by compactness, changing atlases induces equivalent
\mathcal{C}^{r}
-sizes. In the present paper,
N
will often be
[0,1]
and the atlas we choose will be the trivial one.

Definition 4.1

(
(r,\epsilon)
-curves)
.

Let
r>1
and
\epsilon>0
. Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
be a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
curve. We say that
\gamma
is a
(r,\epsilon)
-curve if
\lVert\gamma\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{r}}<\epsilon
.

In this section, we fix a
(r,C\sigma)
-curve
\gamma
. We will often denote by
\gamma
the image of
[0,1]
under the map
\gamma
. We assume that
\gamma
intersects the rectangles:

\gamma\cap\big(\bigcup_{i}R_{i}\big)\neq\emptyset.

To study the intersection between the curve
\gamma
and the rectangles, we will introduce two different types of intersecting intervals. See Figure
6
.

Definition 4.2

(Crossing/folding intervals)
.

Let
[s,t]\subset[0,1]
and let
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
.

We say that
[s,t]
is a crossing
(\gamma,R_{i})
-interval if:

–

\gamma(s)\in\partial^{u,1}R_{i}
and
\gamma(t)\in\partial^{u,2}R_{i}
or vice versa.

–

\gamma((s,t))\subset R_{i}
.

We say that
[s,t]
is a crossing
(\gamma,\mathcal{R})
-interval if there exists some
i
such that
[s,t]
is a crossing
(\gamma,R_{i})
-interval.

We say that
[s,t]
is a folding
(\gamma,R_{i})
-interval if:

–

\gamma(s),\gamma(t)\in\partial^{u,l}R_{i}
for some
l\in\{1,2\}
.

–

\gamma((s,t))\subset R_{i}
.

We say that
[s,t]
is a folding
(\gamma,\mathcal{R})
-interval if there exists some
i
such that
[s,t]
is a folding
(\gamma,R_{i})
-interval.

We denote by
\text{Cross}(\gamma,R_{i})
the collection of all crossing
(\gamma,R_{i})
-intervals and
\text{Cross}(\gamma,\mathcal{R})
the collection of all crossing
(\gamma,\mathcal{R})
-intervals. We denote by
\text{Fold}(\gamma,R_{i})
the collection of all folding
(\gamma,R_{i})
-intervals and
\text{Fold}(\gamma,\mathcal{R})
the collection of all folding
(\gamma,\mathcal{R})
-intervals.

When there is no ambiguity about the family of
us
-rectangles
\mathcal{R}
we consider, we will often omit the dependence on
\mathcal{R}
.

(a)

A crossing
\gamma
-interval.

(b)

A folding
\gamma
-interval.

Figure 6
.

The goal of this section is to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 4.3
.

Fix
r>1
. Let
\sigma,\alpha>0
. Let
\mathcal{R}:=(R_{1},\dots,R_{L})
be a family of
us
-rectangles satisfying the setting given before Definition
4.1
, i.e., they satisfy the properties of the first family in Proposition
3.11
. Fix
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
a
(r,C\sigma)
-curve such that
\gamma\cap\cup_{i}R_{i}\neq\emptyset
. Suppose the following:

(4.1)

\gamma\cap W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i}^{l})=\emptyset

where
\partial^{s,l}R_{i}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i}^{l})
for
\hat{y}_{i}^{l}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
. Then, the following holds for
\sigma:=\sigma(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l},C)
sufficiently small.

There exist two collections
\mathcal{C}_{1}\subset\text{Cross}(\gamma)
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}\subset\{1,\dots,L\}
with the following properties:

(1)

\text{Cross}(\gamma)=\bigcup_{[s,t]\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}[s,t]
. Here we make the slight abuse of notation by considering
\text{Cross}(\gamma)
as a union of intervals.

(2)

|\mathcal{C}_{1}|\leq 4C
.

(3)

\gamma\big(\text{Fold}(\gamma)\big)=\gamma\big(\text{Fold}(\gamma)\big)\cap\bigcup_{i\in\mathcal{C}_{2}}R_{i}
. Here we also make the slight abuse of notation by considering
\text{Fold}(\gamma)
as a union of intervals.

(4)

|\mathcal{C}_{2}|\leq 8C
.

(5)

There exist
s_{1},s_{2}\in[0,1]
such that:

\gamma([0,1])\cap\bigcup_{i}R_{i}=\gamma\big([0,s_{1}]\big)\cup\gamma\big(\text{Fold}(\gamma)\big)\cup\gamma\big(\text{Cross}(\gamma)\big)\cup\gamma\big([s_{2},1]\big)

where there exist
i,j\in\{1,\dots,L\}
such that
\gamma([0,s_{1}))\subset R_{i}
and
\gamma((s_{2},1])\subset R_{j}
. If
s_{1}=0
or
s_{2}=1
, we consider
[0,s_{1}]
or
[s_{2},1]
to be empty.

Let us make some comments on the previous statement. The idea is that we can choose a small number of crossing
\gamma
-intervals to cover the whole set of such intervals by controlling their lengths. In the case of folding intervals, this is no longer possible because we are not able to control their lengths. Nevertheless, we can pick a small number of
us
-rectangles from the family
\mathcal{R}
to cover the whole set of folding
\gamma
-intervals. In both cases, it is very important that the bound is uniform, in the sense that it depends only on the constant
C
. The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

4.2.
Key lemmas for treating crossing and folding intervals

Throughout this paragraph, we assume that we are in the same setting as Proposition
4.3
. We begin by estimating the size of a crossing
\gamma
-interval.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
[s,t]\in\text{Cross}(\gamma)
. Then we have:

|t-s|\geq\frac{1}{2C}.

Proof.

Let
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
such that
[s,t]\in\text{Cross}(\gamma,R_{i})
. Recall that we then have:

–

\gamma(s)\in\partial^{u,1}R_{i}
,

–

\gamma(t)\in\partial^{u,2}R_{i}
,

–

\gamma((s,t))\subset R_{i}
.

Let
\tilde{\gamma}:[0,1]\to M
be the curve defined by:

\tilde{\gamma}(x)=\gamma\big((t-s)x+s\big).

Then, the curve
\tilde{\gamma}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
curve such that
\tilde{\gamma}(0)\in\partial^{u,1}R_{i}
and
\tilde{\gamma}(1)\in\partial^{u,2}R_{i}
. We then have:

l(\tilde{\gamma})\geq\sigma^{u}_{\min}(R_{i})\geq\sigma/2.

On the other hand, since
\gamma
is a
(r,C\sigma)
-curve, we have:

l(\tilde{\gamma})=\int_{0}^{1}\lVert\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(x)\rVert dx=\int_{s}^{t}\lVert\gamma^{\prime}(x)\rVert dx<|t-s|C\sigma.

Then, putting everything together leads to:

|t-s|>\frac{1}{2C}.

∎

Let us continue with a useful lemma on the geometry of the intersection between the curve
\gamma
and the family of
us
-rectangles
\mathcal{R}
. The idea is that, using (
4.1
), the curve
\gamma
must be contained in a domain delimited by two stable manifolds containing the stable boundaries of rectangles intersecting
\gamma
. See Figure
7
.

Lemma 4.5
.

The following holds for
\sigma:=\sigma(\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l})
sufficiently small.

There exists a chart
\Psi:(0,1)^{2}\to M
that displays the following properties.

–

\gamma([0,1])\subset\Psi((0,1)^{2})
.

–

For any
R\in\mathcal{R}
such that
R
intersects
\gamma
, the image under
\Psi^{-1}
of each unstable boundary of
R
is a vertical Lipschitz graph that separates
(0,1)^{2}
into two disjoint open connected components. This gives a natural meaning to being on the left (or the right) of such a curve.

In particular, for any
R\in\mathcal{R}
, if there exist
s,t\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(s)
is on the left of the left unstable boundary of
R
and
\gamma(t)
is on the right of the right unstable boundary of
R
, then the interval
[s,t]
contains a crossing
\gamma
-interval.

Figure 7
.
The proof of Lemma
4.5
in the case where
\gamma
intersects three rectangles
R_{i},R_{j}
and
R_{k}
. Horizontals represent the stable direction. The chart
\Psi
is given by the colored domain.

Proof.

If
\gamma
does not intersect any
R\in\mathcal{R}
, there is nothing to prove. Let
R\in\mathcal{R}
such that
\gamma([0,1])\cap R\neq\emptyset
. Let
\hat{x}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
and let
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
be an adapted chart for
R
. Recall that since
\gamma
is a
(r,C\sigma)
-curve, taking
\sigma
uniformly small enough implies that
\gamma([0,1])\subset\Psi_{\hat{x}}\big(R(0,q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))\big)
. Recall that for any
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
, each of the stable boundaries of
R^{\prime}
is contained in the local stable manifold of some point of
\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
. Let
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
be a
us
-rectangle intersecting
\gamma
, let
l\in\{1,2\}
and let
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
such that
\partial^{s,l}R^{\prime}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y})
. Up to reducing the size of the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
by a uniform factor, the curve
\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}(W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}))
is a horizontal Lipschitz graphs of size
q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})
. Such Lipschits graphs separates the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
into two disjoints open connected component and (
4.1
) implies that
\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}(\gamma)
is contained in one of these two connected components. This implies that there exist
\hat{y}^{+},\hat{y}^{-}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\chi,\epsilon,l}
and a Jordan domain
Q
displaying the following properties.

–

The curve
\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}(\gamma)
is contained in
Q
.

–

Its boundary is the union of the four following curves.

\bullet

The two Lipschitz horizontal graphs of size
q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})
that are the images of
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}^{+})
and
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}^{-})
under
\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}
.

\bullet

The left and right vertical boundaries of the chart
\big(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\big)
, i.e.,
\{\pm q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})\}\times[-q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}),q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x})]
.

–

For any
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
and any
\hat{y}
such that
\partial^{s,l}R^{\prime}\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y})
, the curve
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y})
does not intersect the domain
Q
. Note that
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y})
has to be above
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}^{+})
or below
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}^{-})
(up to changing the notation) in the chart
(\Psi_{\hat{x}},q_{\tilde{\epsilon}}(\hat{x}))
.

Note that for any rectangle
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
that intersects
\gamma
, we have that
\partial^{u,l}R^{\prime}
separates
Q
into two disjoint open connected components. Indeed, (
4.1
) implies that
\Psi_{\hat{x}}^{-1}(\gamma)
must be contained in the Jordan domain formed by the left and right vertical boundaries of the chart and the two horizontal graphs defined by the local stable manifolds containing
\partial^{s}R^{\prime}
. Remark that this domain is separated into two disjoint open connected components by each of the
\partial^{u,l}R^{\prime}
. Since local stable manifolds are either equal or disjoint, we also obtain that
Q
must be contained inside this domain. We conclude that
Q
is also separated into two disjoint open connected components by each of the
\partial^{u,l}R^{\prime}
.

Take now a diffeomorphism
\varphi:[0,1]^{2}\to\overline{Q}
that sends each horizontal, respectively vertical, boundary of
(0,1)^{2}
to each horizontal, respectively vertical, boundary of
Q
. Since
\varphi
is a diffeomorphism, it preserves the properties of
Q
discussed above. Taking
\Psi=\Psi_{\hat{x}}\circ\varphi
then concludes the proof, since the last statement is obvious by connectedness of
\gamma
.
∎

4.3.
Proof of the curve-covering Proposition
4.3

We split the proof into two distinct parts. In the first part, we treat the crossing intervals using Lemma
4.4
. In the second part, we study the folding intervals. Before going into the details, let us quickly explain the idea of the proof. First, in the case of crossing intervals, Lemma
4.4
tells us that crossing
\gamma
-intervals are long. Then, using the fact that the unstable boundaries of the
us
-rectangles of the family
\mathcal{R}
are disjoint, we conclude that if some crossing
\gamma
-intervals intersect, then taking the first and the last intervals of the intersection is enough to cover all the intersecting intervals. The case of folding intervals is more delicate. We use the fact that
\gamma
cannot cross the stable manifolds containing the stable boundaries of the rectangles to conclude that
\gamma
is trapped in a rectangular domain where each unstable boundary of each rectangle of
\mathcal{R}
separates it into two disjoint connected components. We use this fact to select a uniformly small number of rectangles containing the image of all the folding
\gamma
-intervals between two crossing
\gamma
-intervals.

Step 1: A bound on the number of crossing
\gamma
-intervals.

Let us start with the crossing intervals. Since the number of crossing
\gamma
-intervals is at most countable (they cannot accumulate since they have uniform length by Lemma
4.4
), we can order the starting parameter
s
of all crossing
\gamma
-intervals
[s,t]
. Denote by
m
the cardinality of
\text{Cross}(\gamma)
. Note that Lemma
4.4
implies that there exists at most a finite number of disjoint crossing
\gamma
-intervals. Combining this with the fact that
\mathcal{R}
has finite cardinality, we obtain that
m
must be finite. We can then write:

\text{Cross}(\gamma)=\big\{[s_{i},t_{i}],\ i\in\{1,\dots,m\}\big\},

where
s_{1}\leq\dots\leq s_{m}
.

For any
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, define
j(i)\in\{1,\dots,m\}
to be the integer such that:

s_{j(i)}=\inf\{s_{j},\ j\in\{1,\dots,m\}\ \text{such that}\ s_{j}>t_{i}\}.

If such an integer does not exist, set
j(i)=m+1
with
s_{m+1}=t_{m+1}=1
. The integer
s_{j(i)}
is just the starting parameter of the next crossing
\gamma
-interval disjoint from
[s_{i},t_{i}]
.

We are going to build the collection
\mathcal{C}_{1}
by induction. The idea is to start with a crossing
\gamma
-interval
[s_{i},t_{i}]
that we add to
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. Then we can cover all the crossing
\gamma
-intervals that are not disjoint from
[s_{i},t_{i}]
by the union of
[s_{i},t_{i}]
and the last crossing
\gamma
-interval intersecting
[s_{i},t_{i}]
. We also add this one to
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and move to the next crossing interval disjoint from
[s_{i},t_{i}]
to repeat the argument. Lemma
4.4
then gives a bound on
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. This is contained in the following claim. See Figure
8
.

Claim 4.6
.

Let
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. The following is true:

[s_{i},t_{j(i)-1}]\subset[s_{i},t_{i}]\cup[s_{j(i)-1},t_{j(i)-1}].

Proof.

Let
s\in[s_{i},t_{j(i)-1}]
. For any
l\in\{i,\dots,j(i)-1\}
, the interval
[s_{l},t_{l}]
intersects the interval
[s_{i},t_{i}]
since, by definition of
j(i)
, we have
s_{l}\leq t_{i}
. Then, either we have
s_{l}\leq s_{j(i)-1}
and
s\in[s_{i},t_{i}]
, or
s_{l}>s_{j(i)-1}
and
s\in[s_{j(i)-1},t_{j(i)-1}]
.
∎

Figure 8
.
Proof of Claim
4.6
.

We now explain the induction. We start by letting
\mathcal{C}_{1,1}=\{[s_{1},t_{1}],[s_{j(1)},t_{j(1)}]\}
. Note that by Claim
4.6
we have:

\bigcup_{i=1}^{j(1)-1}[s_{i},t_{i}]\subset\bigcup_{[s,t]\in\mathcal{C}_{1,1}}[s,t].

Suppose that there exists a collection of crossing
\gamma
-intervals
\mathcal{C}_{1,n}=\{[\tilde{s}_{1},\tilde{t}_{1}],\dots,[\tilde{s}_{2n},\tilde{t}_{2n}]\}
, where
\tilde{s}_{2n-1}=s_{j^{n-1}(1)}
and
\tilde{s}_{2n}=s_{j^{n}(1)-1}
, and such that:

\bigcup_{i=1}^{j^{n}(1)-1}[s_{i},t_{i}]\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{2n}[\tilde{s}_{i},\tilde{t}_{i}].

where we define
j^{n}(1):=j(j^{n-1}(1))
by induction, with
j(m+1)=m+1
. Suppose also that every intervals with odd indices are pairwise disjoints. Let us explain how we build
\mathcal{C}_{1,n+1}
.

Let
k=j^{n}(1)
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{1,n+1}=\mathcal{C}_{1,n}\cup\{[s_{k},t_{k}],[s_{j(k)-1},t_{j(k)-1}]\}
. Combining the induction hypothesis and Claim
4.6
we have:

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i=1}^{j^{n+1}(1)-1}[s_{i},t_{i}]

\displaystyle\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{2n}[\tilde{s}_{i},\tilde{t}_{i}]\cup[s_{k},t_{k}]\cup[s_{j(k)-1},t_{j(k)-1}].

The disjointness property is also satisfied since
k=j(j^{n-1}(1))
and
s_{2n-1}=s_{j^{n-1}(1)}
. We stop the induction when we reach
n_{\star}
such that
j^{n_{\star}}(1)=m+1
. We let
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}_{1,n_{\star}}
. Note that we have by construction:

\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}[s_{i},t_{i}]\subset\bigcup_{[s,t]\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}[s,t].

Note that the disjointness property combined with Lemma
4.4
implies that
|\mathcal{C}_{1}|\leq 4C
since there are at most
2C
pairwise disjoint crossing
\gamma
-intervals. This proves the first two points of Proposition
4.3
.

Step 2: A bound on the number of rectangles needed to cover the folding
\gamma
-intervals.

Contrary to Step 1, the number of folding
\gamma
-intervals can be infinite. Moreover, it may be possible to have a sequence of
(\gamma,R_{i})
-folding intervals accumulating to a point, for some
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
, and then the set
\text{Fold}(\gamma)
may not be orderable as in Step 1. Nevertheless, since the family
\mathcal{R}
is finite and their unstable boundaries are contained in different leaves of
\mathscr{F}^{u}
, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
such that:

(4.2)

\max_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in\{1,\dots,L\}}\max_{l_{1},l_{2}\in\{1,2\}}d\big(\partial^{u,l_{1}}R_{i_{1}},\partial^{u,l_{2}}R_{i_{2}}\big)\geq C^{\prime}.

We construct inductively a family
\mathcal{R}^{\star}
of rectangles which cover the curve
\gamma
and such that the following holds. Let
t\in(0,1)
be the ending parameter of a crossing
\gamma
-interval and let
s>t
be the starting parameter of the first crossing
\gamma
-interval after
t
. Then the number of rectangle of
\mathcal{R}^{\star}
needed to cover
\gamma([t,s])
is uniformly bounded.

Lemma 4.7
.

There exists a finite collection of
us
-rectangles
\mathcal{R}^{\star}:=\{R_{i_{1}},\dots,R_{i_{m}}\}\subset\mathcal{R}
and a finite collection of parameters
t_{0}=0<t_{1}<\dots<t_{m+1}=1
that have the following properties.

–

For any
k=1,\dots,m
, the parameter
t_{k}
is the ending parameter of a crossing
\gamma
-interval
I_{k}
.

–

The intervals
(I_{k})_{k=1,\dots,m}
are pairwise disjoint.

–

i_{m}\leq 4m
.

–

We have the following covering property:

\gamma\big(\text{Fold}(\gamma)\big)=\gamma\big(\text{Fold}(\gamma)\big)\cap\big(\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}R_{i_{m}}\big).

Proof.

The proof goes by induction. Let
t_{0}=0
and
\mathcal{R}^{\star}_{0}=\emptyset
. Let
n\geq 1
. Suppose the following. There exists a collection of
us
-rectangles
\mathcal{R}^{\star}_{n-1}:=\{R_{i_{1}},\dots,R_{i_{n-1}}\}\subset\mathcal{R}
and a sequence of parameters
t_{0}=0<\dots<t_{n-1}
with the following properties.

–

For any
k=1,\dots,n-1
, the parameter
t_{k}
is the ending parameter of a crossing
\gamma
-interval
I_{k}
.

–

The intervals
(I_{k})_{k=1,\dots,n-1}
are pairwise disjoint.

–

i_{n-1}\leq 4(n-1)
.

–

We have the following covering property. For any folding
\gamma
-interval
[s,t]\subset[0,t_{n-1}]
we have:

[s,t]\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^{i_{n-1}}R_{j}.

If
t_{n-1}=1
, the induction stops and the claim is proven. Suppose then that
t_{n-1}<1
. We are now going to explain how to construct the family
\mathcal{R}^{\star}_{n}
and the parameter
t_{n}
such that all the previous listed properties are still satisfied at rank
n
.

By Lemma
4.5
, we can suppose that the curve
\gamma
and its intersection with any
us
-rectangle
R\in\mathcal{R}
is contained in
(0,1)^{2}
. Moreover, for any
l=1,2
, the curve
\partial^{u,l}R
separates
(0,1)^{2}
into two disjoint open connected components. Recall that this property gives a meaning to being on the left (or the right) of
\partial^{u,l}R
. Let
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
such that both
R
and
R^{\prime}
intersect
\gamma
. We say that
R
is on the left (or the right) of
R^{\prime}
if the left (or the right) unstable boundary of
R
is on the left (or the right) of the left (or the right) unstable boundary of
R^{\prime}
.

We say that a folding
\gamma
-interval
[s,t]
is a left (or right) folding
\gamma
-interval if
\gamma(s)
belongs to the left (or the right) unstable boundary of its associated rectangle.

Let us consider the next end of a crossing
\gamma
-interval starting after
t_{n-1}
. Define:

t_{c}=\inf\{t\in(t_{n-1},1]\ \exists s>t_{n-1},\ [s,t]\in\text{Cross}(\gamma)\}.

If such a crossing interval does not exist, we let
t_{c}=1
. Let
s_{c}\in(s^{\prime}_{1},t_{c})
be the starting parameter of the crossing
\gamma
-interval ending at
t_{c}
and let
R_{c}\in\mathcal{R}
be the
us
-rectangle associated to
[s_{c},t_{c}]
. Remark that since
s_{c}>t_{n-1}
, the interval
[s_{c},t_{c}]
is disjoint from all the
I_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,n-1
.

From now on, and until the end of the proof of Lemma
4.7
, we suppose that
t_{n-1}
belongs to the right boundary of the rectangle associated to the crossing
\gamma
-interval
I_{n-1}
. The case where
t_{n-1}
belongs to the left boundary is exactly symmetric.

Step 2.a: Select the rectangles to cover the folding intervals starting before
t_{n-1}
and ending before
t_{c}
.

Note that the induction allows us to cover the folding
\gamma
-intervals included in
[0,t_{n-1}]
. We begin by covering such intervals that intersect
[0,t_{n-1}]
but are not contained in it.

Claim 4.8
.

There exist two
us
-rectangles
R_{i_{n}},R_{i_{n+1}}\in\mathcal{R}
that display the following property. For any folding
\gamma
-interval
[s,t]
satisfying:

s<t_{n-1}\quad\text{and}\quad t_{n-1}<t<t_{c},

then
\gamma([s,t])
is contained in either
R_{i_{n}}
or
R_{i_{n-1}}
.

Proof.

We are first going to prove that there exists a rectangle
R_{i_{n}}\in\mathcal{R}
that contains all the left folding
\gamma
-intervals intersecting
[0,t_{n-1}]
, which are not contained in it. Note that by (
4.2
), this set is finite. Denote it as
\{[s^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}],\dots,[s^{\prime}_{l},t^{\prime}_{l}]\}
such that
s^{\prime}_{1}<\dots<s^{\prime}_{l}
. We are going to prove that the following order holds:

s^{\prime}_{1}<\dots<s^{\prime}_{l}\leq t_{n-1}<t^{\prime}_{l}<\dots<t^{\prime}_{1}.

For any
k\in\{1,\dots,l\}
, denote the
us
-rectangle associated with
[s^{\prime}_{k},t^{\prime}_{k}]
by
R^{\prime}_{k}
. Using the chart given by Lemma
4.5
and since
s^{\prime}_{1}<\dots<s^{\prime}_{l}
, the following property holds. For any
k\in\{1,\dots,l-1\}
the left unstable boundary of
R^{\prime}_{k}
is on the left of the left unstable boundary of
R^{\prime}_{k+1}
. Indeed, suppose by contradiction that there exists
k\in\{1,\dots,l-1\}
such that the left unstable boundary of
R^{\prime}_{k}
is on the right of the left unstable boundary of
R^{\prime}_{k+1}
. Since
s^{\prime}_{k}<s^{\prime}_{k+1}
, the curve
\gamma
crosses first the left unstable boundary of
R^{\prime}_{k}
and then the left unstable boundary of
R^{\prime}_{k+1}
. But
\gamma
must then cross again the left unstable boundary of
R^{\prime}_{k}
before
\gamma(s^{\prime}_{k+1})
. It implies that
t^{\prime}_{k}<s^{\prime}_{k+1}
, which is a contradiction.

Since any
s^{\prime}_{k}\leq t_{n-1}<t^{\prime}_{1}
for
k=1,\dots,l
, we know that
\gamma
must cross first all the left unstable boundaries of
R^{\prime}_{k}
for
k=2,\dots,l
before crossing again the left unstable boundary of
R^{\prime}_{1}
. This property combined with the order on the left unstable boundaries of the
R^{\prime}_{k}
implies the desired order
s^{\prime}_{1}<\dots<s^{\prime}_{l}<t^{\prime}_{l}<\dots<t^{\prime}_{1}
.

We then proved that for any
k=2,\dots,l
we have
[s^{\prime}_{k},t^{\prime}_{k}]\subset[s^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}]
. But since
\gamma([s^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}])\subset R^{\prime}_{1}
, we obtain that any left folding
\gamma
-interval
[s,t]
intersecting
[0,t_{n-1}]
, which is not contained in
[0,t_{n-1}]
, satisfies
\gamma([s,t])\subset R^{\prime}_{1}
. We then define
R_{i_{n}}:=R^{\prime}_{1}
.

The argument is the same for the right folding
\gamma
-intervals intersecting
[0,t_{n-1}]
, but not contained in it. Denote the rectangle containing them by
R_{i_{n+1}}
. This concludes the proof of Claim
4.8
.
∎

Step 2.b: Select the rectangles to cover the intervals starting after
t_{n-1}
and find the next crossing
\gamma
-interval.

We now want to select rectangles to cover the folding
\gamma
-intervals included in
(t_{n-1},t_{c})
. We are first going to define three time parameters that will be crucial to choose these rectangles.

Define first
s^{\prime}_{1}\in[t_{n-1},1]
to be the first starting parameter of a folding
\gamma
-interval after
t_{n-1}
.

s^{\prime}_{1}=\inf\{s\in[t_{n-1},1],\ \exists\ t>s,\ [s,t]\in\text{Fold}(\gamma)\}.

If such a parameter does not exist, there is no folding
\gamma
-interval left after
t_{n-1}
and we set
t_{n}=1
and
\mathcal{R}^{\star}_{n}=\{R_{i_{1}},\dots,R_{i_{n+1}}\}
, which stops the induction and proves Lemma
4.7
. Suppose then that such a parameter exists and let
t^{\prime}_{1}\in[0,1]
and
R^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
such that
[s^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}]\in\text{Fold}(\gamma,R^{\prime}_{1})
. Without loss of generality, we can suppose that
[s^{\prime}_{1},t^{\prime}_{1}]
is a left folding
\gamma
-interval.

Secondly, define
s^{\prime}_{2}\in[s^{\prime}_{1},1)
to be the next starting parameter of a folding
\gamma
-interval associated to a rectangle disjoint from
R^{\prime}_{1}
. Equation (
4.2
) guarantees that
s^{\prime}_{2}>s^{\prime}_{1}
.

s^{\prime}_{2}=\inf\{s\in(s^{\prime}_{1},1),\ \exists\ R\in\mathcal{R},\ R\cap R^{\prime}_{1}=\emptyset,\ \exists\ t>s,\ [s,t]\in\text{Fold}(\gamma,R)\}.

Let
t^{\prime}_{2}>s^{\prime}_{2}
and
R^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathcal{R}
such that
[s^{\prime}_{2},t^{\prime}_{2}]\in\text{Fold}(\gamma,R^{\prime}_{2})
. If such an interval does not exist, we set
s^{\prime}_{2}=t^{\prime}_{2}=1
and
R^{\prime}_{2}=\emptyset
.

Finally, define
s^{\prime}_{3}>s^{\prime}_{2}
to be the starting parameter of the first folding
\gamma
-interval associated to a rectangle disjoint from
R^{\prime}_{1}
and
R^{\prime}_{2}
. If
s^{\prime}_{2}=1
, set
s^{\prime}_{3}=1
. Otherwise, set:

s^{\prime}_{3}=\inf\{s\in(s^{\prime}_{2},1),\ \exists\ R\in\mathcal{R},\ R\cap R^{\prime}_{1}=\emptyset\ \text{and}\ R\cap R^{\prime}_{2}=\emptyset,\ \exists\ t>s,\ [s,t]\in\text{Fold}(\gamma,R)\}.

Let
t^{\prime}_{3}>s^{\prime}_{3}
and
R^{\prime}_{3}\in\mathcal{R}
such that
[s^{\prime}_{3},t^{\prime}_{3}]\in\text{Fold}(\gamma,R^{\prime}_{3})
. If such an interval does not exist, we set
s^{\prime}_{3}=t^{\prime}_{3}=1
and
R^{\prime}_{3}=\emptyset
. Remark the following important fact:
t_{c}<s_{3}^{\prime}
. Indeed, since the three rectangles
R_{1}^{\prime},R_{2}^{\prime}
and
R_{3}^{\prime}
are pairwise disjoints, we can suppose without loss of generality that
R_{1}^{\prime}
is the leftmost,
R_{2}^{\prime}
is in the middle and
R_{3}^{\prime}
is the rightmost. Then
\gamma(s_{1}^{\prime})
is on the left of the left unstable boundary of
R_{2}^{\prime}
and
\gamma(s_{3}^{\prime})
is on the right of the right unstable boundary of
R_{2}^{\prime}
. By Lemma
4.5
, it implies the existence of a crossing
\gamma
-interval ending before
s_{3}^{\prime}
.

Claim 4.9
.

There exist two
us
-rectangles
R_{i_{n+2}},R_{i_{n+3}}\in\mathcal{R}
that display the following property. For any folding
\gamma
-interval
[s,t]\subset[s^{\prime}_{1},t_{c}]
, we have
\gamma([s,t])\subset R_{i_{n+2}}\cup R_{i_{n+3}}
.

Proof.

Let us first prove that for any left folding
\gamma
-interval
[s,t]\subset[s^{\prime}_{1},t_{c}]
, we have
\gamma([s,t])\subset R^{\prime}_{1}\cup R_{i_{n}}\cup R_{i_{n+1}}
. Let
[s,t]
be such an interval.

Suppose first that
s^{\prime}_{1}<s<s^{\prime}_{2}
. This implies that
[s,t]
is associated to a
us
-rectangle
R\in\mathcal{R}
that intersects
R^{\prime}_{1}
. Define
R_{1}^{\star}
to be the rectangle intersecting
R_{1}^{\prime}
displaying the following properties.

–

There exists a left folding
\gamma
-interval
[s_{\star},t_{\star}]
associated to
R_{1}^{\star}
such that
s_{\star}>t_{n-1}
.

–

Let any rectangle
R
intersecting
R_{1}^{\star}
and such that there exists a left folding
\gamma
-interval, starting after
t_{n-1}
, associated to
R
. Then the left unstable boundary of
R
is on the right of the left unstable boundary of
R_{1}^{\star}
.

Let us prove that
\gamma([s,t])\subset R_{1}^{\star}
. Indeed, since the left unstable boundary of
R
is on the right of the left unstable boundary of
R_{1}^{\star}
, then
\gamma([s,t])
intersects
R_{1}^{\star}
. Suppose now that
\gamma([s,t])
is not contained in
R_{1}^{\star}
. Then there must exists a parameter
u\in(s,t)
such that
\gamma(u)
is on the right of the right unstable boundary of
R_{1}^{\star}
. But since
\gamma(s_{\star})
is on the left of the left unstable boundary of
R_{1}^{\star}
and
s_{\star}<t_{c}
, there exists a crossing
\gamma
-interval associated to
R_{1}^{\star}
ending before
t_{c}
, which is a contradiction.

Letting
R_{i_{n+2}}:=R_{1}^{\star}
, we then proved that the image by
\gamma
of any folding
\gamma
-interval
[s,t]\subset[s^{\prime}_{1},t_{c}]
, such that
s<s^{\prime}_{2}
, is contained in
R_{i_{n+2}}
.

Suppose now that
s^{\prime}_{2}\leq s<s^{\prime}_{3}
. This implies that
[s,t]
is associated to a
us
-rectangle
R\in\mathcal{R}
that intersects either
R^{\prime}_{1}
or
R^{\prime}_{2}
. If
R
intersects
R^{\prime}_{1}
, then by the same argument as before we must have
\gamma([s,t])\subset R_{i_{n+2}}
. Suppose then that
R
intersects
R^{\prime}_{2}
. Note that
R^{\prime}_{2}
must be on the left of
R^{\prime}_{1}
, otherwise Lemma
4.5
would give the existence of a crossing
(\gamma,R^{\prime}_{1})
-interval that ends before
s^{\prime}_{2}
, but we have
s^{\prime}_{2}\leq s\leq t_{c}
, which is a contradiction. A similar argument shows that
[s^{\prime}_{2},t^{\prime}_{2}]
must be a right folding
\gamma
-interval. So if
s>s^{\prime}_{2}
is the starting point of a left folding
\gamma
-interval, then, again, there must exist a crossing
(\gamma,R)
-interval ending before
s
, by Lemma
4.5
, which is a contradiction. The case
s^{\prime}_{2}\leq s<s^{\prime}_{3}
is then impossible.

To sum up, since
t_{c}<s^{\prime}_{3}
, any left folding
\gamma
-interval
[s,t]
such that
[s,t]\subset[s^{\prime}_{1},t_{c}]
satisfies
\gamma([s,t])\subset R_{i_{n+2}}
. By symmetric arguments, there exists a rectangle
R_{i_{n+3}}\in\mathcal{R}
displaying the following property. Any right folding
\gamma
-interval
[s,t]
such that
[s,t]\subset[s^{\prime}_{1},t_{c}]
satisfies
\gamma([s,t])\subset R_{i_{n+3}}
. This proves Claim
4.9
.
∎

We then define
t_{n}:=t_{c}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\star}_{n}=\{R_{i_{1}},\dots,R_{i_{n+3}}\}
. Claim
4.8
and Claim
4.9
allow us to complete the induction at rank
n
. We stop when we reach some
N
such that
t_{N}=1
, which has to happen since the number of crossing
\gamma
-intervals is finite.

Step 2.d: The covering family
\mathcal{R}^{\star}
and a bound on its cardinality.

By Lemma
4.7
, the family
\mathcal{R}^{\star}=\{R_{i_{1}},\dots,R_{i_{m}}\}
contains the image under
\gamma
of all the folding
\gamma
-intervals. Let us now give a bound on its cardinality. We know by Lemma
4.4
that the number of disjoint crossing
\gamma
-intervals is smaller than
2C
. We then have that the number of intervals
(I_{k})_{k=1,\dots,m}
from Lemma
4.7
is smaller than
2C
and then:

|\mathcal{R}^{\star}|\leq 4m\leq 8C.

This proves the third and fourth points of Proposition
4.3
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
4.7
.
∎

Step 3: The remaining part of
\gamma
.

In this step, we conclude the proof. The remaining part of the intersection between
\gamma
and the union of the rectangles of
\mathcal{R}
may be composed of the beginning and the end of the curve.

If
\gamma(0)\in M\setminus\cup_{i}R_{i}
, we define
s_{1}=0
. If
\gamma(0)\in R_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
, we define
s_{1}\in[0,1]
to be the first
s\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(s)\in\partial^{u}R_{i}
. If such an
s
does not exist, we let
s_{1}=1
. Note that if
s_{1}=1
, Proposition
4.3
holds trivially.

If
\gamma(1)\in M\setminus\cup_{i}R_{i}
, we define
s_{2}=1
. If
\gamma(1)\in R_{j}
for some
j\in\{1,\dots,L\}
, we define
s_{2}
to be the last
s\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(s)\in\partial^{u}R_{j}
. If such an
s
does not exist, we let
s_{2}=0
. Again, note that in this case Proposition
4.3
holds trivially.

Finally, by (
4.1
), if
y\in\gamma([0,1])\cap\cup_{i}R_{i}
and
y\notin\gamma([0,s_{1}])\cup\gamma([s_{2},1])
, then there exists an interval
[s,t]\subset[0,1]
with
y\in\gamma([s,t])
such that
\gamma(s),\gamma(t)\in\partial^{u}R_{i}
and
\gamma((s,t))\in R_{i}
. Thus
y
is contained either in a crossing or a folding
\gamma
-interval. This concludes the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

5.
Homoclinic classes for endomorphisms

Throughout this section,
f:M\rightarrow M
is a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
local diffeomorphism, for
r>1
.

5.1.
Homoclinic classes of hyperbolic periodic orbits and hyperbolic measures of saddle type

Let
\hat{\mathcal{O}}\subset M_{f}
be a periodic orbit of period
p
. We say that
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
is hyperbolic if
d_{\hat{x}}\hat{f}^{p}
is a hyperbolic matrix for some
\hat{x}\in\hat{\mathcal{O}}
. By Theorem
2.20
, any
\hat{x}\in\hat{\mathcal{O}}
admits local stable and unstable manifolds
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}),W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})\subset M
such that:

f(W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{f}(\hat{x}))\ \text{and}\ W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})\subset f(W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{f}^{-1}(\hat{x}))).

We can define the global stable and unstable manifolds:

W^{s}(\hat{x})=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}f^{-n}\big(W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{f}^{n}(\hat{x}))\big)\ \text{and}\ W^{u}(\hat{x})=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}f^{n}\big(W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{x}))\big).

We also write:

W^{s}(\hat{\mathcal{O}})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}W^{s}(\hat{f}^{i}(\hat{x}))\ \text{and}\ W^{u}(\hat{\mathcal{O}})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}W^{u}(\hat{f}^{i}(\hat{x}))

for the stable and unstable manifolds of the whole orbit. Note that for any periodic orbit
\mathcal{O}\subset M
, there exists a unique periodic orbit
\hat{\mathcal{O}}\subset M_{f}
that lifts
\mathcal{O}
. This justifies the notation
W^{s/u}(\mathcal{O})
.

As we saw in Section
2
, we can also define stable and unstable manifolds given an ergodic hyperbolic saddle
f
-invariant measure
\mu
. Let
\hat{\mu}:=\pi^{-1}_{\star}\mu
. For
\hat{\mu}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, there also exist local stable and unstable manifolds
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}),W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})\subset M
that are invariant. Recall that
\hat{Y}^{\#}
is contained in the set of
\hat{x}\subset M_{f}
that admits sequences of forward iterates
\hat{f}^{n_{k}}(\hat{x})
and backward iterates
\hat{f}^{-m_{k}}(\hat{x})
in a common Pesin block, both converging to
\hat{x}
. Recall also that
\hat{\mu}(\hat{Y}^{\#})=1
. We can then define global stable and unstable manifolds, as for periodic orbits. Let
\hat{x}\in\hat{Y}^{\#}
and let
\hat{f}^{n_{k}}(\hat{x}),\hat{f}^{-m_{k}}(\hat{x})
be the sequences of forward and backward iterates in a common Pesin block converging to
\hat{x}
. We then define:

W^{s}(\hat{x})=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}f^{-n_{k}}\big(W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{f}^{n_{k}}(\hat{x}))\big)\ \text{and}\ W^{u}(\hat{x})=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}f^{m_{k}}\big(W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{f}^{-m_{k}}(\hat{x}))\big).

We also want to define the analogous of these sets in the natural extension. First, recall that for any
\hat{x}=(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in M_{f}
, since
f
is a local diffeomorphism, we can define its inverse on branches:
f^{-1}_{x_{n}}
is the unique map such that
f^{-1}_{x_{n}}\circ f=\operatorname{id}
in a neighborhood of
x_{n-1}
for
n\leq 0
.

Definition 5.1

(Invariant sets in
M_{f}
.)
.

The local stable set of
\hat{x}\in\hat{Y}^{\#}
is defined by:

V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})=\{\hat{y},\ \pi(\hat{y})\in W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})\}

and its local unstable set is defined by:

V^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})=\{\hat{y}=(y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},\ y_{0}\in W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x}),\ y_{n}=f^{-1}_{x_{n}}(y_{n+1})\ \forall n<0\}=\{\hat{y},\ y_{n}\in W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{y}))\ \forall n<0\}.

As for the classical invariant manifolds, we can then define the global invariant manifolds:

V^{s}(\hat{x})=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\hat{f}^{-n_{k}}\big(V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{f}^{n_{k}}(\hat{x}))\big)\ \text{and}\ V^{u}(\hat{x})=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\hat{f}^{m_{k}}\big(V^{u}_{loc}(\hat{f}^{-m_{k}}(\hat{x}))\big)

where
\hat{f}^{n_{k}}(\hat{x}),\hat{f}^{-m_{k}}(\hat{x})
are sequences of forward and backward iterates in the same Pesin block, both converging to
\hat{x}
. We define it in the same way for hyperbolic periodic orbits.

Recall that if
U,V\subset M
are two submanifolds, we denote their transverse intersection by
U\pitchfork V
. We are now able to define the homoclinic relation.

Definition 5.2

(Homoclinic relation)
.

Let
\hat{\mu}_{1},\hat{\mu}_{2}
be two ergodic hyperbolic saddle
\hat{f}
-invariant measures. We write
\hat{\mu}_{1}\preceq\hat{\mu}_{2}
if there exist
A_{1},A_{2}\subset M_{f}
such that:

–

A_{i}
is
\hat{\mu}_{i}
-measurable and
\hat{\mu}_{i}(A_{i})>0
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

–

For any
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in A_{1}\times A_{2}
, we have
V^{u}(\hat{x})\pitchfork V^{s}(\hat{y})\neq\emptyset
.

We write
\hat{\mu}_{1}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mu}_{2}
if
\hat{\mu}_{1}\preceq\hat{\mu}_{2}
and
\hat{\mu}_{2}\preceq\hat{\mu}_{1}
. In this case, we say that
\hat{\mu}_{1}
and
\hat{\mu}_{2}
are homoclinically related.

If
\hat{\mathcal{O}}\subset M_{f}
is a hyperbolic periodic orbit, we write
\hat{\mu}_{1}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mathcal{O}}
if
\hat{\mu}_{1}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mu}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}
, where
\hat{\mu}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}}
is the Dirac measure on the orbit
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
. This is equivalent to requiring that:

V^{u}(\hat{x})\pitchfork V^{s}(\hat{\mathcal{O}})\neq\emptyset\ \text{and}\ V^{s}(\hat{x})\pitchfork V^{u}(\hat{\mathcal{O}})\neq\emptyset

for
\hat{\mu}_{1}
-almost every
\hat{x}
. We still say that
\hat{\mu}_{1}
is homoclinically related to
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
.

If
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1},\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}\subset M_{f}
are two hyperbolic periodic orbits, we write
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
if
\hat{\mu}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mu}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}}
. This is equivalent to requiring that:

W^{u}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1})\pitchfork W^{s}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2})\neq\emptyset\ \text{and}\ W^{s}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1})\pitchfork W^{u}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}).

In this case, we also say that
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
and
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
are homoclinically related.

Note that by graph transform arguments, which are local, the classical Katok Theorem holds also for hyperbolic measures of saddle type which are invariant by a local diffeomorphism.

Proposition 5.3
.

The homoclinic relation is an equivalence relation on the set of ergodic hyperbolic saddle
\hat{f}
-invariant measures. In particular, if
\hat{\mu}_{1},\hat{\mu}_{2}
are two ergodic hyperbolic saddle
\hat{f}
-invariant measures and
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
is a hyperbolic saddle periodic orbit such that
\hat{\mu}_{1}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mathcal{O}}
and
\hat{\mu}_{2}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mathcal{O}}
, then
\hat{\mu}_{1}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mu}_{2}
. The homoclinic class of a measure
\hat{\mu}
is then defined as the set of all measures homoclinically related to
\hat{\mu}
. Moreover, for any hyperbolic
\hat{f}
-invariant measure
\hat{\mu}
of saddle type, there exists a hyperbolic saddle periodic orbit
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
such that
\hat{\mu}(\{\hat{x}\in M_{f},\ \hat{x}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mathcal{O}}\})=1
.

The proof of this proposition can be found in
[
20
, Proposition 4.3]
. It is very similar to the case of diffeomorphisms, which can be found in
[
5
, Proposition 2.11]
.
The following lemma is a version for endomorphisms of the well-known
\lambda
-lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\hat{y}\in\hat{Y}^{\#}
and let
\Delta\subset M
be a disc of the same dimension as
W^{s}(\hat{y})
. Let also
D\subset W^{s}(\hat{y})
be any disc. If
\Delta
intersects transversely
W^{u}(\hat{y})
, then there exists a sequence of discs
\Delta_{k}\subset\Delta
and times
n_{k}\rightarrow+\infty
such that
f^{-n_{k}}(\Delta_{k})
are discs that converge to
D
for the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
topology.

A similar statement holds for forward iterates
f^{n_{k}}(\Delta_{k})
of discs transverse to
W^{s}(\hat{y})
.

Since this is a local statement, once a point in the natural extension is chosen, the proof proceeds by graph transform in the Pesin charts, and is thus the same as in the case of diffeomorphisms. Complete proofs can be found in
[
20
, Appendix]
in the case of endomorphisms.

5.2.
Measures of maximal entropy in the same homoclinic class

Using coding techniques that go back to Sarig
[
30
]
and Buzzi-Crovisier-Sarig
[
5
]
, Lima, Obata, and Poletti proved in
[
20
, Theorem 4.1]
that each homoclinic class carries at most one measure of maximal entropy. We will use this later to show the finiteness of ergodic measures of maximal entropy by showing the finiteness of homoclinic classes.

Theorem 5.5

(
[
20
]
)
.

Let
\hat{\mu}_{1},\hat{\mu}_{2}
be two ergodic hyperbolic saddle
\hat{f}
-invariant measures of maximal entropy. If
\hat{\mu}_{1}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mu}_{2}
, then
\hat{\mu}_{1}=\hat{\mu}_{2}
.

6.
Entropy, Yomdin theory and dynamical Sard lemma

Throughout this section,
f:M\rightarrow M
is a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
local diffeomorphism, for
r>1
.

6.1.
Generalities on entropy

We recall some well-known facts about measured and topological entropy. In this paragraph,
g
will be either
f
or
\hat{f}
, or some iterate of these maps, and
X
will be either
M
or
M_{f}
. Given an integer
n
and
\epsilon>0
, the
(g,n,\epsilon)
-Bowen ball at
x\in X
, or
(n,\epsilon)
-Bowen ball when the choice of
g
is unambiguous, is the following set:

B_{g}(x,n,\epsilon)=\{y\in X,\ d\big(g^{i}(x),g^{i}(y)\big)<\epsilon,\ \forall\ 0\leq i\leq n-1\}.

Consider
Y\subset X
. A set
F\subset X
is called
(g,n,\epsilon)
-spanning if:

Y\subset\bigcup_{x\in F}B_{g}(x,n,\epsilon).

Define
r_{g}(n,\epsilon,Y)
to be the minimal cardinality of a
(g,n,\epsilon)
-spanning set of
Y
. When
Y=X
, we just write
r_{g}(n,\epsilon)
.

Topological entropy of a subset.
The topological entropy of
g
on a set
Y\subset X
is defined by:

(6.1)

h_{top}(g,Y)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}h_{top}(g,Y,\epsilon)\ \text{where}\ h_{top}(g,Y,\epsilon)=\limsup_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log r_{g}(n,\epsilon,Y).

Measured entropy and Katok formula.
Let
\nu
be an ergodic
g
-invariant measure. For
0<\lambda<1
, an integer
n
and
\epsilon>0
, let:

r_{g}(n,\epsilon,\nu,\lambda)=\inf\{r_{g}(n,\epsilon,Y),\ Y\subset X\ \text{measurable s.t.}\ \nu(Y)>\lambda\}.

For any
0<\lambda<1
, the measured entropy of
\nu
satisfies:

(6.2)

h(g,\nu)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}h(g,\nu,\epsilon)\ \text{where}\ h(g,\nu,\epsilon)=\limsup_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log r_{g}(n,\epsilon,\nu,\lambda).

In the case where
\nu
is not ergodic, we proceed as follows. Let
\nu:=\int_{M}\nu_{x}\ d\nu(x)
be an ergodic decomposition of
\nu
. We define
h(g,\nu)=\int_{M}h(g,\nu_{x})\ d\nu(x)
.

Tail entropy.

The tail entropy of
g
is defined by:

(6.3)

h^{\star}(f)=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}h^{\star}(g,\epsilon)\ \text{where}\ h^{\star}(g,\epsilon)=\sup_{x\in X}h_{top}(g,\{y\in X,\ \forall n\geq 0\ d\big(f^{n}(x),f^{n}(y)\big)<\epsilon\}).

The relevance of this concept lies in the following estimate. Let
\nu
be an ergodic
g
-invariant measure. We have:

(6.4)

h(g,\nu)\leq h(g,\nu,\epsilon)+h^{\star}(f,\epsilon).

Secondly, if
(\nu_{n})_{n}
is a sequence of ergodic
g
-invariant measures weak-
\star
converging to
\nu
, we have the following upper semicontinuity property:

(6.5)

\limsup_{n\rightarrow+\infty}h(g,\nu_{n})\leq h(g,\nu)+h^{\star}(g).

Recall that
\lambda(g):=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert dg^{n}\rVert
. The following control on the tail entropy holds:

(6.6)

h^{\star}(g)\leq\frac{\lambda(g)}{r}.

In the special case where
g\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, we then have
h^{\star}(g)=0
.

6.2.
Unstable entropy

Let
\mu
be an ergodic hyperbolic saddle
f
-invariant measure. Let
\hat{\mu}:=\pi^{-1}_{\star}\mu
. We recall that this means that:

\lambda^{s}(f,\mu)<0<\lambda^{u}(f,\mu)

where
\lambda^{s/u}(f,\mu)
are the two Lyapunov exponents of the measure
\mu
. The Pesin unstable manifold theorem (Theorem
2.20
) asserts that
\hat{\mu}
-almost every point
\hat{x}
belongs to an unstable set
V^{u}(\hat{x})
(see Section
5
for a definition). This set is contained in the connected component of
M_{f}
containing
\hat{x}
and is an injectively immersed
\mathcal{C}^{r}
curve characterized by:

V^{u}(\hat{x})=\{\hat{y}\in M_{f},\ \limsup_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\log d\big(\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{x}),\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{y})\big)<0\}.

A measurable partition
\xi
is subordinated to the unstable lamination
V^{u}
if for
\hat{\mu}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, the atom
\xi(\hat{x})
is a neighborhood of
\hat{x}
inside
V^{u}(\hat{x})
and
\xi
is increasing: every atom of
\hat{f}(\xi)
is a union of atoms of
\xi
. Qian and Zhu proved the existence of such partitions for endomorphisms in
[
29
, Proposition 3.2]
. Their work was inspired by the famous Ledrappier-Young theory
[
17
,
18
]
.

Since
\xi
is measurable, Rokhlin’s disintegration theorem applies and then, for
\hat{\mu}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, there exists a probability measure
\hat{\mu}_{\hat{x}}^{u}
supported on
\xi(\hat{x})
such that:

(6.7)

\hat{\mu}=\int\hat{\mu}_{\hat{x}}^{u}\ d\hat{\mu}(\hat{x}).

Such a family
\{\hat{\mu}_{\hat{x}}^{u}\}_{\hat{x}}
is not unique but, given
\xi
, any two families displaying these properties are equal outside a set of
\hat{x}
of
\hat{\mu}
-measure zero. We then call
\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}}
the conditional measure on
\xi(\hat{x})
.

One of the main consequences of the Ledrappier-Young theory is that the entropy along these unstable conditional measures is equal to the entropy of the measure. In the case of endomorphisms, this was proved by Qian, Xie, and Zhu in
[
28
, Proposition IX 2.14]
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\hat{\mu}
be an ergodic hyperbolic saddle
\hat{f}
-invariant measure. Let
\{\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}}\}_{\hat{x}}
be a system of conditional measures on local unstable sets. Then, for
\hat{\mu}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, we have:

h(\hat{f},\hat{\mu})=\inf_{\lambda>0}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\liminf_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log r_{\hat{f}}(n,\epsilon,\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}},\lambda).

Note that the definition of
r_{\hat{f}}
, mentioned in Theorem
6.1
, extend to non-invariant measures.

The next lemma states that we can also compute the unstable entropy in the manifold, by looking at the projection of
\hat{\mu}_{\hat{x}}^{u}
which is supported on the local unstable manifold of
\hat{x}
. It is an easy corollary once we know that
\pi:V^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})\rightarrow W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})
is a true conjugacy between
\hat{f}
and
f
. This last statement is straightforward from the definition of the local unstable sets
5.1
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\hat{\mu}
be an ergodic hyperbolic saddle
\hat{f}
-invariant measure. Let
\{\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}}\}_{\hat{x}}
be a system of conditional measures on local unstable sets. For any
\hat{x}
, define
\mu_{\hat{x}}^{u}:=\pi_{\star}\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}}
. For
\hat{\mu}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, we then have:

h(\hat{f},\hat{\mu})=\inf_{\lambda>0}\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\liminf_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log r_{f}(n,\epsilon,\mu^{u}_{\hat{x}},\lambda).

Recall also the following version of the Brin-Katok theorem, proved in
[
18
]
in the case of diffeomorphisms and adapted in
[
28
]
for endomorphisms.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\hat{\mu}
be an ergodic hyperbolic saddle
\hat{f}
-invariant measure. Let
\{\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}}\}_{\hat{x}}
be a system of conditional measures on local unstable sets. For any
\hat{x}
, define
\mu_{\hat{x}}^{u}:=\pi_{\star}\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}}
. Then, for
\hat{\mu}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, we have:

h(\hat{f},\hat{\mu})=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\liminf_{n\rightarrow+\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\log\mu^{u}_{\hat{x}}\big(B_{f}(\pi(\hat{x}),n,\epsilon)\big).

6.3.
Yomdin theory and reparametrizations

Let
r>1
and
\epsilon>0
. Recall the definition of an
(r,\epsilon)
-curve from Definition
4.1
. Suppose that
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M
is a curve. If we cut
[0,1]
into small intervals
[a_{i},b_{i}]
and reparametrize
\gamma
by
\gamma\circ\psi_{i}
where
\psi_{i}:[a_{i},b_{i}]\rightarrow[0,1]
is affine, then
\lVert\gamma\circ\psi_{i}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{r}}<\kappa\lVert\gamma\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{r}}
where
\kappa=|a_{i}-b_{i}|
. Thus, by cutting into sufficiently small pieces, we can obtain a covering by pieces with affine reparametrizations of
\mathcal{C}^{r}
size as small as we want. Yomdin measured the complexity of a curve by counting how many reparametrized pieces with
\mathcal{C}^{r}
size less than
\epsilon
are needed to cover it.

Definition 6.4

(Reparametrization)
.

Let
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M
be a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
curve. A reparametrization of
\gamma
is a non-constant affine map
\psi:[0,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
.

A family of reparametrizations of
\gamma
over a set
T\subset[0,1]
is a collection
\mathscr{R}
of reparametrizations such that:

T\subset\bigcup_{\psi\in\mathscr{R}}\psi([0,1]).

Definition 6.5

(Admissible reparametrizations)
.

Let
\epsilon>0
,
N\geq 1
and
T\subset[0,1]
. Let
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M
be a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
curve. A reparametrization
\psi
of
\gamma
is
(f,r,N,\epsilon)
-admissible up to time
n
if there exists an increasing sequence of integers
(n_{0},n_{1},...,n_{l})
such that:

–

n_{0}=0
,
n_{l}=n
and
n_{j}-n_{j-1}\leq N
for
j\in\{1,...,l\}
,

–

the curve
f^{n_{j}}\circ\gamma\circ\psi
is an
(r,\epsilon)
-curve for each
j\in\{0,...,l\}
.

We call the integers
n_{j}
the admissible times.

A family of reparametrizations
\mathscr{R}
of
\gamma
over
T
is
(f,r,N,\epsilon)
-admissible up to time
n
if each
\psi\in\mathscr{R}
is
(f,r,N,\epsilon)
-admissible up to time
n
.

Let us now cite Yomdin’s theorem. It asserts that covers by Bowen balls generate reparametrizations with cardinality of the same order of magnitude.

Theorem 6.6

(Yomdin)
.

Given a real number
2\leq r<\infty
, there exist
\Upsilon:=\Upsilon(r)
and
\epsilon_{\Upsilon}:=\epsilon_{\Upsilon}(r)
with the following properties. For any:

–

regular
(r,\epsilon)
-curve
\gamma
with
0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{\Upsilon}
,

–

x\in\gamma([0,1])
and
T=\{t\in[0,1],\ f\circ\gamma(t)\in B(f(x),\epsilon)\}
,

there exists a family of reparametrizations
\mathscr{R}
of
\gamma
over
T
such that:

(1)

for any
\psi\in\mathscr{R}
,
f\circ\gamma\circ\psi
is a
(r,\epsilon)
-curve.

(2)

|\mathscr{R}|\leq\Upsilon\lVert df\rVert^{1/r}
.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
2\leq r<\infty
and let
\Upsilon:=\Upsilon(r)
and
\epsilon_{\Upsilon}:=\epsilon_{\Upsilon}(r)
be the constants given by Theorem
6.6
. Suppose that:

–

\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M
is a regular
(r,\epsilon)
-curve with
0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{\Upsilon}
,

–

N,n\geq 1
and
T\subset[0,1]
.

Then there exists a family
\mathscr{R}_{n}
of reparametrizations over the set
T
which is
(f,r,N,\epsilon)
-admissible up to time
n
such that:

|\mathscr{R}_{n}|\leq\Upsilon^{\lceil n/N\rceil}\lVert df\rVert^{n/r}\lVert df\rVert^{N/r}r_{f}\big(n,\epsilon,f\circ\gamma(T)\big).

Yomdin theory combined with the characterization of entropy along unstable manifolds, Lemma
6.2
, allows us to compute the entropy in terms of reparametrizations. Again, for a more precise proof in the case of diffeomorphisms, see
[
4
, Corollary 4.16]
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\mu
be an ergodic
f
-invariant hyperbolic saddle measure and let
\hat{\mu}:=\pi_{\star}^{-1}\mu
. Let
\{\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}}\}
be any system of conditional measures along unstable manifolds and let
\mu^{u}_{\hat{x}}=\pi_{\star}\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}}
. The following holds for any
\hat{X}\subset M_{f}
of positive
\hat{\mu}
-measure, for
\hat{\mu}
-almost every
\hat{x}\in\hat{X}
and for any choice of:

–

\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})
a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
curve with
r>2
,

–

T\subset[0,1]
such that
\mu_{\hat{x}}^{u}\big(\gamma(T)\big)>0
.

If
(\mathscr{R}_{n})
is a family of reparametrizations of
\gamma
which are
(f,N,\epsilon)
-admissible up to time
n
over the set
T
for some
\epsilon>0
and
N\geq 1
independent of
n
, then:

h(f,\mu,\epsilon)\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\#\mathscr{R}_{n}.

6.4.
Dynamical laminations and Sard lemma

6.4.1.
Generalities about laminations

Laminations.
Fix some
r>1
. Let us first define what we mean by a continuous lamination with
\mathcal{C}^{r}
leaves. Let
K\subset M
. A
\mathcal{C}^{r}
lamination
\mathscr{L}
of
K
is an atlas of coordinates such that for any
x\in K
, there exists
U\cap K
a neighborhood of
x
in
M
homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}\times T
, where
T
is some given topological space. The image of any
\mathbb{R}\times\{t\}
is an embedded
\mathcal{C}^{r}
curve which is called a plaque of the lamination. The neighborhood
U
is called a lamination neighborhood of
x
. We also require that the composition of two intersecting change of coordinates is
\mathcal{C}^{r}
and that it preserves the plaques. The set of the maximal union of intersecting plaques forms a partition of
K
such that the atoms are injectively immersed curves. For
x\in K
, we denote by
\mathscr{L}(x)
the atom of the lamination containing
x
and we call each of these atoms a leaf of the lamination.

Transversals.
A transversal to a continuous lamination with
\mathcal{C}^{r}
leaves
\mathscr{L}
of a set
K
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
embedded one-dimensional submanifold
\tau\subset M
that intersects
K
such that for every
x\in\tau\cap K
and every plaque
L
through
x
,
\tau
is transverse to
L
. Denote by
\text{dim}_{H}
the Hausdorff dimension.

Definition 6.9

(Transverse dimension of a lamination)
.

The transverse dimension of a continuous lamination
\mathscr{L}
of a set
K
is defined by:

\text{\dj}(\mathscr{L})=\sup\{\text{dim}_{H}(\tau\cap K),\ \tau\ \text{is a transversal to}\ \mathscr{L}\}.

Holonomies.
Consider a continuous lamination
\mathscr{L}
of a set
K
. Consider a point
x_{0}
, a lamination neighborhood
U_{0}
of
x_{0}
and a transversal
\tau_{0}
containing
x_{0}
. Up to reducing
\tau_{0}
, the plaque
L_{y}
at every
y
sufficiently close to
x_{0}
intersects
\tau_{0}
in exactly one point. There exists then an open set
V
containing
x_{0}
such that for any transversal
\tau
sufficiently uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{1}
close to
\tau_{0}
, the map
H_{\tau}:V\cap K\rightarrow\tau
is well-defined as the unique intersection between a plaque through
y
and
\tau
.

We say that
\mathscr{L}
has Lipschitz holonomies if, given
x_{0},U_{0}
and
\tau_{0}
, there exists a constant
L>0
, a uniformly
\mathcal{C}^{1}
neighborhood
\mathcal{T}
of
\tau_{0}
and a neighborhood
V
of
x_{0}
with the following property. For any transversals
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
, the holonomy projection map
H_{\tau_{1}\rightarrow\tau_{2}}:V\cap K\cap\tau_{1}\rightarrow\tau_{2}
has Lipschitz constant less than or equal to
L
. Here
H_{\tau_{1}\rightarrow\tau_{2}}:V\cap K\cap\tau_{1}\rightarrow\tau_{2}
denotes the restriction of
H_{\tau_{2}}
to
\tau_{1}
.

6.4.2.
Dynamical laminations

The following theorem gives a bound on the transverse dimension of the stable lamination of an ergodic hyperbolic saddle measure, which depends on the entropy and its positive exponent. For an ergodic hyperbolic
f
-invariant measure
\mu
, recall that
\lambda^{u}(\mu)
denotes its largest Lyapunov exponent, which is positive.

Theorem 6.10
.

Let
\mu
be an ergodic, hyperbolic saddle
f
-invariant measure. Let
\hat{\mu}:=\pi^{-1}_{\star}\mu
. The following holds for
\hat{\mu}
-almost every
\hat{x}
. Fix any
\delta>0
. There exists a measurable set
\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}:=\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}(\delta)
displaying following properties.

(1)

W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}):=\bigcup_{\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}}W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y})
defines a continuous lamination with
\mathcal{C}^{r}
leaves which has Lipschitz holonomies.

(2)

The following dimension estimate holds:

\text{dim}_{H}\big(W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})\cap\Lambda_{\hat{x}}\big)\geq h(f,\mu)/\lambda^{u}(\mu)-\delta

where
\Lambda_{\hat{x}}:=\pi(\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}})
.

(3)

In the case where
\text{dim}_{H}\big(W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})\cap\Lambda_{\hat{x}}\big)>0
, the point
\pi(\hat{x})
is accumulated by points of
\Lambda_{\hat{x}}
on both sides of
W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})
belonging to the support of
\hat{\mu}
restricted to some common Pesin block.

We emphasize that
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
and
W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})
are the
\mathcal{C}^{r}
embedded curves defined by Definition
2.21
. This theorem is a combination of
[
34
]
and
[
5
, Theorem 4.1]
. Let us give the main lines of the proof for the sake of completeness.

Proof.

Recall the definition of the local invariant sets from Definition
5.1
. Recall also the definition of the conditional measures (
6.7
) and let
\{\hat{\mu}_{\hat{x}}^{u}\}_{\hat{x}}
be a system of conditional measures subordinate to the unstable lamination of
\hat{\mu}
. Let us choose
\hat{x}
such that Oseledets’ theorem and Proposition
6.3
are satisfied. Note that this is a set of full
\hat{\mu}
-measure. Fix
\delta>0
. We are first going to construct a set
\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}\subset M_{f}
of positive
\hat{\mu}_{\hat{x}}^{u}
-measure, contained in a Pesin block, such that for any
\delta,\epsilon>0
small enough and any
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}
we have:

\liminf_{\rho\rightarrow 0}\frac{\log\mu^{u}_{\hat{x}}\big(B(y,\rho)\big)}{\log\rho}\geq h(f,\mu)/\lambda^{u}(\mu)-\delta

where
y:=\pi(\hat{y})
and
\mu_{\hat{x}}:=\pi_{\star}\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}}
. This follows the same lines as
[
34
, Section 3]
.

Choose
\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}
such that for any
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}
it satisfies the following properties.

–

There exists a positive constant
\rho_{0}
such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}-\frac{1}{n}\log\mu^{u}_{\hat{x}}\Big(B_{f}(y,n,\rho_{0})\Big)\geq h(f,\mu)-\delta_{1}
, for
\delta_{1}>0
small and where
y:=\pi(\hat{y})
.

–

\hat{y}
is in the support of
\hat{\mu}
restricted to some fixed Pesin block of positive
\hat{\mu}
-measure.

–

The map
\hat{y}\mapsto W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y})
is continuous for the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
topology.

The first item is possible using Proposition
6.3
and taking
\hat{y}
in a set of positive
\hat{\mu}_{\hat{x}}^{u}
-measure. The second item is possible by restricting to a Pesin block of positive
\hat{\mu}^{u}_{\hat{x}}
-measure. Now using the classical (and more precise, see
[
16
, Chapter S]
) version of Theorem
2.7
, it is easy to see that for any
\delta_{2}>0
small, there exists
\chi>\lambda^{u}(\mu)-\delta_{2}
such that for all integer
n\geq 0
and for all
\hat{y}\in\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}
we have:

B(y,C(\hat{y})\rho_{0}e^{-n\chi})\subset B_{f}(y,n,\rho_{0})

for some constant
C(\hat{y})
. In other words, for any
\rho<<\rho_{0}
, there exists an integer
n(\rho)
such that:

–

B(y,\rho)\subset B_{f}(y,n(\rho),\rho_{0})
,

–

\lim_{\rho\rightarrow 0}\frac{\log\rho}{n(\rho)}\geq-\chi
.

We can now compute the following:

\displaystyle\frac{\log\mu^{u}_{\hat{x}}\big(B(y,\rho)\big)}{\log\rho}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\log\mu^{u}_{\hat{x}}\big(B_{f}(y,n(\rho),\rho_{0})\big)}{\log\rho}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{n(\rho)}\log\mu^{u}_{\hat{x}}\big(B_{f}(y,n(\rho),\rho_{0})\big)\times-\frac{n(\rho)}{\log\rho}

\displaystyle\geq\big(h(f,\mu)-\delta_{1}\big)/\big(\lambda^{u}(\mu)-\delta_{2}\big).

Taking the
\liminf
as
\rho\rightarrow 0
and taking
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
small enough gives us the desired result. Now, using
[
34
, Proposition 2.1]
, which is a purely topological result, we get that:

\text{dim}_{H}\big(W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})\cap\Lambda_{\hat{x}}\big)\geq\frac{h(f,\mu)}{\lambda^{u}(\mu)}-\delta.

By construction, we can suppose that
\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}}
is contained in a Pesin block. Moreover, since
f
is
\mathcal{C}^{r}
, the same proof as in
[
5
, Appendix A]
shows that
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\hat{x}})
defines a continuous lamination with
\mathcal{C}^{r}
leaves which have Lipschitz holonomies (their proof works for diffeomorphisms but is local, so the same exact arguments apply in our case). For the last statement, note that the points in
W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})
that are not accumulated on both sides of
W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})
constitute a set of
\hat{\mu}_{\hat{x}}^{u}
-measure zero. This proves the last statement and thus concludes the proof.
∎

6.4.3.
Dynamical Sard lemma

Fix a real number
r>1
and let
\mathscr{L}
be a continuous lamination with
\mathcal{C}^{r}
leaves on a set
K
. Let
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M
be a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
curve. Define the following lamination:

\mathscr{N}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{L}):=\big\{\mathscr{L}(\gamma(t)),\ t\in[0,1],\ \gamma(t)\cap L\neq\emptyset\ \text{for some leaf}\ L\ \text{and}\ \gamma^{\prime}(t)\in T_{\gamma(t)}L\big\}.

This is the lamination made with all leaves such that there exists at least one tangent intersection with
\gamma
. The following theorem asserts that the higher the smoothness of the leaves of
\mathcal{L}
, the lower the transverse dimension of the lamination
\mathscr{N}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{L})
. This is a topological statement which is proved in
[
5
, Theorem 4.2]
.

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
r>1
and let
\mathscr{L}
be a continuous lamination with
\mathcal{C}^{r}
leaves. For any
\mathcal{C}^{r}
curve
\gamma
, we have:

\text{\dj}\big(\mathscr{N}_{\gamma}(\mathscr{L})\big)\leq\frac{1}{r}.

7.
Unstable with stable intersections for saddle ergodic measures

The goal of this section is to prove the following propositions. Proposition
7.2
is a corollary of Proposition
7.1
. Recall that
\lambda(f)=\lim_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\lVert df^{n}\rVert
. Recall the definition of the set
\hat{Y}^{\#}
before Proposition
3.11
. Recall that
\hat{Y}^{\#}
is contained in the set of points satisfying the conclusion of Theorem
6.10
. Recall that such an
\hat{x}
is accumulated by points on both sides of
W^{u}(\hat{x})
and the local stable manifolds of these points form a continuous lamination with
\mathcal{C}^{r}
leaves having Lipschitz holonomies.

7.1.
Statement of the main Propositions

Proposition 7.1

(Unstable Intersections)
.

Let
f:M\rightarrow M
be a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
,
r>1
, local diffeomorphism. Let
(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of ergodic hyperbolic
f
-invariant measures of saddle type such that:

\mu_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}\nu

for the weak-
\star
topology. Suppose that the following holds.

–

There exists
\alpha\in(0,1]
and two
f
-invariant measures
\nu_{1},\nu_{0}
, possibly non-ergodic, such that
\nu=\alpha\nu_{1}+(1-\alpha)\nu_{0}
.

–

\nu
-almost every ergodic component of
\nu_{1}
is hyperbolic of saddle type and has positive entropy.

–

If
\alpha<1
, we also suppose that
\nu
-almost every ergodic component
\nu_{0}^{\prime}
of
\nu_{0}
satisfies:

h(f,\nu_{0}^{\prime})<\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\big(\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}h(f,\mu_{k})-2\lambda(f)/r\big).

Then, there exists a finite number of points
\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{L}\in\hat{Y}^{\#}
displaying the following properties. For
k
sufficiently large, there exists a measurable set
\hat{X}_{k}\subset M_{f}
and an integer
i(k)\in\{1,\dots,L\}
such that the following holds.

(UI1)

\hat{\mu}_{k}(\hat{X}_{k})>0
, where
\hat{\mu}_{k}:=\pi_{\star}^{-1}\mu_{k}
.

(UI2)

For any
\hat{x}\in\hat{X}_{k}
, the following intersection holds:

W^{u}(\hat{x})\cap W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i(k)})\neq\emptyset.

Note that in the case where
f
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, Proposition
7.1
can be applied whenever
\nu
-almost every ergodic component
\nu_{0}^{\prime}
of
\nu_{0}
satisfies
h(f,\nu_{0}^{\prime})<\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}h(f,\mu_{k})
. We emphasize that if
\alpha=1
, we have
\nu=\nu_{1}
so the hypothesis on the entropy of the ergodic components of
\nu_{0}
will always be satisfied. We also emphasize that the entropy hypothesis implies that
\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}h(f,\mu_{k})-2\lambda(f)/r>0
. Finally, note that the integer
k
and the points
\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{L}
depend on the sequence
(\mu_{k})_{k}
and on the decomposition of the measure
\nu
.

The following proposition is a corollary of Proposition
7.1
. Its principal application holds for sequences of measures such that their entropy converges to the topological entropy, and in particular for ergodic m.m.e.’s.

Proposition 7.2

(Homoclinic Unstable Intersections)
.

Let
f:M\rightarrow M
be a
\mathcal{C}^{r}
,
r>1
, local diffeomorphism. Let
(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of ergodic hyperbolic saddle
f
-invariant measures such that:

\mu_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}\nu

for the weak-
\star
topology. Fix
\beta\in(0,1)
. Suppose that the following holds.

–

There exists
\alpha\in(0,1]
and two different
f
-invariant measures
\nu_{1},\nu_{0}
, possibly non-ergodic, such that
\nu=\alpha\nu_{1}+(1-\alpha)\nu_{0}
.

–

\nu
-almost every ergodic component of
\nu_{1}
is hyperbolic of saddle type and has entropy larger than
\lambda(f)/r
. We denote by
\hat{\nu}_{1}:=\pi_{\star}^{-1}\nu_{1}
.

–

For
\nu
-almost every ergodic component
\nu^{\prime}
of
\nu
, we have:

h(f,\nu^{\prime})<\frac{1}{1-\alpha\beta}\big(\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}h(f,\mu_{k})-2\frac{\lambda(f)}{r}\big).

Then for any hyperbolic periodic saddle orbit
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
satisfying
\hat{\nu}_{1}(\{\hat{x}\in M_{f},\ \hat{x}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mathcal{O}}\})>\beta
, the following property holds for
k
sufficiently large. For
\hat{\mu}_{k}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, the following intersection holds:

W^{u}(\hat{x})\pitchfork W^{s}(\hat{\mathcal{O}})\neq\emptyset.

Note that in the case where
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, Proposition
7.2
can be applied whenever
\nu
-almost every ergodic component
\nu^{\prime}
of
\nu
satisfies
h(f,\nu^{\prime})<\frac{1}{1-\alpha\beta}\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}h(f,\mu_{k})
. We emphasize that the hypothesis on the entropy of the ergodic components of
\nu
implies that
\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}h(f,\mu_{k})-2\lambda(f)/r>0
. Finally, note that the integer
k
depends on the sequence of measures
(\mu_{k})_{k}
, on the number
\beta
and on the periodic orbit
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
.

The rest of the section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
7.1
and Proposition
7.2
.

7.2.
Proof of Proposition
7.1

Before going into the details, let us explain first the ideas of the proof of Proposition
7.1
. The main point is to analyze the entropic behavior of the typical orbits of the measures
\mu_{k}
. In Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
, we settle the entropy and reparametrization parameters. In Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
, we use Proposition
3.11
to build the two families
\mathcal{R}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
of
us
-rectangles covering the measure
\nu_{1}
, and the neighborhood
\hat{U}_{1}
of points having a good Pesin-behavior (see Section
3
), which has very large
\nu_{1}
-measure. This measure has to be seen as the saddle high-entropy part of
\nu
. Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
deals with
\nu_{0}
. It has to be seen as a small entropy part of the measure
\nu
, which can contain in its ergodic decomposition non-hyperbolic measures, zero entropy measures, expanding type measures etc. We construct
\hat{U}_{0}
, a neighborhood of its support, of large
\nu_{0}
-measure, which can be covered by a small number of Bowen-balls.

From Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
to Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
, we introduce the decomposition type of typical
\mu_{k}
-orbits. The idea is that for
k
large enough, typical orbits are, provided they are long enough, made by three types of segments.

–

Long segments starting in
\hat{U}_{1}
, which do not intersect
\hat{U}_{0}
: the
rectangle segments
. These segments have to be the ones where entropy is created.

–

Long segments starting in
\hat{U}_{0}
, which do not intersect
\hat{U}_{1}
: the
small entropy segments
. The orbit separation along these segments is small compared with the entropy.

–

Segments of any size which do not intersect
\hat{U}_{1}
nor
\hat{U}_{0}
: the
wild segments
. These segments can see entropy creation, but their total length will be very small.

Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
is the core of the proof. We compute the entropy of the
\mu_{k}
by building families of admissible reparametrizations of the unstable local manifold of typical
\mu_{k}
-points. We suppose that the iterates of these curves do not cross the stable manifolds containing the stable boundaries of the
\nu_{1}
-rectangles. We build the reparametrizations by induction, depending on the class of the segment of the typical
\mu_{k}
-orbits we consider. Our goal will be to control the number of reparametrizations to show that the non-crossing hypothesis between
\mu_{k}
-unstable and
\nu_{1}
-stable manifolds prevents entropy creation. Let us explain our strategy in each class of segment.

Small entropy segments.
These segments are made of iterates staying close to the measure
\nu_{0}
and have then a cover by a small number of Bowen-balls. We use Yomdin theorem to ensure that the number of reparametrizations is small inside each Bowen-ball.

Wild segments.
We control the number of reparametrizations by the norm of the derivative. It can be big, but since these segments are very short in total length, they do not allow entropy creation.

Rectangle segments.
Here, the control is more delicate. Let us develop the strategy, which is the core of the present work. At the beginning of a rectangle segment, any reparametrization of the unstable manifold we study intersects the
\nu_{1}
-rectangles. Pick one of these reparametrizations and let us denote it by
\gamma
. We apply Proposition
4.3
to the curve
\gamma
, and treat separately the crossing and folding
\gamma
-intervals.

Rectangle segments: case of a crossing interval.
We treat each crossing
\gamma
-interval apart. Proposition
4.3
bounds the number of such intervals. Since their image by
\gamma
is connected, we use then Property
(MR5)
and the fact that iterates of
\gamma
do not cross the stable boundaries of the rectangles to trap the iterates of the unstable manifolds inside the rectangles. Indeed, during a rectangle segment, the iterates of any crossing interval is contained in a rectangle of the family
\tilde{R}
. See figure
9
.

Figure 9
.
The proof in the case of a crossing
\gamma
-interval. The horizontal direction represents the stable boundaries of the rectangles.

Rectangle segments: case of folding intervals.
Unlike in the case of crossing
\gamma
-intervals, we do not have a bound on the number of such intervals. Nevertheless, Proposition
4.3
gives a uniform bound on the number of rectangles needed to contain all the images of the folding
\gamma
-intervals. We treat these rectangles separately. Pick such a rectangle
R_{i}
. The set of folding
\gamma
-intervals whose images are contained in
R_{i}
is not connected, so we cannot directly apply the same argument as for crossing intervals. Nevertheless, the curve
\gamma
is connected and is small, combining this with the fact that no iterate of
\gamma
can cross the stable manifolds containing the stable boundaries of the rectangles of the family
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
, Property
(MR5)
still implies that the image by
f
of all the folding
\gamma
-intervals contained in
R_{i}
are still contained in a rectangle
\tilde{R}_{j}
. To apply this argument, we need to control the size of the iterates
f^{n}(\gamma)
. For this reason, we apply Yomdin Theorem every
N_{\star}
iterates for some
N
fixed and well-chosen. Then, we are able to repeat this argument starting with a new reparametrization, which will be contained in some rectangle
\tilde{R}_{l}
.

We conclude in Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
and Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
by giving a bound on the number of reparametrizations to conclude that the iterates of the unstable manifolds of typical
\mu_{k}
-points have to cross the stable manifolds containing the stable boundaries of the
\nu_{1}
-rectangles. See Figure
10
.

Figure 10
.
The proof in the case of a rectangle
R_{i}
containing folding
\gamma
-intervals. The horizontal direction represents the stable boundaries of the rectangles. Note that the set of folding
\gamma
-intervals contained in
\tilde{R}_{i}
is disconnected, whereas
\gamma
is connected. The curve gets bigger as we iterate and we have to reparametrize to continue to apply the argument, using the fact that iterates of
\gamma
do not cross the stable local manifolds containing the stable boundaries of the rectangles.

Step U.I.0.
Let
(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of ergodic hyperbolic
f
-invariant measures of saddle type. Write
\hat{\mu}_{k}:=\pi_{\star}^{-1}\mu_{k}
. We suppose that we have the following convergence:

\mu_{k}\underset{k\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}\nu

in the weak-
\star
topology for some
f
-invariant measure
\nu
, which may not be ergodic. Again, denote by
\hat{\nu}:=\pi_{\star}^{-1}\nu
. By our hypothesis, there exist two
\hat{f}
-invariant probability measures
\hat{\nu}_{0},\hat{\nu}_{1}
and a real number
\alpha\in(0,1]
such that:

\hat{\nu}=\alpha\hat{\nu}_{1}+(1-\alpha)\hat{\nu}_{0}.

Write the ergodic decomposition of
\hat{\nu}_{0}
as
\hat{\nu}_{0}=\int\hat{\nu}_{0,\hat{x}}\ d\hat{\nu}_{0}(\hat{x})
. Take a positive constant
h>0
such that:

(7.1)

\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\big(\liminf_{k\rightarrow+\infty}h(f,\mu_{k})-2\lambda(f)/r\big)>h>\text{ess-sup}_{\hat{x}}h(f,\hat{\nu}_{0,\hat{x}}).

The two measures
\hat{\nu}_{0}
and
\hat{\nu}_{1}
satisfy the following. For
\hat{\nu}
-almost every ergodic component
\hat{\nu}_{0}^{\prime}
of
\hat{\nu}_{0}
, we have
h(\hat{f},\hat{\nu}_{0}^{\prime})\leq h
. We also have that
\hat{\nu}
-almost every ergodic component of
\hat{\nu}_{1}
is hyperbolic of saddle type with entropy larger than
\lambda(f)/r
. Note that we can then apply Proposition
3.11
to the measure
\nu_{1}
.

Step U.I. 1.

Choice of the entropy and reparametrization parameters.

Let us first choose the parameters of entropy. Pick first a constant
\kappa>0
such that:

(7.2)
