, we define the following three classes of a
\hat{f}^{-1}
-orbit segment
(\hat{f}^{-s}(\hat{x}),\dots,\hat{f}^{-(t+s-1)}(\hat{x}))
of a
\hat{f}^{-1}
-orbit
(\hat{x},\dots,\hat{f}^{-(n-1)}(\hat{x}))
.

–

Rectangle segment:

\hat{f}^{-s}\in\hat{U}_{1}
and
t=n_{1}
.

–

Small entropy segment:

\hat{f}^{-s}\in\hat{U}_{0}
and
t=n_{0}
.

–

Wild segment:
one of the following situations occurs.

(a)

\hat{f}^{-(s+j)}(\hat{x})\notin\hat{U}_{0}\cup\hat{U}_{1}
for
j=0,\dots,t-1
and
\hat{f}^{-(t+s)}(\hat{x})\in\hat{U}_{0}\cup\hat{U}_{1}
.

(b)

\hat{f}^{-s}(\hat{x})\in\hat{U}_{0}
and
s+n_{0}>n-1
, in which case
t+s=n
.

(c)

\hat{f}^{-s}(\hat{x})\in\hat{U}_{1}
and
s+n_{1}>n-1
, in which case
t+s=n
.

Claim
7.5
gives the following claim.

Claim 8.6
.

There exists an integer
k_{\star}
such that for any
k\geq k_{\star}
and for
\hat{\mu}_{k}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, there exists an integer
n_{k}(\hat{x})
displaying the following property. For any
n\geq n_{k}(\hat{x})
, any orbit
(\hat{x},\dots,\hat{f}^{-(n-1)}(\hat{x}))
can be decomposed as follows. There exists an integer
l
and a string:

\theta^{\prime}(\hat{x},n):=\big((t_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(t_{l},b_{l})\big)\in(\mathbb{Z}\times\{1,2,3\})^{l+1}

such that
t_{0}=-1
,
t_{l}=n-1
,
b_{l}=1
and for any
j\in\{0,\dots,l-1\}
the orbit segment
(\hat{f}^{-(t_{j}+1)}(\hat{x}),\dots,\hat{f}^{-t_{j+1}})
is: a rectangle segment if
b_{j}=1
, a small entropy segment if
b_{j}=2
, and a wild segment if
b_{j}=3
. Moreover, we have the following estimates:

(a)

rectangle segments have total length at most
\alpha n+2\eta n
,

(b)

small entropy segments have total length at most
(1-\alpha)n+2\eta n
,

(c)

wild segments have total length at most
2\eta n
.

For
k\geq k_{\star}
, for
\hat{\mu}_{k}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, and for
n\geq n_{k}(\hat{x})
, Claim
7.5
allows us to construct the decomposition string
\theta^{\prime}(\hat{x},n)\in(\mathbb{Z}\times\{1,2,3\})^{l+1}
for some integer
l
. For a given orbit
(\hat{x},\dots,\hat{f}^{-(n-1)}(\hat{x}))
, we will also need to keep track of which iterates are inside
\hat{U}_{0}\cup\hat{U}_{1}
and which are not. For that, we introduce the word
v(\hat{x},n)\in\{0,1\}^{n}
which satisfies
v(\hat{x},n)_{i}=1
if
\hat{g}^{i}\in\hat{U}_{0}\cup\hat{U}_{1}
and
v(\hat{x},n)_{i}=0
otherwise.

We define the
decomposition type
of an orbit
(\hat{x},\dots,\hat{f}^{-(n-1)}(\hat{x}))
as the following data:

\theta(\hat{x},n):=\Big[v(\hat{x},n),\theta^{\prime}(\hat{x},n)\Big]\in\{0,1\}^{n}\times\big(\mathbb{N}\times\{1,2,3\}\big)^{l+1}.

Recall that we denote by
H
the map
H(t)=-t\log t-(1-t)\log(1-t)
. Claim
7.6
gives us the following bound on the number of decomposition types.

Claim 8.7
.

There exists an integer
n_{H}:=n_{H}(\eta)
such that the number of decomposition types of any
\hat{f}^{-1}
-orbit as in Claim
8.6
with length
n>n_{H}
is at most
\exp\big(n(5\eta+2H(5\eta))\big)
.

Step S.I. 5.

Conditional measures.

This step can be compared with Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
in the proof of Proposition
7.1
. Let
k\geq k_{\star}
from Claim
8.6
. Denote by
\{\hat{\mu}_{k}^{s,\hat{x}}\}_{\hat{x}}
a family of conditional measures of
\hat{\mu}_{k}
on local stable sets. Recall that the measure
\hat{\mu}_{k}^{s,\hat{x}}
is supported on
V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
. We fix an integer
N_{k}\geq 0
and a measurable set
\hat{F}_{k}
with
\hat{\mu}_{k}(\hat{F}_{k})>1/2
such that every point
\hat{x}\in\hat{F}_{k}
satisfies:

–

\hat{x}
has a well-defined stable manifold
V^{s}(\hat{x})\subset M_{f}
,

–

\hat{\mu}^{s,\hat{x}}_{k}
is well-defined and
\hat{x}
is in the support of
\hat{\mu}_{k}^{s,\hat{x}}
restricted to
\hat{F}_{k}
,

–

\hat{x}
satisfies Claim
8.6
with
n_{k}(\hat{x})\leq N_{k}
. In particular, for each
n\geq N_{k}
, the orbit
(\hat{x},\dots,\hat{f}^{-n}(\hat{x}))
has a decomposition as in Claim
8.6
. Write
\theta(\hat{x},n)
for its decomposition.

Step S.I. 6.

Construction of a cover by Bowen-balls.

This step can be compared to Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
in the proof of Proposition
7.1
. The cases of small entropy segments and wild segments are treated in the same way.

Recall that for any
x\in M
, the fiber
\pi^{-1}(x)
is homeomorphic to the Cantor set
K
. Then, up to reducing the size of
W^{s}_{loc}
, the stable set
V^{s}_{loc}(x)
is homeomorphic to
[0,1]\times K
. Denote this homeomorphism by
\varphi_{x}
. On each connected component
[0,1]\times\{t\}
, for
t\in K
, the map
\varphi_{x}
is a diffeomorphism and its image is homeomorphic to the curve
W^{s}_{loc}(x)
.

Fix any
\hat{x}\in\hat{F}_{k}
. Let
x=\pi(\hat{x})
. Fix
n\geq\max(N_{k},n_{H})
and fix also
\epsilon,\sigma>0
as in Step
(S.I.
8.4
)
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{0}=V^{s}_{loc}(x)\cap B(\hat{x},\epsilon)\cap\hat{F}_{k}
. Note that by our hypothesis on the set
\hat{F}_{k}
we have
\hat{\mu}^{s,\hat{x}}_{k}(\mathcal{B}_{0})>0
. Recall the definition of the family
\mathcal{T}=\{T_{1},\dots,T_{L}\}
. We are going to show that some
\hat{f}^{-1}
-iterate of
V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
intersects some
\pi^{-1}(\partial^{u,l}T_{i})
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
and
l\in\{1,2\}
. We will proceed by contradiction. From now on, assume the following non-intersection hypothesis.

Hypothesis (H2)
.

For any
m\geq 0
and for any
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
and for any connected component
W\subset\hat{f}^{-m}(V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
such that
W\cap\pi^{-1}(T_{i})\neq\emptyset
, we have
W\subset\pi^{-1}(T_{i})
.

Our goal is to construct a cover
\mathcal{B}_{n}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
by
(n,\epsilon)
-Bowen balls of small cardinality. The strategy is similar to the one explained in Step 8 of the proof of Proposition
7.1
.

We begin by fixing a decomposition type
\theta=\big[(v_{0},\dots,v_{n-1}),\big((t_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(t_{m},b_{m})\big)\big]
with
t_{0}=-1
and
t_{m}=n-1
. We will construct a cover
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\theta}
of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{B}_{0}\cap\{\hat{x},\ \theta(\hat{x},n)=\theta\}
by
(n,\epsilon)
-Bowen balls. We will then take the union over all possible decomposition types and obtain the desired cover
\mathcal{B}_{n}
.

The cover
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{n}
will be obtained inductively by defining covers
\mathcal{B}_{t_{i}}^{\theta}
of
\mathcal{B}_{0}
with
(t_{i},\epsilon)
-Bowen balls. The first cover is
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{0}:=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. Assume now that the cover
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i-1}}
is well-defined. We proceed by concatenation. We will set:

\mathcal{B}_{t_{i}}^{\theta}=\{B\cap\hat{f}^{t_{i-1}}(B^{\prime}),\ B\in\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i-1}},\ B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}(B,t_{i})\}

where
\mathcal{B}(B,t_{i})
is a well-chosen cover of
\hat{f}^{-t_{i-1}}\big(B\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\big)
by
(t_{i}-t_{i-1},\epsilon)
-Bowen balls. Note that
B
is a
(t_{i-1},\epsilon)
-Bowen ball. Let us now construct these covers
\mathcal{B}(B,t_{i})
.

We fix then
B\in\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i-1}}
. As in the proof of Proposition
7.1
, the cases
b_{i-1}=2,3
are easy and are treated in the same way, while the case
b_{i-1}=1
needs more attention.

Case A: small entropy segment.
Here we treat the case
b_{i-1}=2
. Recall that we then have
t_{i}-t_{i-1}=n_{0}
. By Claim
8.5
, there exists a set
\mathcal{C}_{0}\subset M_{f}
such that
|\mathcal{C}_{0}|\leq\exp\big(n_{0}\log\deg(f)(1+\delta)\big)
and such that:

\hat{f}^{-t_{i-1}}\big(B\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\big)\subset\bigcup_{\hat{y}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}}B_{\hat{f}^{-1}}(\hat{y},n_{0},\epsilon).

We then set
\mathcal{B}(B,t_{i})=\{B_{\hat{f}^{-1}}(\hat{y},n_{0},\epsilon),\ \hat{y}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}\}
. Note that it satisfies the following bound:

|\mathcal{B}(B,t_{i})|\leq\exp\big(n_{0}\log\deg(f)(1+\delta)\big).

Repeating this for any
B\in\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i-1}}
allows us to construct the cover
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i}}
, which satisfies:

(8.3)

|\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i}}|\leq\exp\big(n_{0}\log\deg(f)(1+\delta)\big)|\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i-1}}|.

Case B: wild segment.
We suppose now
b_{i-1}=3
. The idea is the same as in Case B in the proof of Proposition
7.1
. We bound the number of Bowen-balls by the derivative. Indeed, if two points
\hat{y},\hat{z}\in\hat{f}^{-t_{i-1}}\big(B\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\big)
are such that
d(\hat{y},\hat{z})<\epsilon/(\lVert d\hat{f}^{-1}\rVert^{t_{i}-t_{i-1}})
, then they must stay
\epsilon
close until time
t_{i}-t_{i-1}
. We construct the cover
\mathcal{B}(B,t_{i})
by taking the union of such balls of radius
\epsilon/(\lVert d\hat{f}^{-1}\rVert^{t_{i}-t_{i-1}})
. There exists then a constant
C\geq 1
, depending on
M
and
f
, such that:

|\mathcal{B}(B,t_{i})|\leq C\lVert d\hat{f}^{-1}\rVert^{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}.

Repeating this argument for all
B\in\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i-1}}
gives us the desired family
\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i}}
, which satisfies:

(8.4)

|\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i}}|\leq C\lVert d\hat{f}^{-1}\rVert^{t_{i}-t_{i-1}}|\mathcal{B}^{\theta}_{t_{i-1}}|.

Case C: rectangle segment.
Let us finally treat the last case
b_{i-1}=1
. Recall that we then have that
t_{i}-t_{i-1}=n_{1}
and that for any
\hat{y}\in\hat{f}^{-t_{i-1}+1}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
, we have
\hat{y}\in\cup_{i}T_{i}
, where the
us
-rectangles
T_{i}
are defined in Step
(S.I.
8.4
)
. Define the two sequences of times
(\tau_{k})_{k}
and
(\tau^{\prime}_{k})_{k}
as in Step 8 of the proof of Proposition
7.1
. Set
\tau_{0}=\tau^{\prime}_{0}=0
. Assume that
\tau_{k}
and
\tau_{k}^{\prime}
are well-defined. Let:

\tau_{k+1}:=\inf\{j>\tau_{k}^{\prime},\ v_{t_{i-1}+j+1}=0\}\quad\text{and}\ \quad\tau_{k+1}^{\prime}:=\inf\{j>\tau_{k+1},\ v_{t_{i-1}+j+1}=1\}.

The induction stops when some
\tau_{k}
or
\tau_{k}^{\prime}
is equal to
n_{1}-1
. In the case where some
\tau_{k}=n_{1}-1
, we set
\tau^{\prime}_{k}=n_{1}-1
too. Note that
\tau_{1}
is well defined since
v_{t_{i-1}+1}=1
. Recall that by Claim
8.5
and by the definition of a rectangle segment, if
v_{t_{i-1}+j}=1
then
\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+j)}(\hat{y})\in\hat{U}_{1}
for any
\hat{y}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. Let us introduce the following quantity:

c^{0}_{i}=\#\{0\leq j\leq n_{1}-1,\ v_{t_{i-1}+j}=0\}.

We are going to proceed again by induction, constructing sequences
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k}^{\prime}}
of covers of the set
\hat{f}^{-t_{i-1}}(B\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
by Bowen balls. For the integer
l\geq 0
such that
\tau_{l}=n-1
, we will then let
\mathcal{B}(B,t_{i})=\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{l}}
. Let first
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{0}}=\mathcal{B}_{\tau^{\prime}_{0}}=\hat{f}^{t_{i-1}}(B\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
. Assume that the cover
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k}^{\prime}}
is well-defined. We are going to construct
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k+1}}
. To do this, as before, we fix some
D\in\mathcal{B}_{\tau^{\prime}_{k}}
and we want to build
\mathcal{B}(D,\tau_{k+1})
, a cover of
\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau^{\prime}_{k}+1)}(D\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
by
(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime}-1,\epsilon)
-Bowen balls. Proceeding by concatenation as before will give us
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k+1}}
. See Case C of Step
(U.I.
7.2
)
in the proof of Proposition
7.1
for more details on the strategy. Let then
D\in\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k}^{\prime}}
.

Recall now the following crucial fact. For any
j\in\llbracket\tau_{k}^{\prime},\tau_{k+1}\rrbracket
, Hypothesis
(H2)
gives that for any
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
and any connected component
V\subset\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+j)}(V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
such that
V\cap\pi^{-1}(T_{i})\neq\emptyset
, we have:

V\subset\pi^{-1}(T_{i}).

For any connected component
V
of
\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime})}(D\cap V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
, denote by
V^{\prime}
the set
V\cap\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime})}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
when the intersection is non-empty.

Claim 8.8
.

There exists
i_{1}\in\{1,\dots,L\}
such that
\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}+1)}(D\cap V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))\subset\pi^{-1}(T_{i_{1}})
. Moreover, for any connected component
V\subset\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime})}(D\cap V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
and any
j\in\llbracket 2,\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime}\rrbracket
, there exists
i_{j}\in\{1,\dots,L\}
depending on
V
such that:

\hat{f}^{-j}(V^{\prime})\subset\pi^{-1}(T_{i_{j}}).

As a consequence,
V^{\prime}
is contained in a single
(\tau_{k+1}-\tau^{\prime}_{k},\epsilon/3)
-Bowen ball.

Proof.

Note first that
\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau^{\prime}_{k}+1)}(D\cap V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
has to project by
\pi
onto a connected component of
f^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau^{\prime}_{k}+1)}(W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
. Indeed, suppose that
\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau^{\prime}_{k})}(D\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
projects onto two different connected components
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
. There exists some
j\in\{1,\dots,t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}\}
such that
f^{j}(W_{1})=f^{j}(W_{2})
but
f^{j-1}(W_{1})\cap f^{j-1}(W_{2})=\emptyset
. But since
f
is a local diffeomorphism, this would imply that
f^{j-1}(W_{1})
and
f^{j-1}(W_{2})
are separated by a uniform distance depending only on
f
, which contradicts the fact that the image by
f^{j}
of points of
\pi(\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau^{\prime}_{k}+1)}(D\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime}))
have to be
\epsilon
-close for
j=0,\dots,t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}+1
.

In particular,
\pi(\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau^{\prime}_{k}+1)}(D\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime}))
is a curve of diameter less than
\epsilon
which intersects
\cup_{i}T_{i}
. Hypothesis
(H2)
implies that there exists
i_{0}\in\{1,\dots,L\}
such that:

V^{\prime}\subset V\subset\pi^{-1}(T_{i_{0}}).

Now Claim
8.8
is a direct consequence of Hypothesis
(H2)
and the fact that for any
j\in\llbracket 1,\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime}\rrbracket
and any
\hat{y}\in V^{\prime}\subset\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime})}(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
we have
\hat{f}^{-j}(\hat{y})\in\cup_{i}\pi^{-1}(T_{i})
. Indeed, since
\hat{f}^{-j}(V)
is connected, it has to be contained in a connected component of
\pi^{-1}(T_{i_{j}})
, which has diameter less than
\epsilon/3
.
∎

Choose an arbitrary
x_{1}\in T_{i_{1}}\cap\pi(\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}+1)}(V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})\cap D)
. We are going to build a cover by Bowen balls of the set
\pi^{-1}(x_{1})
with controlled cardinality.

Claim 8.9
.

For any
n\geq 0
, there exists a set
\mathcal{F}^{n}_{x_{1}}
such that
|\mathcal{F}^{n}_{x_{1}}|\leq K\deg(f)^{n}
, for some constant
K>0
independent of
n
, which satisfies the following:

\pi^{-1}(x_{1})\subset\bigcup_{\hat{y}\in\mathcal{F}^{n}_{x_{1}}}B_{\hat{f}^{-1}}(\hat{y},n,\epsilon/3).

Proof.

Recall that the distance in
M_{f}
is given by:

d(\hat{y},\hat{z})=\sum_{i=0}^{+\infty}\frac{d(y_{i},z_{i})}{2^{i}}.

Since
x_{1}
is fixed and the number of pre-images is constant equal to
\deg(f)
, the dynamics of
\hat{f}^{-1}
on
\pi^{-1}(x_{1})
is conjugate to a Markov shift on a finite alphabet with
\deg(f)
letters. It is now classical that the number of
(n,\epsilon/3)
-Bowen balls to cover such a system is proportional to
\deg(f)^{n}
, the proportionality constant depending only on
\epsilon
.
∎

Apply Claim
8.9
with
\tilde{n}=\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime}-1
. For any
\hat{y}\in\mathcal{F}^{\tilde{n}}_{x_{1}}
, denote by
\hat{T}_{i_{1},\hat{y}}
the union of all the connected components of
\pi^{-1}(T_{i_{1}})
intersecting
B_{\hat{f}^{-1}}(\hat{y},\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime}-1,\epsilon/3)
.

Claim 8.10
.

For any
\hat{y}\in\mathcal{F}_{x_{1}}^{\tilde{n}}
the set
\hat{T}_{i_{1},\hat{y}}\cap\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}+1)}(D\cap V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
is contained in one
(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime}-1,\epsilon)
-Bowen ball.

Proof.

Let
j\in\llbracket 0,\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime}-1\rrbracket
and let
\hat{z}_{1},\hat{z}_{2}\in\hat{T}_{i_{1}\hat{y}}\cap\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}+1)}(D\cap V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
for some
\hat{y}\in\mathcal{F}^{\tilde{n}}_{x_{1}}
. Denote by
\hat{x}_{1}^{l}
the unique point in
\pi^{-1}(x_{1})
which belongs to the same connected component of
\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}+1)}(D\cap V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
as
\hat{z}_{l}
, for
l\in\{1,2\}
. Combining Claim
8.8
and the fact that
\hat{x}_{1}^{1}
and
\hat{x}_{1}^{2}
belong to the same
(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime},\epsilon/3)
-Bowen ball we have:

d\big(\hat{f}^{-j}(\hat{z}_{1}),\hat{f}^{-j}(\hat{z}_{2})\big)\leq d\big(\hat{f}^{-j}(\hat{z}_{1}),\hat{f}^{-j}(\hat{x}_{1}^{1})\big)+d\big(\hat{f}^{-j}(\hat{x}_{1}^{1}),\hat{f}^{-j}(\hat{x}_{1}^{2})\big)+d\big(\hat{f}^{-j}(\hat{x}_{1}^{2}),\hat{f}^{-j}(\hat{z}_{2})\big)<\epsilon

∎

Define now
\mathcal{B}(D,\tau_{k+1})
to be the minimal collection of
(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime}-1,\epsilon)
-Bowen balls containing every set
\hat{T}_{i_{1},\hat{y}}\cap\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}+1)}(D\cap V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
for
\hat{y}\in\mathcal{F}^{\tilde{n}}_{x_{1}}
. Note that combining Claim
8.8
and Claim
8.9
gives the following:

\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}+1)}(D\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\subset\bigcup_{\hat{y}\in\mathcal{F}^{\tilde{n}}_{x_{1}}}\hat{T}_{\hat{y}}\cap\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}+1)}(D\cap V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})).

In particular,
\mathcal{B}(D,\tau_{k+1})
is a cover by
(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime}-1,\epsilon)
-Bowen balls of the set
\hat{f}^{-(t_{i-1}+\tau_{k}^{\prime}+1)}(D\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
. Note also that by Claim
8.10
, we have the following bound on the cardinality of this cover:

|\mathcal{B}(D,\tau_{k+1})|\leq|\mathcal{F}^{\tilde{n}}_{x_{1}}|\leq K\deg(f)^{(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime})}.

This concludes the construction of the cover
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k+1}}
and gives us the following:

|\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k+1}}|\leq K\deg(f)^{(\tau_{k+1}-\tau_{k}^{\prime}-1)}|\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k}^{\prime}}|.

To conclude with Case C, assume now that
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k}}
is well-defined and that you want to construct
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k}^{\prime}}
. Here the argument is the same as in Case A and we construct our cover by taking balls of radius
\epsilon/\lVert d\hat{f}^{-1}\rVert^{\tau_{k}^{\prime}-\tau_{k}}
. This gives us the following bound on the cardinality of
\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k}^{\prime}}
:

|\mathcal{B}_{\tau_{k}^{\prime}}|\leq C\lVert d\hat{f}^{-1}\rVert^{\tau_{k}^{\prime}-\tau_{k}}

for some constant
C>0
.

This concludes the induction and allows us to define
\mathcal{B}(B,t_{i})
and, by induction, it gives us the following bound on its cardinality:

|\mathcal{B}(B,t_{i})|\leq(KC)^{c_{i}^{0}}\times d(f)^{n_{1}}\times\lVert d\hat{f}^{-1}\rVert^{c_{i}^{0}}.

Repeating this construction for all
B\in\mathcal{B}_{t_{i-1}}^{\theta}
allows us to define the cover
\mathcal{B}_{t_{i}}^{\theta}
, which has cardinality:

(8.5)

|\mathcal{B}_{t_{i}}^{\theta}|\leq(KC)^{c_{i}^{0}}\times d(f)^{n_{1}}\times\lVert d\hat{f}^{-1}\rVert^{c_{i}^{0}}|\mathcal{B}_{t_{i-1}}^{\theta}|.

This finishes the induction over the
t_{i}
and allows us to define
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{\theta}
. Repeating all this construction for every decomposition type
\theta
defines the cover
\mathcal{B}_{n}
.

Step S.I. 7.

A bound on the cardinality of the covers by Bowen balls.

This step can be compared to Step
(S.I.
7.2
)
in the proof of Proposition
7.1
. By Step
(S.I.
8.4
)
, gathering all the estimates (
8.3
), (
8.4
) and (
8.5
) together, we obtain the following bound for
n>N_{k}
:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{B}_{n}|\leq

\displaystyle\exp\big(n(5\eta+2H(\eta)\big)

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big(\big((1-\alpha)n+2\eta n\big)\log d(f)(1+\delta)\big)

\displaystyle\times\big(2KC\lVert d\hat{f}^{-1}\rVert\big)^{2\eta n}

\displaystyle\times d(f)^{\alpha n+2\eta n}.

Using Proposition
8.3
, this gives us the following bound on the entropy of
\hat{\mu}_{k}
for
k\geq k_{\star}
:

\displaystyle h(f,\hat{\mu}_{k},\epsilon)\leq\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log|\mathcal{B}_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq 5\eta+2H(\eta)

\displaystyle+(1-\alpha)\log d(f)+\big(\delta(1-\alpha)+2\eta\big)\log d(f)

\displaystyle+2\eta\log(2KC\lVert d\hat{f}^{-1}\rVert)

\displaystyle+\alpha\log d(f)+2\eta\log d(f).

Choosing
\eta
and
\delta
small enough in Steps
(S.I.
8.4
)
and
(S.I.
8.4
)
leads to:
h(f,\hat{\mu}_{k},\epsilon)\leq\log d(f)+h
.
On the other hand, we have, again choosing
\delta
correctly:

h(f,\hat{\mu}_{k},\epsilon)>h(f,\hat{\mu}_{k})-\delta>\log d(f)+h

which leads to a contradiction. Hypothesis
(H2)
can then not be true. This implies that there exists some
m\geq 0
, some
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
and a connected component
V\subset\hat{f}^{-m}(V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
such that
V
intersects
\pi^{-1}(T_{i})
and also
M_{f}\backslash\overline{\pi^{-1}(T_{i})}
. Recall that the stable boundaries of any
us
-rectangle of
\mathcal{T}
is contained in a stable manifold. Recall also that stable manifolds are either disjoint or equal. We then conclude that for any
k\geq k_{\star}
, there exists
i(k)\in\{1,\dots,L\}
such that for any
\hat{x}\in\hat{F}_{k}
, there exists some positive integer
m
such that:

(8.6)

\hat{f}^{-m}(V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))\cap V^{u}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i(k)})\neq\emptyset

where
W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i(k)})
contains one of the unstable boundaries of
T_{i(k)}
.

Step S.I. 8.

End of the proof.

We are now able to conclude. This step uses Sard’s theorem
6.11
and is very similar to Steps
(H.U.I.
7.3
)
and
(H.U.I.
7.3
)
in the proof of Proposition
7.2
.

Fix then
k\geq k_{\star}
, an integer
i(k)\in\{1,\dots,L\}
, a point
\hat{x}\in\hat{F}_{k}
such that
\hat{x}
satisfies Theorem
6.10
, a positive integer
m\geq 0
and a connected component
V\subset\hat{f}^{-m}(V^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x}))
such that
V
intersects both
T_{i(k)}
and
M\backslash\overline{T_{i(k)}}
. Projecting by
\pi
, this implies that there exists a curve
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
such that:

–

f^{m}(\gamma([0,1]))\subset W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
,

–

\gamma
intersects both
T_{i(k)}
and
M\backslash\overline{T_{i(k)}}
,

–

\gamma([0,1])\cap\partial^{u,l}T_{i(k)}\neq\emptyset
for some
l\in\{1,2\}
.

In particular,
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
intersects both
f^{m}(T_{i(k)})
and
M\backslash\overline{f^{m}(T_{i(k)})}
. This implies that for any chart small enough sending
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
to the horizontal, the curve
f^{m}(\partial^{u,l}T_{i(k)})
has a component above
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
and a component below
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
. Let
\kappa:[0,1]\to f^{m}(\partial^{u,l}T_{i(k)})
be an embedding which intersects
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
and has one component above and one below
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{x})
in the previous sense. Since
\hat{x}
satisfies Theorem
6.10
, there exists a compact set
\Lambda_{\hat{x}}
such that:

–

\text{dim}_{H}\big(W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{x})\cap\Lambda_{\hat{x}}\big)>0
,

–

\pi(\hat{x})
is accumulated on both sides of
W^{u}(\hat{x})
by points of
\Lambda_{\hat{x}}
.

Up to taking
\epsilon>0
small enough, the fact that
\pi(\hat{x})
is accumulated on both sides of
W^{u}(\hat{x})
by points of
\Lambda_{\hat{x}}
implies that the curve
\kappa
intersects any leaf of the stable lamination
W^{s}_{loc}(\Lambda_{x}\cap B(\hat{x},\epsilon))
. Now, since
\text{dim}_{H}\big(W^{u}_{\epsilon}(\hat{x})\cap\Lambda_{\hat{x}}\big)>0
, Theorem
6.11
and the continuity of the stable lamination of
\Lambda_{\hat{x}}
for the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
topology implies that there exists a set
\Lambda_{k}
of positive
\mu_{k}
-measure such that for any
x\in\Lambda_{k}
we have:

W^{s}(x)\pitchfork W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i(k)})\neq\emptyset.

Using Katok Theorem
5.3
and the
\lambda
-lemma
5.4
, we can show that
W^{u}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i(k)})
, respectively
W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i(k)})
, is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-accumulated by a sequence of discs belonging to the unstable manifold, respectively the stable manifold, of some hyperbolic saddle periodic orbit
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i(k)}
. We then conclude that for any
x\in\Lambda_{k}
, we have:

W^{s}(x)\pitchfork W^{u}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i(k)})\neq\emptyset.

Note that since the set of
x\in M
such that
W^{s}(x)\pitchfork W^{u}(\mathcal{O}_{i(k)})\neq\emptyset
is invariant and
\mu_{k}
is ergodic, it holds on a set of full
\mu_{k}
-measure.

We consider the family of hyperbolic saddle periodic orbits
\{\mathcal{O}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{O}_{L}\}
. Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
. We still have to show that
\hat{\nu}(\{\hat{x}\in M_{f},\hat{x}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\})>0
. Since
\hat{y}_{i}\in\hat{Y}^{\#}
, it is in the support of
\hat{\nu}
restricted to some Pesin block
\hat{\Lambda}
and
\hat{\nu}(B(\hat{y}_{i},\epsilon)\cap\hat{\Lambda})>0
for any
\epsilon>0
. By continuity of the stable and unstable manifolds in Pesin blocks, for the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
topology, we can find some small
\epsilon>0
such that any
\hat{z}\in B(\hat{y}_{i},\epsilon)\cap\hat{\Lambda}
is homoclinically related to
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, which concludes the proof of Proposition
8.4
.

9.
Proof of the main theorems

This last section is devoted to the proofs of the main theorems presented in the introduction. We begin by proving Theorem
A
assuming Theorem
B
, as a direct corollary.

Proof of Theorem
A
assuming Theorem
B
.

By Theorem
5.5
, any measured homoclinic class contains at most one ergodic measure of maximal entropy. By Theorem
B
, the set of measured homoclinic classes containing a measure of maximal entropy is finite, which concludes the proof.
∎

We continue with the proof of Theorem
B
. We proceed by contradiction, which allows us to find a sequence of ergodic hyperbolic
f
-invariant measures
(\mu_{k})_{k}
of saddle type, which are not homoclinically related and such that their entropy converges to the topological entropy. The idea is then to apply first Proposition
8.4
to the sequence
(\mu_{k})_{k}
, which converges to
\nu
. We then obtain the existence of a finite number of hyperbolic periodic orbits such that the stable manifold of
\mu_{k}
-almost every point intersects transversely the unstable manifold of some of these orbits. Then, Proposition
7.2
will imply that the unstable manifold of
\mu_{k}
-almost every point intersects transversely the stable manifold of all these hyperbolic periodic orbits. This will imply that for
k
large enough, the
\mu_{k}
are homoclinically related to a finite number of periodic hyperbolic orbits, which is a contradiction.

Proof of Theorem
B
.

We proceed by contradiction. Suppose that for any
\epsilon>0
, the set of measured homoclinic classes
\mathcal{M}(\mu)
containing a measure
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}(\mu)
such that
h(f,\mu^{\prime})>h_{\text{top}}(f)-\epsilon
is not finite. This allows us to find a sequence of measures
(\mu_{k})_{k}
such that
\liminf_{k\to+\infty}h(f,\mu_{k})=h_{\text{top}}(f)
and such that for
i\neq j
, the measures
\mu_{i}
and
\mu_{j}
are not homoclinically related. Denote its lift to
M_{f}
by
(\hat{\mu}_{k})_{k}
. Up to taking a subsequence, we can assume that
(\hat{\mu}_{k})_{k}
weak-
\star
converges to a
\hat{f}
-invariant measure
\hat{\nu}
. Again, denote its projection to
M
by
\nu
. Since
f
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, the entropy is upper semi-continuous and
\hat{\nu}
is a measure of maximal entropy. In particular,
\hat{\nu}
-almost every ergodic component is a measure of maximal entropy. Since
h_{\text{top}}(f)>\log\deg(f)
, Ruelle’s inequality (Proposition
2.1
) implies that any measure of maximal entropy is hyperbolic of saddle type. The sequence
(\mu_{k})_{k}
and its limit
\nu
satisfy the hypotheses of Propositions
7.2
and
8.4
with
\alpha=1
.

We begin by applying Proposition
8.4
. We obtain that there exists a finite number of hyperbolic saddle
\hat{f}
-periodic orbits
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
such that the following properties hold.

–

There exists an integer
k_{0}\geq 0
such that for any
k\geq k_{0}
and for
\hat{\mu}_{k}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, the following intersection holds:

(9.1)

W^{s}(\hat{x})\pitchfork W^{u}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{j})\neq\emptyset

for some
j\in\{1,\dots,L\}
.

–

For any
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
, the set
\{\hat{x}\in M_{f},\ \hat{x}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\}
has positive
\hat{\nu}
-measure.

We now apply Proposition
7.2
for all the orbits
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1},\dots,\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
, in the trivial case where
\alpha=1
. We obtain that there exist integers
k_{1},\dots,k_{L}\geq 1
satisfying the following property for any
i\in\{1,\dots,L\}
. For any
k\geq k_{i}
, for
\hat{\mu}_{k}
-almost every
\hat{x}
, the following intersection holds:

(9.2)

W^{u}(\hat{x})\pitchfork W^{s}(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i})\neq\emptyset.

Take now
k_{\star}=\max(k_{0},\dots,k_{L})
. Combining (
9.1
) and (
9.2
), we obtain that for any
k\geq k_{\star}
, there exists an integer
i(k)\in\{1,\dots,L\}
such that
\hat{\mu}_{k}
is homoclinically related to
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i(k)}
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
B
, since there exist
k_{1},k_{2}\geq k_{\star}
such that
i(k_{1})=i(k_{2})
and then
\hat{\mu}_{k_{1}}\overset{h}{\sim}\hat{\mu}_{k_{2}}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Let us now finish with the proof of Theorem
C
. It is a corollary of Proposition
7.1
. The idea is to use Proposition
7.1
to get topological intersections and conclude with Sard’s theorem
6.11
.

Proof of Theorem
C
.

Let
(\mu_{k})_{k}
be a sequence of ergodic hyperbolic measures of saddle type with maximal entropy. Let
(\hat{\mu}_{k})_{k}
be the lifted sequence to
M_{f}
. Up to taking a subsequence, we can assume that
\hat{\mu}_{k}
weak-
\star
converges to a
\hat{f}
-invariant measure
\hat{\nu}
. Since
f
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, the entropy is upper semi-continuous and
\hat{\nu}
is then a measure of maximal entropy. In particular,
\hat{\nu}
-almost every ergodic component is also a measure of maximal entropy.

Claim 9.1
.

There exists
\alpha\in(0,1]
and two
\hat{f}
-invariant probabilities
\hat{\nu}_{1}
and
\hat{\nu}_{0}
such that:

–

\hat{\nu}
-almost every ergodic component of
\hat{\nu}_{1}
is hyperbolic of saddle type,

–

\hat{\nu}=\alpha\hat{\nu}_{1}+(1-\alpha)\hat{\nu}_{0}
.

Proof.

Write
\hat{\nu}=\int_{M_{f}}\hat{\nu}_{\hat{x}}\ d\hat{\nu}(\hat{x})
as the ergodic decomposition of the measure
\hat{\nu}
. The Lyapunov exponents of
\hat{\nu}
are defined as:

\lambda^{\pm}(\hat{\nu})=\int_{M_{f}}\lambda^{\pm}(\hat{\nu}_{\hat{x}})\ d\hat{\nu}(\hat{x}).

Using the subadditive ergodic theorem, it is well known that the upper Lyapunov exponent is upper semi-continuous:

\limsup_{k\to+\infty}\lambda^{+}(\hat{\mu}_{k})\leq\lambda^{+}(\hat{\nu}).

See
[
4
, Theorem 3.5]
for a proof. The following sequence of inequalities then holds:

\displaystyle\lambda^{+}(\hat{\nu})

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{k\to+\infty}\lambda^{+}(\hat{\mu}_{k})

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{k\to+\infty}\lambda^{+}(\hat{\mu}_{k})+\lambda^{-}(\hat{\mu}_{k})=\limsup_{k\to+\infty}\int_{M_{f}}\log|\det d_{\hat{x}}\hat{f}|\ d\hat{\mu}_{k}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{M_{f}}\log|\det d_{\hat{x}}\hat{f}|\ d\hat{\nu}(\hat{x})=\lambda^{+}(\hat{\nu})+\lambda^{-}(\hat{\nu})

where the convergence of the integral holds since
(\hat{\mu}_{k})_{k}
converges weak-
\star
to
\hat{\nu}
and
\det d_{\hat{x}}\hat{f}
is continuous. We then get that
\lambda^{-}(\hat{\nu})\leq 0
. But since almost-every ergodic component of
\hat{\nu}
is hyperbolic, we have
\lambda^{-}(\hat{\nu})<0
. Let
\hat{X}=\{\hat{x},\ \lambda^{-}(\hat{\nu}_{\hat{x}})<0\}
. The fact that
\lambda^{-}(\hat{\nu})
is negative implies that
\hat{X}
has positive
\hat{\nu}
-measure. Denote this measure by
\alpha
. The following two invariant probabilities give us the desired decomposition:

\hat{\nu}_{1}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{\hat{X}}\hat{\nu}_{\hat{x}}\ d\hat{\nu}(\hat{x})\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\nu}_{0}=\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\hat{X}^{c}}\hat{\nu}_{\hat{x}}\ d\hat{\nu}(\hat{x}).

∎

Let us check that the decomposition given by Claim
9.1
satisfies the hypothesis of Proposition
7.1
. Denote by
\nu,\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{0}
the projections to
M
by
\pi
of the measures
\hat{\nu},\hat{\nu}_{1}
and
\hat{\nu}_{0}
. Recall that we supposed that
h_{\text{top}}(f)>0
. Recall also that
\hat{\nu}
-almost every ergodic component of
\hat{\nu}
is a measure of maximal entropy. We then have that
\hat{\nu}
-almost every ergodic component of
\hat{\nu}_{1}
has positive entropy and is hyperbolic of saddle type. Since
\liminf_{k\to+\infty}h(f,\mu_{k})=h_{\text{top}}(f)
and
\alpha>0
, we also have:

h(f,\nu^{\prime})<\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\liminf_{k\to+\infty}h(f,\mu_{k})

for
\nu
-almost every ergodic component of
\nu_{0}
. We can then apply Proposition
7.1
to the sequence
(\mu_{k})_{k}
and the measure
\nu
. There exists then a finite number of recurrent points
\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{L}
, which are accumulated on both sides of their unstable manifold by points such that their stable manifolds form a continuous lamination with Lipschitz holonomies (recall the definition of the set
\hat{Y}^{\#}
), and displaying the following property. For
k
sufficiently large, there exists an integer
i(k)\in\{1,\dots,L\}
and a set
\hat{X}_{k}
of positive
\hat{\mu}_{k}
-measure such that for any
\hat{x}\in\hat{X}_{k}
, the following intersection holds:

W^{u}(\hat{x})\cap W^{s}_{loc}(\hat{y}_{i(k)})\neq\emptyset.

We are going to apply now the same argument as in Step
(H.U.I.
7.3
)
of the proof of Proposition
7.2
. Let us explain it again. Since
\hat{y}_{i(k)}
is accumulated by points of the same Pesin block on both sides of its unstable manifold, any
W^{u}(\hat{x})
, for
\hat{x}\in\hat{X}_{k}
, intersects a continuous lamination by stable local manifolds with Lipschitz holonomies. Since
f
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, we can apply Sard Theorem
6.11
, which implies that
W^{u}(\hat{x})
intersects transversely local stable manifolds of points belonging to a set of positive
\hat{\nu}_{1}
-measure. This implies that
\mu_{k}
is homoclinically related to some ergodic, hyperbolic measure of saddle type
\hat{\nu}_{i(k)}
which has positive entropy, since
\hat{\nu}_{i(k)}
can be chosen as an ergodic component of
\hat{\nu}_{1}
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
C
.
∎

We conclude this work by explaining why our results extend to the setting of the Corollary in Subsection
Corollary
. Let
f
be a map of a two-dimensional manifold and
K
be a non-singular compact attractor. Recall that
K
is a non-singular compact attractor if
K
is compact, invariant, and there exists an open connected neighborhood
U
of
K
such that
f(\overline{U})\subset U
and
U\cap(\cup_{n\geq 0}f^{n}(\mathscr{C}))=\emptyset
, where
\mathscr{C}
is the set of critical points of
f
, and the orbit of any point in
U
converges to
K
. First, since
U
is connected, the number of pre-images is constant in
K
and we denote it by
\deg(f_{|K})
. We can also suppose that for any hyperbolic measure of saddle type
f
-invariant measure supported in
K
, its local stable and unstable manifolds are contained in
U
. The result of Section
7
holds trivially since we only iterate in the future. The results of Section
8
also hold since the local stable manifolds are contained in
U
, which implies that the
n
-th pre-images of any point in such a curve cannot be a critical point, for any
n
. The setting is then the same as in Section
8
. We conclude as in Section
9
.
